老人的话和他轻轻的一触，对男孩发生了奇妙的作用；他激愤的发作止住了；可是他那颗郁积的心太满了。他一下子哭出来，一头冲出了屋子。

有一阵子他漫无目的地走着，心里一片狂怒的混乱。随后他渐渐回过神来。他看见了身边走过的人。他重新看见了这条街，才纳起闷来：自己这是往哪儿走？他有一种压不住的渴望，想找个人说说话，讨一点同情、一点安慰，也许还有一点扶持。可是该往谁那儿去呢？倘若埃利法莱特·霍奇斯还在老屋里，他的脚步自然会拐向那边；可那个避风港已不再属于他。于是他心里把认识的人挨个数了一遍，没有一个名字像伊丽莎白那样黏在他的念头上。这是一场苦斗。他怕羞。换作别的时候，他决不会去的；可眼下这份急切的需要在他心里激起一股不顾一切的拼命劲儿，壮了他的胆。他转过身，循原路往辛普森家走去。

===Original===
Elizabeth was leaning over the gate. The autumn evening was cool: she had a thin shawl about her shoulders. She was humming a song as Fred came up. His own agitation made her seem irritatingly calm. She opened the gate and made room for him at her side.

“You seem dreadfully warm,” she said, “and here I was getting ready to go in because it is so cool.”

“I’ve been walking very fast,” he answered, hesitatingly.

“Don’t you think you’d better go in, so as not to take cold?”

“Oh, I don’t care if I do take cold.” The speech sounded rude. Elizabeth looked at him in surprise.

===Chinese===
伊丽莎白正倚在园门上。秋夜清凉：她肩上披着一条薄披巾。弗雷德走上前来时，她正哼着一支歌。他心潮翻涌，倒显得她那份安静刺眼地扎心。她推开门，在自己身边让出一块地方。

“你看上去热得厉害，”她说，“我这边正因为天凉，打算进屋去呢。”

“我走得太快了。”他迟疑着答道。

“你不觉得最好还是进屋去，免得着凉？”

“哦，着凉就着凉，我才不在乎。”这话听着无礼。伊丽莎白惊讶地望着他。

===Original===
“What’s the matter with you?” she asked.

“I’m mad; that’s what’s the matter.”

“Oh, Fred, you shouldn’t get mad: you know it’s wrong.”

He put up his hand as if she had struck him. “Wrong! wrong! It seems I can’t hear anything else but that word. Everything is wrong. Don’t say any more about it. I don’t want to hear the word again.”

Elizabeth did not know what to make of his words, so she said nothing, and for a while they stood in strained silence. After a while he said, “Aunt Hester wants me to be a preacher.”

===Chinese===
“你怎么了？”她问。

“我在生气，就是这么回事。”

“哎呀，弗雷德，你不该生气：你知道这是不对的。”

他抬起一只手，仿佛她打了他一下。“不对！不对！我耳朵里好像除了这两个字，再听不见别的了。什么都‘不对’。别再提了。我不想再听见这两个字。”

伊丽莎白摸不清他这些话的来路，便什么也没说，两人僵着沉默地站了一阵。过了一会儿他说：“海斯特婶婶要我当传道人。”

===Original===
“I am so glad to hear that,” she returned. “I think you’ll make a good one.”

“You too!” he exclaimed, resentfully. “Why should I make a good one? Why need I be one at all?”

“Oh, because you’re smart, and then you’ve always been good.”

The young man was suddenly filled with disdain. His anger returned. He felt how utterly out of accord he was with everyone else. “Don’t you think there is anything else required besides being ‘smart’ and ‘good’?” He himself would have blushed at the tone in which he said this, could he have recognised it. “I’m smart because I happened to pass all my examinations. I got through the high school at eighteen: nearly everyone does the same. I’m good because I have never had a chance to be bad: I have never been out of Aunt Hester’s sight long enough. Anybody could be good that way.”

“But then older people know what is best for us, Fred.”

===Chinese===
“听了这个我真高兴，”她答道，“我想你会当得很好的。”

“连你也这么说！”他忿忿地嚷道。“凭什么我就会当得好？凭什么我非得当不可？”

“嗐，因为你聪明呀，再说你一向又乖。”

年轻人心里陡然涌起一阵鄙夷。他的怒气又回来了。他感到自己与旁人是何等格格不入。“你以为除了‘聪明’和‘乖’，就再不需要别的了吗？”这话出口的腔调，他自己若能听见，也要脸红的。“我聪明，不过因为碰巧门门考试都及了格。我十八岁念完中学：差不离人人都是这样。我乖，不过因为我从来没有机会学坏：我从来没有离开过海斯特婶婶的眼皮太久。谁那么活着都能乖。”

“可是长辈总知道什么对咱们最好呀，弗雷德。”

===Original===
“Why should they? They don’t know what’s beating inside of us away down here.” The boy struck his breast fiercely. “I don’t believe they do know half the time what is best, and I don’t believe that God intends them to know.”

“I wouldn’t talk about it, if I were you. I must go in. Won’t you come in with me?”

“Not tonight,” he replied. “I must be off.”

“But papa might give you some advice.”

“I’ve had too much of it now. What I want is room to breathe in once.”

===Chinese===
“凭什么就他们知道？他们又不知道咱们这儿底下跳动的是什么。”男孩狠狠捶着自己的胸口。“我不信他们有一半时候真知道什么最好，我也不信上帝存心要他们知道。”

“我要是你，就不谈这个了。我得进屋了。你不跟我一起进来坐坐？”

“今晚不行，”他答道，“我得走了。”

“可爸爸说不定能给你出出主意呢。”

“主意我听得太够了。我现在要的是一回能喘气的地方。”

===Original===
“I don’t understand you.”

“I know you don’t; nobody does, or tries to. Go in, Lizzie,” he said more calmly. “I don’t want you to catch cold, even if I do. Good night.” And he turned away.

The girl stood for a moment looking after him; her eye was moist. Then she pouted, “Fred’s real cross tonight,” and went in.

It is one of the glaring sarcasms of life to see with what complacency a shallow woman skims the surface of tragedy and thinks that she has sounded the depths.

Fred continued his walk towards home. He was thinking. It ran in him that Elizabeth was a good deal of a fool; and then he felt horrified with himself for thinking it. It did not occur to him that the hard conditions through which he had come had made him mentally and spiritually older than the girl. He was thinking of his position, how perfectly alone he stood. Most of the people whom he knew would see only blind obstinacy in his refusal to be a minister. But were one’s inclinations nothing? Was there really nothing in the “call” to preach? So he pondered as he walked, and more and more the hopelessness of his predicament became revealed to him. All his life had been moulded by this one woman’s hands. Would not revolt now say to the world, “I am grown now; I do not need this woman who has toiled. I can disobey her with impunity; I will do so.”

===Chinese===
“我不明白你的意思。”

“我知道你不明白；谁也不明白，也没人打算明白。进屋去吧，莉齐，”他平静了些，说道，“就算我着了凉，我也不愿你着凉。晚安。”说罢他转身走了。

姑娘站了片刻，望着他的背影；她的眼睛湿润了。随后她撅起嘴：“弗雷德今晚脾气真坏。”便进屋去了。

生活里有一种刺眼的讽刺：眼看一个浅薄的女人意气洋洋地掠过悲剧的表层，还自以为探测过它的深渊。

弗雷德继续朝家走。他一路想着。他心里冒出一个念头：伊丽莎白简直是个小傻瓜；随即又为自己的念头吓了一跳。他没有想到，他所经历的严酷境遇，早已使他在心智和性灵上都比这姑娘苍老。他想着自己的处境，想着自己何等彻底地孤立。他认识的人多半只会在他拒绝当牧师这件事上，看见一股盲目的倔强。可是，一个人的志趣就什么也不算吗？传道的“召唤”当真毫无内容吗？他一面走一面这样琢磨，处境的绝望也一分一分向他显露出来。他这整整一生，都是被这一个女人的手捏塑出来的。此刻的反抗，岂不正是对世界宣告：“我长大了；我不需要这个操劳一生的女人了。我违抗她可以不受惩罚；我就要这样。”

===Original===
He went home, and before going in leaned his head long upon the gate and thought. A listless calm had succeeded his storm of passion. He went in and to bed.

At breakfast he seemed almost cheerful, while Mr. Hodges was subdued. His wife had taken refuge in an attitude of injured silence.

“Aunt Hester,” said the young man, apparently without effort, “I was wrong yesterday; I am sorry. I will do whatever you say, even to being a preacher.” Something came up in his throat and choked him as he saw a brightness come into the face and eyes of his beloved “Uncle ’Liph,” but it grew hard and bitter there as Mrs. Hodges replied, “Well, I’m glad the Lord has showed you the errors of your way an’ brought you around to a sense o’ your dooty to Him an’ to me.”

Poor, blind, conceited humanity! Interpreters of God, indeed! We reduce the Deity to vulgar fractions. We place our own little ambitions and inclinations before a shrine, and label them “divine messages.” We set up our Delphian tripod, and we are the priest and oracles. We despise the plans of Nature’s Ruler and substitute our own. With our short sight we affect to take a comprehensive view of eternity. Our horizon is the universe. We spy on the Divine and try to surprise His secrets, or to sneak into His confidence by stealth. We make God the eternal a puppet. We measure infinity with a foot-rule.

===Chinese===
他回到家，进门前把头久久倚在园门上想着什么。一阵激情的风暴过后，接上来的是一种无精打采的平静。他进屋，上了床。

早饭时他显得几乎兴高采烈，霍奇斯先生却蔫蔫的没精神。他的妻子摆出一副受了委屈的沉默，躲在里面。

“海斯特婶婶，”年轻人开口了，仿佛毫不费力似的，“昨天是我错了；我很抱歉。你说什么我都照办，哪怕是当传道人。”他看见心爱的“利夫叔叔”脸上眼里漾起一层光亮，喉咙里便有什么东西涌上来，哽住了他；可是等霍奇斯太太答了话，那光亮便在那里变得又硬又苦：“好吧，我很高兴主让你看清了自己的错处，把你扳回来，知道你对祂、对我应尽的本分了。”

可怜、盲目、自负的人类！还自命上帝的诠释者呢！我们把神明约化成普通分数。我们把自己那点小小的野心和癖好供在神龛前，贴上标签，称之为“神的讯息”。我们架起德尔斐的三足鼎，自己既当祭司，又当神谕。我们蔑视造化之主的计划，拿自己的一套取而代之。我们以寸光之目，偏要装作纵观永恒。我们的地平线便是宇宙。我们窥伺神明，想撞破祂的秘密，或者蹑手蹑脚混进祂的心腹。我们把永恒的上帝做成一个傀儡。我们拿一杆小小的直尺去丈量无穷。

## V / 五

===Original===
“Goodness gracious, Mis’ Smith,” exclaimed Mrs. Martin, rushing excitedly into the house of her next-door neighbour, “you’d ought to seen what I seen jest now.”

“Do tell, Mis’ Martin! What on airth was it?”

“Oh, I’m shore you’d never guess in the wide, wide world.”

“An’ I’m jest as shore that I ain’t a-goin’ to pester my head tryin’ to: so go on an’ tell me what it was.”

“Lawsy me! what next’ll happen, an’ what does things mean, anyhow?”

===Chinese===
“老天爷，史密斯太太，”马丁太太嚷道，激动地冲进隔壁邻居家里，“你真该瞧瞧我刚才瞧见的事。”

“快说说，马丁太太！到底是什么事呀？”

“嗐，我敢说，你就是想破了天也猜不着。”

“我也敢说，我才不费那个神去猜呢：所以快说吧，到底是什么。”

“我的老天！这往后还要出什么事？这世道到底算个什么意思？”

===Original===
“I can’t tell you. Fur my part, I ain’t heerd what ‘things’ air yit.” Mrs. Smith was getting angry.

“My! Mis’ Smith, don’t git so impatient. Give me time to git my breath: it’ll be enough, when I do tell you, to take away yore breath, jest like it did mine.”

“Sallie Martin, you do beat all fur keepin’ a body on the hooks.”

“T ain’t my fault, Mis’ Smith. I declare I’m too astonished to speak. You know I was a-standin’ in my window, not a-thinkin’ nor expectin’ nothin’, jest like any person would, you know﻿—”

===Chinese===
“我怎么说得上。眼下我连‘世道’是什么都还没听着呢。”史密斯太太有点儿上火了。

“哎哟，史密斯太太，你别急嘛。容我喘口气：等我真说出来，保准把你的气也抽上去，跟我刚才一样。”

“萨莉·马丁，你这吊人胃口的本事真是没谁了。”

“这不赖我，史密斯太太。我实在是惊得说不出话。你知道，我正站在窗户跟前，什么也没想、什么也没盼，就跟平常人一样，你知道——”

===Original===
“Yes, yes; go on.”

“I was jest a-lookin’ down the street, careless, when who should I see drive up to Miss Prime’s door, an’ hitch his hoss an’ go in, but Brother ’Liphalet Hodges!”

“Well, sakes alive, Sallie Martin, I hope you ain’t a-considerin’ that strange. Why, you could ’a’ seen that very same sight any time these fifteen years.”

“But wait a minute till I tell you. I ain’t done yit, by no means. The strange part ain’t come. I thought I’d jest wait at the window and see how long Brother Hodges would stay: not that it was any o’ my bus’ness, of course, or that I wanted to be a spyin’ on anybody, but sorter fur﻿—fur cur’osity, you know.”

===Chinese===
“是了，是了；接着说。”

“我正漫不经心地朝街那头望，你猜我瞧见谁赶着车到了普赖姆小姐门口，拴了马走进去？不是别人，正是埃利法莱特·霍奇斯弟兄！”

“嗐，我的天，萨莉·马丁，你可别把这也当成新鲜事。这十五年里，你哪天看不着这老一套。”

“可你等等，听我说完。我还远远没说完呢，稀奇的还在后头。我想索性就在窗前等等，看霍奇斯弟兄待多久：当然不是说这事跟我相干，也不是我想窥探谁，就是——就是有点儿好奇，你知道。”

===Original===
“Cert’n’y,” said Mrs. Smith, feelingly. She could sympathise with such a sentiment.

“Well, after a while he come out a-smilin’ as pleasant as a basket o’ chips; an’ I like to fell through the winder, fur he was a-leadin’ by the hand﻿—who do you suppose?”

“I ain’t got a mortal idea who,” said Mrs. Smith, “unless it was Miss Hester, an’ they’re married at last.”

“No, indeed, ’t wa’n’t her. It was that little Brent boy that his mother died the other day.”

===Chinese===
“那当然。”史密斯太太深有同感地说。这种心情她最能体谅。

“后来过了一阵，他出来了，笑得跟朵花儿似的；我险些从窗户里栽出去，因为他手里牵着——你猜是谁？”

“我一点儿也猜不着，”史密斯太太说，“除非是海斯特小姐——敢情他们俩总算成了亲。”

“不是，真不是她。是那个小布伦特，前几天死了娘的那个孩子。”

===Original===
“Sallie Martin, what air you a-tellin’ me?”

“It’s the gospel truth, Melviny Smith, as shore as I’m a-settin’ here. Now what does it mean?”

“The good Lord only knows. Leadin’ that little Brent boy? Ef it wasn’t you a-settin’ there tellin’ me this, Mis’ Martin, I wouldn’t believe it. You don’t suppose Hodges has took him to raise, do you?”

“How in the name of mercy is he goin’ to raise any child, when there ain’t no women folks about his house ’ceptin’ old Marier, an’ she so blind an’ rheumaticky that she kin sca’cely git about?”

===Chinese===
“萨莉·马丁，你这是跟我说什么呀？”

“千真万确，梅尔维妮·史密斯，我坐在这儿说话，字字属实。你说这是怎么回事？”

“只有老天爷知道。牵着那个小布伦特？要不是你坐在这儿亲口告诉我，马丁太太，我说什么都不信。你该不是说，霍奇斯把他领去抚养了吧？”

“老天爷，他拿什么抚养孩子？他家里除了老玛丽埃连个女人都没有，她那眼睛、那风湿腿，自己都迈不动步子。”

===Original===
“Well, what’s he a-doin’ with the child, then?”

“That’s jest what I’m a-goin’ to find out. I’m a-goin’ down to Miss Prime’s. Len’ me yore shawl, Melviny.”

“You ain’t never goin’ to dare to ask her, air you?”

“You jest trust me to find things out without givin’ myself away. I won’t never let her know what I want right out, but I’ll talk it out o’ her.”

===Chinese===
“那他带着孩子做什么呢？”

“这正是我要去打探的。我这就去普赖姆小姐家。把你的披巾借我，梅尔维妮。”

“你总不敢当面问她吧？”

“你放心，我有法子打听出来，还不露马脚。我绝不直通通告诉她我想知道什么，我要一点点把话套出来。”

===Original===
“What a woman you air, Sallie Martin!” said Mrs. Smith, admiringly. “But do hurry back an’ tell me what she says: I’m jest dyin’ to know.”

“I’ll be back in little or no time, because I can’t stay, nohow.”

Mrs. Martin threw the borrowed shawl over her head and set off down the street. She and her friend were not dwellers on the mean street, and so they could pretend to so nearly an equal social footing with Miss Prime as to admit of an occasional neighbourly call.

Through the window Miss Prime saw her visitor approaching, and a grim smile curved the corners of her mouth. “Comin’ fur news,” muttered the spinster. “She’ll git all she wants before she goes.” But there was no trace of suspicion in her manner as she opened the door at Mrs. Martin’s rap.

===Chinese===
“你可真是个人物，萨莉·马丁！”史密斯太太叹服道，“可得赶紧回来把她说的话告诉我：我都快急死了。”

“我一眨眼就回来，横竖我也待不长。”

马丁太太把借来的披巾往头上一裹，便顺街去了。她和她的朋友不住在那条寒酸街道上，因此还能装出跟普赖姆小姐身份相仿的体面，偶尔登门串个邻里之谊。

普赖姆小姐从窗子里望见来客走近，嘴角弯出一丝冷冷的笑。“打听消息来了，”老小姐咕哝道，“她走之前保准听个够。”可马丁太太叩门时，她开门的神情里没有一丝起疑。

===Original===
“Hey oh, Miss Hester, busy as usual, I see.”

“Yes, indeed. People that try to do their dooty ain’t got much time fur rest in this world.”

“No, indeed; it’s dig, dig, dig, and work, work, work.”

“Take off yore shawl an’ set down, Sallie. It’s a wonder you don’t take yore death o’ cold or git plum full o’ neuralgy, a-runnin’ around in this weather with nothin’ but a shawl over yore head.”

===Chinese===
“哎哟，海斯特小姐，照旧忙得很哪。”

“可不。在这世上想尽本分的人，是没多少工夫歇着的。”

“可不是嘛；就是挖呀，挖呀，干呀，干呀，没个完。”

“把披巾解了坐下吧，萨莉。这种天儿，头上就搭条披巾满处跑，竟没冻死、没落下一身神经痛，真是奇迹。”

===Original===
“La, Miss Hester, they say that worthless people’s hard to kill. It ain’t allus true, though, fur there was poor Margar’t Brent, she wasn’t worth much, but my! she went out like a match.”

“Yes, but matches don’t go out until their time ef they’re held down right; an’ it’s jest so with people.”

“That’s true enough, Miss Hester. Was you to Margar’t’s funeral?”

“Oh, yes, I went.”

===Chinese===
“嗐，海斯特小姐，人家说没出息的人命硬。这话也不尽然，可怜的玛格丽特·布伦特，她也没什么出息，可是老天！她跟根火柴似的，一吹就灭了。”

“话是这么说，可火柴要是按得得当，不到时候也不灭；人也是一样。”

“这话不假，海斯特小姐。玛格丽特的葬礼你去了吗？”

“去了，去了。”

===Original===
“Did you go out to the cimetery?”

“Oomph huh.”

“Did she look natural?”

“Jest as natural as one could expect after a hard life an’ a hard death.”

===Chinese===
“送出城到墓园去了吗？”

“嗯哼。”

“她脸色还自然吗？”

“苦了一辈子、又苦了一场死，能指望的样子，也就是那样了。”

===Original===
“Pore Margar’t!” Mrs. Martin sighed. There was a long and embarrassed silence. Miss Prime’s lips were compressed, and she seemed more aggressively busy than usual. She bustled about as if every minute were her last one. She brushed off tables, set chairs to rights, and tried the golden-brown cupcake with a straw to see if it were done. Her visitor positively writhed with curiosity and discomfiture. Finally she began again. “Margar’t only had one child, didn’t she?”

“Yes, that was all.”

“Pore little lamb. Motherless childern has a hard time of it.”

“Indeed, most of ’em do.”

===Chinese===
“可怜的玛格丽特！”马丁太太叹了口气。接着是长长一段难堪的沉默。普赖姆小姐抿紧嘴唇，比平时更加卖力地忙活，仿佛过一分钟少一分钟似的团团转。她掸桌子，摆正椅子，还拿根麦秆戳戳那黄澄澄的杯形蛋糕，看烤透了没有。她的客人简直被好奇心和窘迫折磨得坐立不安。末了她又开了口：“玛格丽特就一个孩子，是吧？”

“是啊，就那么一个。”

“可怜的小羊羔。没娘的孩子日子难过啊。”

“可不是，多半都难。”

===Original===
“Do you know what’s become of the child, Miss Hester?”

“Yes, I do, Sallie Martin, an’ you do too, or you wouldn’t be a-settin’ there beatin’ about the bush, askin’ me all these questions.”

This sudden outburst gave Mrs. Martin quite a turn, but she exclaimed, “I declare to goodness, Miss Hester, I ain’t heerd a livin’ thing about it, only﻿—”

She checked herself, but her relentless hostess caught at the word and demanded, “Only what, Mis’ Martin?”

===Chinese===
“你知道那孩子后来怎样了吗，海斯特小姐？”

“我知道，萨莉·马丁，你也知道；不然你不会坐在那儿绕来绕去，问我这一大堆话。”

这突如其来的发作吓了马丁太太一大跳，可她还是叫起屈来：“我对天发誓，海斯特小姐，这事我半个字都没听说过，只不过——”

她赶紧收住话头，可那不肯放过人的女主人一把揪住那个字眼，追问道：“只不过什么，马丁太太？”

===Original===
“Well, I seen Brother ’Liphalet Hodges takin’ him away from here in his buggy﻿—”

“An’ so you come down to see what was what, eh, so’s you could be the first to tell the neighbourhood?”

“Now, Miss Hester, you know that I ain’t one o’ them that talks, but I do feel sich an interest in the pore motherless child, an’ when I seen Brother Hodges a-takin’ him away, I thought perhaps he was a-goin’ to take him to raise.”

“Well, Brother Hodges ain’t a-goin’ to take him to raise.”

===Chinese===
“这个，我瞧见埃利法莱特·霍奇斯弟兄用他的马车把他从这儿带走了——”

“于是你就跑来探个究竟，嗯，好抢在头里把这街坊四邻都告诉遍？”

“嗐，海斯特小姐，你知道我不是那种搬弄是非的人，可我这心里实在惦记那没娘的可怜孩子；我瞧见霍奇斯弟兄把他带走，还当他是要领去抚养呢。”

“霍奇斯弟兄可不打算领他去抚养。”

===Original===
“Mercy sakes! Miss Hester, don’t git mad, but who is?”

“I am, that’s who.”

“Miss Prime, what air you a-sayin’? You shorely don’t mean it. What kin you do with a child?”

“I kin train him up in the way he ought to go, an’ keep him out o’ other people’s houses an’ the street.”

===Chinese===
“老天爷！海斯特小姐，你可别恼——那到底是谁呢？”

“是我，没别人。”

“普赖姆小姐，你说的是哪门子话？你总不会是当真的吧。你拿个孩子怎么办呢？”

“我能教养他走他当走的路，不许他泡在别人家里和大街上。”

===Original===
“Well, o’ course, that’s somethin’,” said Mrs. Martin, weakly.

“Somethin’? Why, it’s everything.”

The visitor had now gotten the information for which she was looking, and was anxious to be gone. She was absolutely bursting with her news.

“Well, I must be goin’,” she said, replacing her shawl and standing in embarrassed indecision. “I only run in fur a minute. I hope you ain’t got no hard feelin’s at my inquisitiveness.”

===Chinese===
“嗐，那当然，也算是一桩了。”马丁太太有气无力地说。

“一桩？嗐，这就是全部。”

来客这时已经拿到了她要打探的消息，急着想走。她满肚子的新闻，眼看就要撑破了。

“嗐，我该走了，”她说着重新裹上披巾，进退两难地站着，一脸窘态，“我只是进来坐一分钟的。我这爱打听，你可别见怪。”

===Original===
“Not a bit of it. You wanted to know, an’ you come and asked, that’s all.”

“I hope you’ll git along all right with the child.”

“I shan’t stop at hopin’. I shall take the matter to the Lord in prayer.”

“Yes, He knows best. Goodbye, Miss Hester.”

===Chinese===
“一点儿也不。你想知道，就来问了，就这么回事。”

“盼你跟那孩子处得顺顺当当。”

“我可不止于盼着。我要把这事在祷告里交托给主。”

“是，祂最知道好歹。再见了，海斯特小姐。”

===Original===
“Goodbye, Sallie; come in ag’in.” The invitation sounded a little bit sarcastic, and once more the grim smile played about Miss Prime’s mouth.

“I ’low,” she observed to herself, as she took the cake from the oven for the last time, tried it, and set it on the table﻿—“I ’low that I did give Sallie Martin one turn. I never did see sich a woman fur pryin’ into other folks’ business.”

Swift are the wings of gossip, and swift were the feet of Mrs. Sallie Martin as she hurried back to tell the news to her impatient friend, who listened speechless with enjoyment and astonishment.

“Who would ’a’ thought you could ’a’ talked it out o’ her so?” she gasped.

===Chinese===
“再见，萨莉；有空再来。”这邀请听着有点儿挖苦，冷冷的笑意又一次在普赖姆小姐嘴角边荡漾。

“我估摸着，”她自言自语道，一边最后一次把蛋糕从炉里取出来，试了试，摆上桌——“我估摸着，我算是结结实实唬了萨莉·马丁一跳。爱刨根问底打听别人闲事的女人，我还没见过第二个。”

闲话的翅膀飞得快，萨莉·马丁太太的脚也快，她一路赶回去，把消息讲给那位望眼欲穿的朋友；后者听得目瞪口呆，又惊又受用。

“谁能想到，你竟这样从她嘴里把话套了出来？”她倒抽一口气说。

===Original===
“Oh, I led her right along tell she told me everything,” said Mrs. Martin, with a complacency which, remembering her reception, she was far from feeling.

Shortly after her departure, and while, no doubt, reinforced by Mrs. Smith, she was still watching at the window, ’Liphalet Hodges drove leisurely up to the door again.

“Well, Freddie,” he said, as he helped the child to alight, “we’ve had a great time together, we have, an’ we ain’t frozen, neither: I told Miss Prime that she needn’t be afeared. Don’t drop yore jumpin’-jack, now, an’ be keerful an’ don’t git yore hands on yore apron, ’cause they’re kind o’ sticky. Miss Hester ’u’d take our heads off ef we come back dirty.”

The child’s arms were full of toys﻿—a jumping-jack, a climbing monkey, a popgun, and the etceteras of childish amusement﻿—and his pockets and cheeks bulged with candy.

===Chinese===
“嗐，我牵着她一路说下去，直到她把什么都倒了出来。”马丁太太一副得意的神气——只是想起自己挨的那顿抢白，她实在得意不到哪儿去。

她走后不久——想必那会儿有史密斯太太助阵，她还在窗前守着望呢——埃利法莱特·霍奇斯又不慌不忙地赶着车到了门口。

“嗐，弗雷迪，”他一边扶孩子下车一边说，“咱们爷儿俩玩得痛快，是吧，也没冻着：我跟普赖姆小姐说了，她用不着怕。当心别摔了你的跳跳偶，留神别把手往围裙上搁，手上黏糊糊的。咱们要是脏着回来，海斯特小姐非把咱们的脑袋拧下来不可。”

孩子怀里抱满了玩具——跳跳偶、爬猴、玩具枪，还有孩子们耍的那些零七八碎——口袋和腮帮子都被糖果撑得鼓鼓的。

===Original===
“La, ’Liphalet,” exclaimed Miss Prime, when she saw them, “what on airth have you been a-buyin’ that child﻿—jumpin’-jacks an’ sich things? They ain’t a bit o’ good, ’ceptin’ to litter up a house an’ put lightness in childern’s minds. Freddie, what’s that on yore apron? Goodness me! an’ look at them hands﻿—candy! ’Liphalet Hodges, I did give you credit fur better jedgment than this. Candy is the cause o’ more aches an’ pains than poison; an’ some of it’s reelly coloured with ars’nic. How do you expect a child to grow up healthy an’ with sound teeth when you feed him on candy?”

“Now, Miss Hester, now, now, now. I don’t want to be a-interferin’ with yore bus’ness; but it’s jest like I said before, an’ I will stick to it, you ain’t never had no experunce in raisin’ children. They can’t git along jest on meat an’ bread an’ jam: they need candy﻿—an’﻿—ah﻿—candy﻿—an’ sich things.” Mr. Hodges ended lamely, looking rather guiltily at the boy’s bulging pockets. “A little bit ain’t a-goin’ to hurt no child.”

“ ’Liphalet, I’ve got a dooty to perform towards this motherless child, an’ I ain’t a-goin’ to let no foolish notions keep me from performin’ it.”

“Miss Hester, I’m a-tryin’ to follow Him that was a father to the fatherless an’ a husband to the widow﻿—strange, that was made only to the widow﻿—an’ I’ve got somethin’ of a idee o’ dooty myself. You may think I’m purty presumptuous, but I’ve took a notion into my head to kind o’ help along a-raisin’ Freddie. I ain’t a-goin’ to question yore authority, or nothin’, but I thought mebbe you’d len’ me the child once in a while to kind o’ lighten up that old lonesome place o’ mine: I know that Freddie won’t object.”

===Chinese===
“嗐，埃利法莱特，”普赖姆小姐一见他们便嚷起来，“你到底给孩子买的这些什么玩意儿——跳跳偶之类的东西？这些一点好处没有，只会把屋里弄得乱七八糟，把孩子的脑子弄得轻飘飘。弗雷迪，你围裙上是什么？老天爷！再瞧瞧那双手——糖果！埃利法莱特·霍奇斯，我原当你见识比这强。糖果招的病痛比毒药还多；有些还真是拿砒霜上的色。拿糖果喂孩子，你还指望他长得结实、牙齿牢靠？”

“哎，海斯特小姐，哎，哎，哎。我不是要插手你的事；可就像我先前说的，这话我还要说，你从来就没有养孩子的经验。光靠肉啊、面包啊、果酱啊，孩子过不下去：他们得要糖——还有——呃——糖——还有这类东西。”霍奇斯先生结尾结得没劲儿，眼望着孩子鼓鼓的口袋，颇有几分心虚。“一点儿糖，坏不了孩子。”

“埃利法莱特，我对这没娘的孩子负有一份本分，什么糊涂念头也别想拦着我不去尽它。”

“海斯特小姐，我尽力跟着祂学——祂作无父者的父，作寡妇的丈夫——怪的是，只说作寡妇的丈夫——我自家对本分也略有一二想法。你兴许觉得我自不量力，可我脑子里起了个念头，想搭把手，帮着把弗雷德拉扯大。我不是要分你的权，绝没有那意思；只是想着，兴许你肯时不时把孩子借给我，让我那老冷清的家里也添点儿活气：我知道弗雷迪不会不乐意。”

===Original===
“Oh, ’Liphalet, do go ’long: I scarcely know whether you air a man or a child, sometimes.”

“There’s One that says, ‘Except you become as a little child’﻿—”

“ ’Liphalet, will you go ’long home?”

“I’spect I’d better be gittin’ along.﻿—Goodbye, Freddie; be a good boy, an’ some day I’ll take you up to my house an’ let you ride old Bess around.﻿—Goodbye, Miss Hester.” And as he passed out to his buggy he whistled tenderly something that was whistled when he was a boy.

===Chinese===
“嗐，埃利法莱特，快去吧：有时候我简直分不清，你到底是个大人还是个孩子。”

“有一位说过，‘你们若不回转，变成小孩子的样式’——”

“埃利法莱特，你还回不回家去了？”

“我看我还是走吧。——再见了，弗雷迪；乖乖的，赶明儿我带你上我家，让你骑着老贝斯遛一圈。——再见，海斯特小姐。”他出去上马车的时候，轻轻地吹起了一支口哨曲子，那还是他小时候流行的调子。

## VI / 六

===Original===
The life of one boy is much like that of another. They all have their joys and their griefs, their triumphs and their failures, their loves and their hates, their friends and their foes, much as men have them in that maturer life of which the days of youth are an epitome. It would be rather an uninteresting task, and an entirely thankless one, to follow in detail the career of Frederick Brent as he grew from childhood to youth. But in order to understand certain traits that developed in his character, it will be necessary to note some, at least, of the circumstances that influenced his early life.

While Miss Prime grew to care for him in her own unemotional way, she had her own notions of how a boy should be trained, and those notions seemed to embody the repression of every natural impulse. She reasoned thus: “Human beings are by nature evil: evil must be crushed: ergo, everything natural must be crushed.” In pursuance of this principle, she followed out a deliberate course of restriction, which, had it not been for the combating influence of Eliphalet Hodges, would have dwarfed the mental powers of the boy and cramped his soul beyond endurance. When he came of an age to play marbles, he was forbidden to play, because it was, to Miss Hester’s mind, a species of gambling. Swimming was too dangerous to be for a moment considered. Fishing, without necessity, was wanton cruelty. Flying kites was foolishness and a waste of time.

The boy had shown an aptitude at his lessons that had created in his guardian’s mind some ambition for him, and she held him down to his books with rigid assiduity. He was naturally studious, but the feeling that he was being driven made his tasks repellent, although he performed them without outward sign of rebellion, while he fumed within.

===Chinese===
一个男孩子的日子，跟另一个男孩子的日子大同小异。他们都有自己的欢乐与哀愁、得意与失意、爱与恨、朋友与冤家，一如大人们在他们那段更成熟的岁月里所有的——少年的时光，本就是那段岁月的缩影。要把弗雷德里克·布伦特从孩提到少年间的经历一一追述下来，未免太乏味，也太不讨巧。可要理解后来在他性格里显露出来的某些特点，至少得留意一下影响他早年生活的若干境遇。

普赖姆小姐固然以她那种不动声色的方式日渐疼爱他，可她自有一套男孩该怎么教养的主张，而那套主张，似乎就是把一切自然的天性统统压制下去才算数。她的推理是：“人性本恶：恶必扑灭：所以，凡是天性，必在扑灭之列。”本着这条原则，她推行了一套深思熟虑的钳制方案；若不是有埃利法莱特·霍奇斯从中抗衡化解，这套方案非把孩子的心智压得发育不良、把他的灵魂拘束得难以忍受不可。到了玩弹子的年纪，他却不许玩弹子，因为在海斯特小姐看来，那是一种赌钱。游泳太危险，想都不用想。无缘无故去钓鱼，是无谓的残忍。放风筝是蠢事，是浪费时间。

这孩子功课上显露的天分，让监护人心里对他起了一番抱负，于是她把他死死按在书本上，容不得半点懈怠。他天性本也好学，可那种被人驱赶着的感觉叫功课变得面目可憎；只是他把功课照做不误，外表没有一丝反抗的迹象，肚子里却憋着一团火。

===Original===
His greatest relaxations were his trips to and from his old friend Hodges. If Miss Prime crushed him, this gentle soul comforted him and smoothed out his ruffled feelings. It was this influence that kept him from despair. Away from his guardian, he was as if a chain that galled his flesh had been removed. And yet he could not hate Miss Hester, for it was constantly impressed upon him that all was being done for his good, and the word “duty” was burned like a fiery cross upon his heart and brain.

There is a bit of the pagan in every natural boy, and to give him too much to reverence taxes his powers until they are worn and impotent by the time he reaches manhood. Under Miss Hester’s tutelage too many things became sacred to Fred Brent. It was wicked to cough in church, as it was a sacrilege to play with a hymnbook. His training was the apotheosis of the nonessential. But, after all, there is no rebel like Nature. She is an iconoclast.

When he was less than ten years old, an incident occurred that will in a measure indicate the manner of his treatment. Miss Prime’s prescription for making a good boy was two parts punishment, two parts admonition, and six parts prayer. Accordingly, as the watchful and sympathetic neighbours said, “she an’ that pore child fairly lived in church.”

It was one class-meeting night, and, as usual, the boy and his guardian were sitting side by side at church. It was the habit of some of the congregation to bring their outside controversies into the classroom under the guise of testimonies or exhortations, and there to air their views where their opponents could not answer them. One such was Daniel Hastings. The trait had so developed in him that whenever he rose to speak, the question ran around, “I wonder who Dan’l’s a-goin’ to rake over the coals now.” On this day he had been having a tilt with his old-time enemy, Thomas Donaldson, over the advent into Dexter of a young homeopathic doctor. With characteristic stubbornness, Dan’l had held that there was no good in any but the old-school medical men, and he sneered at the idea of anybody’s being cured with sugar, as he contemptuously termed the pellets and powders affected by the new school. Thomas, who was considered something of a wit and who sustained his reputation by the perpetration of certain timeworn puns, had replied that other hogs were sugar-cured, and why not Dan’l? This had turned the laugh on Hastings, and he went home from the corner grocery, where the men were congregated, in high dudgeon.

===Chinese===
他最大的松快，便是往返老朋友霍奇斯家的那几趟路。普赖姆小姐若是压制他，这个温和的人便宽慰他，把他揉皱的心一一抚平。正是这份影响，才使他没陷于绝望。一离开监护人，他就像卸下了磨得皮肉生疼的锁链。然而他恨不起海斯特小姐来，因为总有人在他耳边敲打：这一切全是为他好；“本分”两个字，像一具火红的十字烙在他的心上、脑子里。

凡是天性健全的男孩子，心里都存着一点异教的根苗；给他的敬畏太多，透支了他的心神，等他长大成人，那份心力便磨损得所剩无几了。在海斯特小姐的调教下，对弗雷德·布伦特来说，变得神圣不可侵犯的东西实在太多了。在教堂里咳嗽是罪过，翻弄赞美诗集是亵渎。他受的教养，简直是把无关紧要之事奉若神明。可是说到底，天下没有谁比得上“天性”这个造反派。她专会捣毁偶像。

他还不满十岁的时候，出过一桩事，多少可以说明他受的是怎样的管教。普赖姆小姐造就好孩子的方子是：两份责罚，两份训诫，六份祷告。难怪那些眼睛尖、心肠热的街坊都说，“她跟那可怜的孩子简直就住在教堂里。”

那晚是班组聚会，孩子跟监护人照例并排坐在教堂里。会众里有些人惯于把外头的私怨带进班会上来，借着作见证或劝勉的名目，趁对手无法还嘴的当口宣扬自己的主张。丹尼尔·黑斯廷斯便是其中一位。他这脾性发展到这样的地步：只要他一起身讲话，底下就传开一句话：“不知这回丹尼尔要把谁架在火上烤。”这一天，他刚跟老对头托马斯·唐纳森斗了一场嘴，为的是一位年轻的顺势疗法医生来到了德克斯特。丹尼尔凭着那股特有的倔劲儿，咬定除了旧派的郎中，旁的医生一概不行；还说谁要能叫糖把病治好，他嗤之以鼻——“糖”是他对新派爱用的药丸药粉的蔑称。托马斯素来被大家当作一个机灵人，也靠几句用滥了的双关语维持着这份名声；他回敬道：别的猪都能用糖腌着治，丹尼尔怎么就不能？这一句惹得众人大笑，黑斯廷斯败下阵来，从众人聚会的街角杂货铺憋着一肚子气回了家。

===Original===
Still smarting with the memory of his defeat, when he rose to speak that evening, he cast a glance full of unfriendly significance at his opponent and launched into a fiery exhortation on true religion. “Some folks’ religion,” he said, “is like sugar, all sweetness and no power; but I want my religion like I want my medicine: I want it strong, an’ I want it bitter, so’s I’ll know I’ve got it.” In Fred Brent the sense of humour had not been entirely crushed, and the expression was too much for his gravity. He bowed his head and covered his mouth with his hand. He made no sound, but there were three pairs of eyes that saw the movement﻿—Miss Prime’s, Eliphalet Hodges’, and the Rev. Mr. Simpson’s. Miss Prime’s gaze was horrified, Mr. Simpson’s stern; but in the eye of Mr. Hodges there was a most ungodly twinkle.

When Dan’l Hastings had finished his exhortation﻿—which was in reality an arraignment of Thomas Donaldson’s medical heresies﻿—and sat down, the Rev. Mr. Simpson arose, and, bending an accusing glance upon the shrinking boy, began: “I perceive on the part of some of the younger members of the congregation a disposition towards levity. The house of God is not the place to find amusement. I never see young people deriding their elders without thinking of the awful lesson taught by the Lord’s judgment upon those wicked youths whom the she-bears devoured. I never see a child laughing in church without trembling in spirit for his future. Some of the men whom I have seen in prison, condemned to death or a life of confinement, have begun their careers just in this way, showing disrespect for their elders and for the church. Beware, young people, who think you are smart and laugh and titter in the sanctuary; there is a prison waiting for you, there is a hell yawning for you. Behold, there is death in the pot!”

With a terrible look at the boy, Mr. Simpson sat down. There was much craning of necks and gazing about, but few in the church would have known to whom the pastor’s remarks were addressed had not Miss Prime, at their conclusion, sighed in an injured way, and, rising with set lips, led the culprit out, as a criminal is led to the scaffold. How the boy suffered as, with flaming face, he walked down the aisle to the door, the cynosure of all eyes! He saw in the faces about him the accusation of having done a terrible thing, something unheard of and more wicked than he could understand. He felt revolted, child as he was, at the religion that made so much of his fault. Inwardly, he vowed that he would never “get religion” or go into a church when he was big enough to have his own way.

They had not gone far when a step approached them from behind, and Eliphalet Hodges joined them. Miss Prime turned tragically at his greeting, and broke out, “Don’t reproach me ’Liphalet; it ain’t no trainin’ o’ mine that’s perduced a child that laughs at old foks in the Lord’s house.”

===Chinese===
那天晚上他起来讲话时，败阵的余痛犹在，便朝对手投去满含敌意的一瞥，随即就“真正的宗教”发起一场火辣辣的劝勉。“有些人的宗教，”他说，“就像糖，光有甜味，没有力量；我要我的宗教，就像我要我的药：要烈，要苦，这样我才知道自己吃下了它。”弗雷德·布伦特心里的幽默感还没有被斩尽杀绝，这句话实在超出了他的严肃所能承受的限度。他低下头，用手捂住嘴。他没有出声，可这动作有三双眼睛瞧见了——普赖姆小姐的、埃利法莱特·霍奇斯的，还有辛普森牧师先生的。普赖姆小姐的目光里满是惊骇，辛普森先生的严峻无比；可霍奇斯先生眼睛里，却闪着一点顶不虔诚的笑意。

丹尼尔·黑斯廷斯结束了他那篇劝勉——其实是对托马斯·唐纳森医学异端的控诉——坐下之后，辛普森牧师先生站起身来，把责难的目光投向那个缩着身子的孩子，开腔道：“我察觉会众里某些年纪较轻的成员存着轻浮之心。上帝的殿不是寻开心的地方。每逢看见年轻人嘲笑长辈，我就想起主的审判临到那些恶少年、被母熊撕咬的可怕教训。每逢看见孩子在教堂里发笑，我就为他的前程瑟瑟发抖。我在监狱里见过的一些人，被判了死刑或终身监禁，他们正是这样起步的——不敬长辈，不敬教会。当心啊，你们这些自作聪明、在圣所里嬉笑的年轻人；牢房等着你们，地狱张着口等你们。看哪，锅中有致死的毒物！”

辛普森先生狠狠瞪了那孩子一眼，坐下了。教堂里许多人伸长脖子四下张望，可若不是普赖姆小姐在他说完时负气地叹了口气、抿着嘴站起身、像押赴刑场似的把罪人领了出去，会众里怕没有几人知道牧师这番话是对谁而发。那孩子涨红着脸走过通向大门的过道，成为全场目光的焦点，那份罪受得才叫深重！他在周围的脸上读出的，是控告他做下了一件可怕的事，一件闻所未闻、比他所能理解的还要邪恶的事。他虽然还是个孩子，却对这样小题大做的宗教生出了反感。他在心里发誓：等他大了、能自己做主了，绝不“信教”，也绝不进教堂。

他们没走出多远，身后赶上来一串脚步，埃利法莱特·霍奇斯与他们同行了。听见他的招呼，普赖姆小姐悲悲切切地转过身，爆发了：“别责备我，埃利法莱特；会出这么个在主的殿里笑话长辈的孩子，可不是我教养出来的。”

===Original===
“I ain’t a-goin’ to reproach you, Miss Hester, never you fear; I ain’t a-goin’ to say a word ag’in’ yore trainin’; but I jest thought I’d ask you not to be too hard on Freddie. You know that Dan’l is kind o’ tryin’ sometimes even to the gravity of older people; an’ childern will be childern; they ain’t got the sense, nor﻿—nor﻿—the deceit to keep a smooth face when they’re a-laughin’ all in their innards.”

Miss Prime turned upon him in righteous wrath. “ ’Liphalet,” she exclaimed, “I think it’s enough fur this child to struggle ag’inst natural sin, without encouragin’ him by makin’ excuses fur him.”

“It ain’t my intention nor my desire to set a bad example before nobody, especially the young lambs of the flock, but I ain’t a-goin’ to blame Freddie fur doin’ what many another of us wanted to do.”

“ ’Deed an’ double, that is fine talk fur you, ’Liphalet Hodges! you a trustee of the church, an’ been a class-leader, a-holdin’ up fur sich onregenerate carryin’s-on.”

===Chinese===
“我不是来责备你的，海斯特小姐，你放心；你那套教养，我一个字也不说；我只是想求你，别对弗雷迪太狠了。你知道，就连大人的持重，有时也架不住丹尼尔那一套；孩子到底是孩子；他们没那份心计，也没——没那份假正经，肚子里笑开了花，还能绷着一张脸。”

普赖姆小姐义愤填膺地转过身冲着他。“埃利法莱特，”她嚷道，“这孩子够费劲的了，要跟天生的罪性搏斗，你再替他描补开脱，这不是给他打气吗。”

“我无意也不愿给谁立坏榜样，尤其是羊群里的羔羊们；可要是因为弗雷迪做了一件咱们许多人心里都想做的事就责备他，这事我不干。”

“当真了，还加倍地当真！这话由你说出来可真漂亮，埃利法莱特·霍奇斯！你还是教会的执事，当过班长的人，居然替这种不悔改的胡闹撑腰。”

===Original===
“I ain’t a-holdin’ up fur nothin’, Miss Hester, ’ceptin’ nature an’ the very couldn’t-help-it-ness o’ the thing altogether. I ain’t a boy no more, by a good many years, but there’s times when I’ve set under Dan’l Hastings’s testimonies jest mortally cramped to laugh; an’ ef it’s so with a man, how will it be with a pore innercent child? I ain’t a-excusin’ natural sin in nobody. It wa’n’t so much Freddie’s natural sin as it was Dan’l’s natural funniness.” And there was something very like a chuckle in ’Liphalet’s throat.

“ ’Liphalet, the devil’s been puttin’ fleas into yore ear, but I ain’t a-goin’ to let you argy me out o’ none o’ my settled convictions, although the Old Man’s put plenty of argyment into yore head. That’s his way o’ capturin’ a soul.﻿—Walk on ahead, Frederick, an’ don’t be list’nin’. I’ll ’tend to yore case later on.”

“It’s funny to me, Miss Hester, how it is that Christians know so much more about the devil’s ways than they do about the Lord’s. They’re allus a-sayin’, ‘the Lord moves in a mysterious way,’ but they kin allus put their finger on the devil.”

“ ’Liphalet Hodges, that’s a slur!”

===Chinese===
“我什么也不撑腰，海斯特小姐，我只替两样东西说话：一样是天性，一样是这事压根儿由不得人的那股子劲儿。我早就不做孩子好多年了，可有时候，我坐在底下听丹尼尔·黑斯廷斯作见证，也憋得要死，差一点笑出来；大人尚且如此，一个可怜的、天真的孩子又怎么绷得住？我不是替谁的天生罪性开脱。弗雷迪那算不得什么天生罪性，丹尼尔的天生滑稽才是。”埃利法莱特的喉咙里，滚过一声极像轻笑的东西。

“埃利法莱特，魔鬼往你耳朵里放了跳蚤了；可任凭那老家伙往你脑袋里灌了多少论辩的词儿，也别想把我定下的信念辩掉一丝一毫。那老家伙就是这么俘获灵魂的。——往前走，弗雷德里克，别在这儿听。你的案子，回头我自有处置。”

“我就纳闷，海斯特小姐，怎么基督徒对魔鬼的门道，比对主的门道知道得还多。他们老把‘主的行事奥秘’挂在嘴上，可对魔鬼的行踪，他们回回都能一指头点中。”

“埃利法莱特·霍奇斯，你这是讥讽！”

===Original===
“I ain’t a-meanin’ it as no slur, Miss Hester; but most Christians do seem to have a powerful fondness for the devil. I notice that they’re allus admirin’ his work an’ praisin’ up his sharpness, an’ they’d be monstrous disappointed ef he didn’t git as many souls as they expect.”

“Well, after all the years that I’ve been a-workin’ in the church an’ a-tryin’ to let my light so shine before the world, I didn’t think that you’d be the one to throw out hints about my Christianity. But we all have our burdens to bear, an’ I’m a-goin’ to bear mine the best I kin, an’ do my dooty, whatever comes of it.” And Miss Hester gave another sigh of injured rectitude.

“I see, Miss Hester, that you’re jest bent an’ bound not to see what I mean, so I might as well go home.”

“I think my mind ain’t givin’ way yit, an’ I believe that I do understand plain words; but I ain’t a-bearin’ you no grudge. You’ve spoke yore mind, an’ it’s all right.”

===Chinese===
“我没有讥讽的意思，海斯特小姐；只是大多数基督徒对魔鬼好像真有一份割舍不掉的疼爱。我留意到，他们总在称羡魔鬼的作为，夸他手段高明；魔鬼要是少捞了几个灵魂、没达到他们的指望，他们怕要失望透了。”

“唉，我在教会里辛苦了这么多年，一直想让我的光这样照在人前，没承想，偏偏是你来暗指我的基督教信仰成色不足。不过，人人都有一副担子要挑，我总要尽我所能挑起我的，尽我的本分，管它落个什么结果。”海斯特小姐又发出一声受了委屈的正人君子式的叹息。

“我看出来了，海斯特小姐，你是铁了心不肯明白我的意思，那我还是回家去吧。”

“我想我的脑子还没糊涂，明白话我听得懂；我也不记你的仇。你把心里话说出来了，这就没什么。”

===Original===
“But I hope there ain’t no hard feelin’s, after all these years.”

“Oh, ’Liphalet, it ain’t a part of even my pore weak religion to bear hard feelin’s towards no one, no matter how they treat me. I’m jest tryin’ to bear my cross an’ suffer fur the Lord’s sake.”

“But I hope I ain’t a-givin’ you no cross to bear. I ain’t never doubted yore goodness or yore Christianity: I only thought that mebbe yore methods, yore methods﻿—”

Miss Prime’s lips were drawn into a line. She divided that line to say, “I know what the Scriptures say: ’If thy right hand offend thee’﻿—”

===Chinese===
“只是处了这么多年，我希望你别存芥蒂。”

“嗐，埃利法莱特，别说我是这么个可怜的、软弱的信徒，就是我的宗教，也容不得我记恨任何人，随他们怎样待我。我不过是想背起我的十字架，为主受苦罢了。”

“可我不指望是我给你十字架背。我从不怀疑你的良善，也不怀疑你的信仰：我只是想着，兴许你的法子，你的法子——”

普赖姆小姐的双唇抿成了一条线。她把那条线抿开，说道：“我知道《圣经》上是怎么说的：‘若是你的右手叫你跌倒’——”

===Original===
“Hester, Hester!” he cried, stretching out his hands to her.

“Good night, Brother Hodges. I must go in.” She turned and left him standing at the gate with a hurt look in his face.

On going into the house, Miss Hester did not immediately ’tend to Fred, as she had promised. Instead, she left him and went into her own room where she remained awhile. When she came out, her lips were no less set, but her eyes were red. It is hardly to be supposed that she had been indulging in that solace of woman’s woes, a good cry.

“Take off yore jacket, Freddie,” she said, calmly, taking down a switch from over the clothespress. “I’m a-goin’ to whip you; but, remember, I ain’t a-punishin’ you because I’m mad. It’s fur the purpose of instruction. It’s fur yore own good.”

===Chinese===
“海斯特，海斯特！”他喊着，朝她伸出双手。

“晚安，霍奇斯弟兄。我该进去了。”她转身进屋，把他一个人留在门口，脸上带着受伤的神情。

进了屋，海斯特小姐并没有像她许诺的那样立刻处置弗雷德。她撂下他，进了自己的房间，待了好一阵。出来的时候，她的嘴唇抿得照旧紧，眼睛却红了。谁也不会当她是躲在里面享受女人家消愁的灵药——痛痛快快哭了一场。

“把褂子脱了，弗雷迪，”她平静地说，从衣橱顶上取下一根笞条。“我要打你了；可是记住，我打你不是因为恼你。是为了管教你。是为你好。”

===Original===
Fred received his dressing-down without a whimper. He was too angry to cry. This Miss Prime took as a mark of especial depravity. In fact, the boy had been unable to discover any difference between an instructive and a vindictive whipping. It was perfectly clear in his guardian’s mind, no doubt, but a cherry switch knows no such distinctions.

This incident only prepared Fred Brent for a further infraction of his guardian’s rules the next day. One of Miss Prime’s strictest orders had to do with fighting. Whatever the boys did to Fred, he was never to resent it. He must come to her, he thought, “if I should laugh out while she is whipping me?” And he laughed at the very thought. He was full of pleasure at himself. He had satisfied the impulse within him for once, and it made him happy.

Miss Prime read the ominous note, and looked at her charge thoughtfully. Fred glanced expectantly in the direction of the top of the clothespress. But she only said, “Go out an’ git in yore kindlin’, Freddie; git yore chores done, an’ then come in to supper.” Her voice was menacingly quiet. The boy had learned to read the signs of her face too well to think that he was to get off so easily as this. Evidently, he would “get it” after supper, or Miss Prime had some new, refined mode of punishment in store for him. But what was it? He cudgelled his brain in vain, as he finished his chores, and at table he could hardly eat for wondering. But he might have spared himself his pains, for he learned all too soon.

Immediately after supper he was bidden to put on his cap and come along. Miss Prime took him by the hand. “I’m a-goin’ to take you,” she said, “to beg Willie Tompkins’s pardon fur the way you did him.”

===Chinese===
弗雷德挨了那顿打，一声也没吭。他是气得哭不出来。普赖姆小姐把这一点当作格外堕落的表现。其实，这孩子从来分辨不出“管教的鞭打”和“泄愤的鞭打”有什么不同。在他监护人的心里，这区别无疑一清二楚；可惜樱桃木的笞条不懂得这些分别。

这件事只是为弗雷德·布伦特第二天进一步违犯监护人的规矩做了铺垫。普赖姆小姐最严厉的训令之一，便是关于打架的。不管别的男孩对弗雷德做什么，他万万不可还手，只能回来告诉她。他心里转念道：“要是她抽我的时候，我笑出声来呢？”想到这里，他笑了。他对自己很是得意。他心里那股冲动，这一回总算痛快地遂了一次愿，这叫他快活。

普赖姆小姐读了那张不祥的字条，若有所思地打量她的被监护人。弗雷德满怀期待地朝衣橱顶上瞟了一眼。可她只说：“出去把引火柴抱进来，弗雷迪；把你的活儿干完，就进来吃晚饭。”她的声音平静得吓人。这孩子太会察看她的脸色了，绝想不到这回能这样轻易过关。看样子，“那一顿”要留到晚饭后；再不然，就是普赖姆小姐又备下了什么别致的新刑罚。可究竟是什么呢？他把活儿干完，脑子里翻来覆去，愣是想不出；上了饭桌，疑心得几乎吃不下饭。其实他大可不必费这份神，因为答案很快就揭晓了。

晚饭一过，他便奉命戴上帽子跟人走。普赖姆小姐牵着他的手。“我要带你去，”她说，“为你那样对待威利·汤普金斯，去给他赔个不是。”

===Original===
Did the woman know what it meant to the boy? She could not, or her heart would have turned against the cruelty. Fred was aghast. Beg his pardon! A whipping was a thousand times better: indeed, it would be a mercy. He began to protest, but was speedily silenced. The enforced silence, however, did not cool his anger. He had done what other boys did. He had acted in the only way that it seemed a boy could act under the circumstances, and he had expected to be punished as his fellows were; but this﻿—this was awful. He clinched his hands until the nails dug into the palms. His face was as pale as death. He sweated with the consuming fire of impotent rage. He wished that he might run away somewhere where he could hide and tear things and swear. For a moment only he entertained the thought, and then a look into the determined face of the woman at his side drove the thought away. To his childish eyes, distorted by resentment, she was an implacable and relentless monster who would follow him with punishment anywhere he might go.

And now they were at Billy Tompkins’s door. They had passed through, and he found himself saying mechanically the words which Miss Prime put into his mouth, while his tormentor grinned from beside his mother’s chair. Then, after a few words between the women, in which he heard from Mrs. Tompkins the mysterious words, “Oh, I don’t blame you, Miss Hester; I know that blood will tell,” they passed out, and the grinning face of Billy Tompkins was the last thing that Fred saw. It followed him home. The hot tears fell from his eyes, but they did not quench the flames that were consuming him. There is nothing so terrible as the just anger of a child﻿—terrible in its very powerlessness. Polyphemus is a giant, though the mountain hold him down.

Next morning, when Fred went to school, Billy Tompkins with a crowd of boys about was waiting to deride him; but at sight of his face they stopped. He walked straight up to his enemy and began striking him with all his might.

“She made me beg your pardon, did she?” he gasped between the blows; “well, you take that for it, and that.” The boys had fallen back, and Billy was attempting to defend himself.

===Chinese===
这女人可知道这对那孩子意味着什么？她不可能知道，否则她的心早该起来反对这般残酷了。弗雷德吓呆了。赔不是！挨一千顿鞭子也比这个强：那样反倒算是恩典了。他开始抗辩，却立刻被喝止。可这强按下去的沉默浇不灭他的怒火。他干的是别的男孩都干的事。他照着一个男孩子在那种情形下唯一可行的样子行事，也料到了会像同伴们那样挨罚；可是这个——这个太可怕了。他攥紧拳头，指甲掐进了掌心。他脸白得像死人。无能为力的怒火烧灼着他，逼出一身汗。他恨不得逃到什么地方，躲起来，撕东西，骂娘。这念头只在心里转了一瞬，可他一侧头，看见身边那女人一脸决绝，念头便吓跑了。在他那双被怨愤扭曲的孩童的眼睛里，她是一头穷追不舍、绝不容情的怪物，他逃到哪里，惩罚就跟到哪里。

说着说着就到了比利·汤普金斯家门口。他们进了门，普赖姆小姐一句一句教着，他也便机械地照念；他那折磨者却靠在母亲的椅子旁边咧着嘴笑。随后两个女人寒暄了几句，他从汤普金斯太太口中听到一句神秘的话：“嗐，我不怪你，海斯特小姐；我知道血统是要显形的。”他们告辞出来，比利·汤普金斯那张咧着的嘴脸，是弗雷德看到的最后一眼。它一路跟着他回了家。滚烫的眼泪从他眼里落下来，却浇不灭吞噬他的火焰。没有什么比一个孩子的正当愤怒更可怕的了——可怕就可怕在它全然无能为力。波吕斐摩斯纵然被大山压住，也还是个巨人。

第二天早晨弗雷德上学去，比利·汤普金斯正带着一群男孩等着讥笑他；可一看见他的脸，他们都不作声了。他径直走到对头跟前，抡起全身力气动起手来。

“是她叫我来赔不是的，是不是？”他边打边喘着气说；“那好，这一下是赔它的，还有这下。”男孩们退开了圈，比利招架着。

===Original===
“Mebbe she’ll make me do it again tonight. If she does, I’ll give you some more o’ this tomorrow, and every time I have to beg your pardon. Do you hear?”

The boys cheered lustily, and Billy Tompkins, completely whipped and ashamed, slunk away.

That night no report of the fight went home. Fred Brent held the master hand.

In life it is sometimes God and sometimes the devil that comes to the aid of oppressed humanity. From the means, it is often hard to tell whose handiwork are the results.

===Chinese===
“兴许今晚她还要叫我来赔不是。她要是再叫，我明天就再给你一顿这样的，往后每赔一回不是，我就揍你一顿。听见没有？”

男孩们放声喝彩，比利·汤普金斯被彻底打垮，臊得抬不起头，溜走了。

那天晚上，打架的事没有一个字传回家去。弗雷德·布伦特占住了上风。

人生在世，有时是上帝、有时是魔鬼来搭救受欺压的人们。单看结果，往往分不清那究竟出自谁的手笔。

## VII / 七

===Original===
Cynics and fools laugh at calf-love. Youth, which is wiser, treats it more seriously. When the boy begins to think of a girl, instead of girls, he displays the first budding signs of a real growing manhood. The first passion may be but the enthusiasm of discovery. Sometimes it is not. At times it dies, as fleeting enthusiasms do. Again it lives, and becomes a blessing, a curse, or a memory. Who shall say that the first half-sweet pang that strikes a boy’s heart in the presence of the dear first girl is any less strong, intoxicating, and real to him than that which prompts him to take the full-grown woman to wife? With factitious sincerity we quote, “The boy is father to the man,” and then refuse to believe that the qualities, emotions, and passions of the man are inherited from this same boy﻿—are just the growth, the development, of what was embryonic in him.

Nothing is more serious, more pleasant, and more diverting withal, than a boy’s brooding or exultation﻿—one is the complement of the other﻿—over his first girl. As, to a great extent, a man is moulded by the woman he marries, so to no less a degree is a boy’s character turned and shaped by the girl he adores. Either he descends to her level, or she draws him up, unconsciously, perhaps, to her own plane. Girls are missionaries who convert boys. Boys are mostly heathens. When a boy has a girl, he remembers to put on his cuffs and collars, and he doesn’t put his necktie into his pocket on the way to school.

In a boy’s life, the having of a girl is the setting up of an ideal. It is the new element, the higher something which abashes the unabashed, and makes John, who caused Henry’s nose to bleed, tremble when little Mary stamps her foot. It is like an atheist’s finding God, the sudden recognition of a higher and purer force against which all that he knows is powerless. Why doesn’t John bully Mary? It would be infinitely easier than his former exploit with Henry. But he doesn’t. He blushes in her presence, brings her the best apples, out of which heretofore he has enjoined the boys not to “take a hog-bite,” and, even though the parental garden grow none, comes by flowers for her in some way, queer boyish bouquets where dandelions press shoulders with spring-beauties, daffodils, and roses﻿—strange democracy of flowerdom. He feels older and stronger.

In Fred’s case the object of adoration was no less a person than Elizabeth Simpson, the minister’s daughter. From early childhood they had seen and known each other at school, and between them had sprung up a warm childish friendship, apparently because their ways home lay along the same route. In such companionship the years sped; but Fred was a diffident boy, and he was seventeen and Elizabeth near the same before he began to feel those promptings which made him blushingly offer to carry her book for her as far as he went. She had hesitated, refused, and then assented, as is the manner of her sex and years. It had become a settled thing for them to walk home together, he bearing her burdens, and doing for her any other little service that occurred to his boyish sense of gallantry.

===Chinese===
愤世者和蠢人都要笑话少年的初恋。青春比他们聪明，对它郑重其事。当一个男孩开始想的是“某一个”姑娘，而不是“姑娘们”的时候，他便显露了真正成人的最初的萌芽。初恋也许只是发现的热情。有时却不是。它有时消逝，如同一切转瞬即逝的热情；有时却存活下来，成为福祉、祸根，或一段记忆。谁敢说，那半是甜蜜的悸动初次击中男孩的心——只因为亲爱的第一个姑娘在场——对他而言，就比不上日后促使他迎娶一个成年女子的那股情潮那样强烈、醉人、真切？我们装腔作势地引一句“男孩者，男人之父也”，转过身去却不肯相信：男人的品性、情感和激情，正是从这同一个男孩承袭而来——不过是那胚芽在他身上的抽条与长成。

再没有比一个男孩对初恋姑娘的痴想或狂喜更严肃、更愉快、也更有趣的事了——二者互为表里。一个男人在相当程度上由他所娶的女人塑造，同样，一个男孩的性格也在不相上下的程度上被他倾慕的姑娘扭转、塑造。不是他降到她的水平，就是她把他——也许是不知不觉地——提升到自己的高度。姑娘们是传教士，专司感化男孩。男孩们多半是异教徒。一个男孩有了心上的姑娘，他就记得戴上袖口和领子，也不会在上学的路上把领带塞进口袋了。

在男孩的一生里，有了心上的姑娘，便是立起了一尊理想。它是新的元素，是更高的什么，能叫不知羞的人害臊，能叫打得亨利鼻子流血的约翰，在小玛丽跺脚的时候浑身发抖。这就像无神论者找到了上帝，猛然认出一种更高、更纯的力量，自己所知的一切在它面前全无用处。约翰为什么不欺负玛丽？那可比他先前对亨利的那番武功省事多了。可他不。他在她跟前脸红，把最好的苹果带给她——从前他还叮嘱过别的孩子，啃这些苹果不许“下猪嘴”呢；哪怕自家园子里一样花也不长，他也会想方设法给她弄来花，扎成古怪的孩子气的花束：蒲公英和春美草、黄水仙、玫瑰摩肩接踵——花国里一桩奇怪的民主。他觉得自己又大了些，又强了些。

弗雷德倾慕的对象，不是别人，正是牧师的女儿伊丽莎白·辛普森。打孩提时候起，他们就在学校里抬头不见低头见，两个孩子中间生出了亲热的情谊——表面上看，无非因为他们回家的路同走一段。在这样的同伴情分里，年岁飞驰而过；可弗雷德是个腼腆的男孩，直到他十七岁、伊丽莎白也差不离的年纪，他才开始感到那种鼓动，红着脸提出要替她拿书，一直拿到他要拐弯的地方。她先是迟疑、推辞，随后应允了——这正是她那个年纪的女性共有的做派。一同回家成了他们雷打不动的事：他替她拿东西，凡是他那男孩子气的殷勤所能想到的小差事，他都替她做了。

===Original===
Without will of his own, and without returning the favour, he had grown in the Rev. Mr. Simpson’s esteem. This was due mostly to his guardian’s excellent work. In spite of his rebellion, training and environment had brought him greatly under her control, and when she began to admonish him about his lost condition spiritually she had been able to awaken a sort of superstitious anxiety in the boy’s breast. When Miss Prime perceived that this had been accomplished, she went forthwith to her pastor and unburdened her heart.

“Brother Simpson,” said she, “I feel that the Lord has appointed me an instrument in His hands for bringin’ a soul into the kingdom.” The minister put the tips of his fingers together and sighed piously and encouragingly. “I have been labourin’ with Freddie in the sperrit of Christian industry, an’ I believe that I have finally brought him to a realisin’ sense of his sinfulness.”

“H’m-m,” said the minister. “Bless the Lord for this evidence of the activity of His people. Go on, sister.”

“Freddie has at last come to the conclusion that hell is his lot unless he flees unto the mountain and seeks salvation.”

===Chinese===
他不曾刻意经营，也无从回礼，却渐渐得了辛普森牧师先生的看重。这多半要归功于他监护人的出色工作。尽管心里不服，管教与环境毕竟叫他大大落在了她的手心里；待她开始就他灵魂的沉沦状态训诫他时，她已经能在男孩胸中唤起一种近乎迷信的惶恐了。普赖姆小姐见火候已到，便即刻去找她的牧师，倾吐衷肠。

“辛普森弟兄，”她说，“我觉得，主已经立我作祂手中的器皿，好领一个灵魂进祂的国。”牧师把指尖对在一起，虔诚而鼓舞地叹了口气。“我一直本着基督徒的勤勉，在弗雷迪身上下功夫，我相信，我终于叫他真切地意识到了自己的罪。”

“嗯——”牧师道，“感谢主，祂的子民如此殷勤，有了这个凭据。接着说，姐妹。”

“弗雷迪终于认定，地狱就是他的份，除非他逃往山上，寻求救恩。”

===Original===
“Bless the Lord for this.”

“Now, Brother Simpson, I have done my part as fur as the Lord has showed me, except to ask you to come and wrastle with that boy.”

“Let not thy heart be troubled, Sister Prime, for I will come as you ask me, and I will wrastle with that boy as Jacob did of old with the angel.”

“Oh, Brother Simpson, I knowed you’d come. I know jest how you feel about pore wanderin’ souls, an’ I’m so glad to have yore strong arm and yore wisdom a-helpin’ me.”

===Chinese===
“为此感谢主。”

“辛普森弟兄，主怎样指点我，我已尽了本分，只剩一件事没办：请你来，跟那孩子较一把劲。”

“你心里不要忧愁，普赖姆姐妹，你既求我，我必来，我必像古时的雅各与天使角力那样，跟那孩子角力。”

“哎呀，辛普森弟兄，我就知道你会来。你对可怜的迷途灵魂是怎样一副心肠，我一清二楚；有你强有力的臂膀和智慧帮衬我，我真高兴。”

===Original===
“I hope, my sister, that the Lord may smile upon my poor labours, and permit us to snatch this boy as a brand from eternal burning.”

“We shall have to labour in the sperrit, Brother Simpson.”

“Yes, and with the understanding of the truth in our hearts and minds.”

“I’m shore I feel mighty uplifted by comin’ here today. Do come up to dinner Sunday, dear Brother Simpson, after preachin’.”

===Chinese===
“我盼望，我的姐妹，主肯垂顾我这点微薄的努力，容我们把这孩子从永火里抢出来，像从火中抽出的一根柴。”

“咱们必须在灵里做工，辛普森弟兄。”

“是，还要心存真理的悟性。”

“今天这一趟，我真觉得灵里大得奋兴。礼拜天讲完道，一定来舍下吃顿饭吧，亲爱的辛普森弟兄。”

===Original===
“I will come, Sister Prime, I will come. I know by experience the worth of the table which the Lord provides for you, and then at the same season I may be able to sound this sinful boy as to his spiritual state and to drop some seed into the ground which the Lord has mercifully prepared for our harvest. Goodbye, sister, goodbye. I shall not forget, Sunday after preaching.”

In accordance with his promise, the Rev. Mr. Simpson began to labour with Fred, with the result of driving him into a condition of dogged revolt, which only Miss Prime’s persistence finally overcame. When revival time came round, as, sure as death it must come, Fred regularly went to the mourners’ bench, mourned his few days until he had worked himself into the proper state, and then, somewhat too coldly, it is true, for his anxious guardian, “got religion.”

On the visit next after this which Mr. Simpson paid to Miss Prime, he took occasion to say, “Ah, my sister, I am so glad that you pointed me to that lost lamb of the house of Israel, and I am thanking the Maker every day that He blessed my efforts to bring the straying one into the fold. Ah, there is more joy over the one lamb that is found than over the ninety and nine that went not astray!”

Mr. Simpson’s parishioner acquiesced, but she had some doubts in her mind as to whose efforts the Lord had blessed. She felt a little bit selfish. She wanted to be the author of everything good that came to Fred. But she did not argue with Mr. Simpson. There are some concessions which one must make to one’s pastor.

===Chinese===
“我会来的，普赖姆姐妹，我会来的。主为你摆设的这桌饭食有何等价值，我是有过亲身体验的；届时正好，我也可以探一探这个罪孽的孩子灵里的光景，在主慈悲地为我们预备收割的田地里，撒下几粒种子。再见了，姐妹，再见。我忘不了，礼拜天，讲完道就来。”

辛普森牧师先生依着诺言，开始对弗雷德下功夫，结果却把他逼进了一种顽强的抵触状态，末了还是普赖姆小姐的坚韧把它磨平了。奋兴会的时候一到——它必定要来，像死一样确凿——弗雷德便照例走上哀伤者的长凳，哀痛了几天，把自己折腾到了该有的火候，随后便“信了主”——诚然，在他那位焦灼的监护人看来，信得未免冷淡了些。

这之后辛普森先生再来拜访普赖姆小姐，趁机说道：“啊，我的姐妹，你把以色列家那只迷羊指给我，我真高兴；我天天感谢造物主，祂赐福我的劳苦，把那走迷的领回了羊圈。啊，一只失而复得的羊，比九十九只没有走迷的，喜乐更大！”

辛普森先生这位教友口里应承，心里却犯嘀咕：主赐福的究竟是谁的劳苦呢。她觉得自己有几分自私。凡是落到弗雷德头上的好处，她都想做始作俑者。但她没有同辛普森先生争执。对自己的牧师，有些让步是免不了的。

===Original===
From this time on the preacher was Fred’s friend, and plied him with good advice in the usual friendly way; but the boy bore it well, for Elizabeth smiled on him, and what boy would not bear a father’s tongue for a girl’s eyes?

The girl was like her mother, dark and slender and gentle. She had none of her father’s bigness or bumptiousness. Her eyes were large and of a shade that was neither black nor brown. Her hair was very decidedly black. Her face was small, and round with the plumpness of youth, but one instinctively felt, in looking at it, that its lines might easily fall into thinness, even pitifulness, at the first touch of woman’s sorrow. She was not, nor did she look to be, a strong girl. But her very weakness was the source of secret delight to the boy, for it made him feel her dependence on him. When they were together and some girlish fear made her cling to his arm, his heart swelled with pride and a something else that he could not understand and could not have described. Had anyone told him that he was going through the half-sweet, half-painful, timid, but gallant first stages of love, he would have resented the imputation with blushes. His whole training would have made him think of such a thing with terror. He had learned never to speak of girls at home, for any reference to them by him was sure to bring forth from Miss Prime an instant and strong rebuke.

“Freddie,” was the exclamation that gave his first unsuspecting remarks pause, “you’re a-gittin’ too fresh: you’d better be a-mindin’ of yore studies, instead o’ thinkin’ about girls. Girls ain’t a-goin’ to make you pass yore examination, an’, besides, you’re a-gettin’ mannish; fur boys o’ yore age to be a-talkin’ about girls is mannish, do you hear, sir? You’re a-beginnin’ to feel yore keepin’ too strong. Don’t let me hear no more sich talk out o’ you.”

There never was a manly boy in the world whom the word “mannish,” when applied to him, did not crush. It is a horrid word, nasty and full of ugly import. Fred was subdued by it, and so kept silence about his female friends. Happy is the boy who dares at home to pour out his heart about the girls he knows and likes, and thrice unhappy he who through mistaken zeal on the part of misguided parents is compelled to keep his thoughts in his heart and brood upon his little aproned companions as upon a secret sin. Two things are thereby engendered, stealth and unhealth. If Fred escaped certain youthful pitfalls, it was because he was so repressed that he had learned to hide himself from himself, his thoughts from the mind that produced them.

===Chinese===
从这时起，传道人成了弗雷德的朋友，照友谊的常例对他谆谆劝导；可这孩子受得住，因为伊丽莎白对他微笑。为了一个姑娘的眼波，哪个男孩受不了长辈的絮叨呢？

这姑娘像她的母亲，肤色深，身量纤细，性子温柔。她一点也没有她父亲的那份粗豪与自负。她的眼睛很大，颜色说黑不黑、说棕不棕。头发却黑得斩钉截铁。她的脸小小的，因青春的丰润而圆，可谁看着它都会本能地觉得，女人的悲苦头一碰，那线条便很容易陷下去，瘦削下去，甚至可怜下去。她不是个强健的姑娘，看上去也不像。可正是她的柔弱，成了男孩暗中的欢喜，因为这让他感到她少不了他。他们在一起时，哪一点女孩家的惊怕叫她偎紧他的手臂，他的心便涨满了骄傲，还有一样他说不清、也讲不出的东西。倘若有人告诉他，他正在经历爱情的最初阶段——半甜半苦、怯生生而又带着骑士风度的最初阶段——他准会红着脸动怒，骂人冤枉他。他所受的全部教养，都会叫他怀着恐怖去想这样的事。他学会了在家里从不提起女孩，因为只要他一沾这个话头，普赖姆小姐必定当场一顿痛斥。

“弗雷迪，”——他起初那些没心没肺的话头，就是这样被喝断的——“你放肆得没边儿了：你还是多操心操心功课，少惦记什么姑娘。姑娘又不能帮你考及格；再说，你学着摆大人谱了；你这个年纪的男孩家，开口闭口谈姑娘，就是摆大人谱，听见没有，先生？你开始觉得自己了不得了。别再叫我听见你讲这种话。”

天底下从来没有一个有男子气的男孩，被“摆大人谱”这个词说不垮的。这是个可憎的词，恶毒，包藏着种种难听的暗示。弗雷德被它制服了，从此绝口不提自己的女友。哪个男孩敢在家里畅谈他认识、他喜欢的姑娘，他是幸福的；哪个男孩被引入歧途的父母的一片错爱逼得把心事闷在肚里、把围裙下的小伙伴当作一桩隐秘的罪来琢磨，他就三倍地不幸。这样便养成两样东西：鬼祟，和不健康。弗雷德之所以躲开了某些青春的陷阱，正因为他被压制得太狠，学会了对自己也藏起自己，把念头从生出它们的头脑里藏开。

===Original===
He was a boy strong and full of blood. The very discipline that had given a gloomy cast to his mind had given strength and fortitude to his body. He was austere, because austerity was all that he had ever known or had a chance of knowing; but too often austerity is but the dam that holds back the flood of potential passion. Not to know the power which rages behind the barricade is to leave the structure weak for a hapless day when, carrying all before it, the flood shall break its bonds and in its fury ruin fair field and smiling mead. It was well for Fred Brent that the awakening came when it did.

In the first days of June, when examinations are over, the annual exhibition done, and the graduating class has marched away proud in the possession of its diplomas, the minds of all concerned turn naturally towards the old institution, the school picnic. On this occasion parents join the teachers and pupils for a summer day’s outing in the woods. Great are the preparations for the festal day, and great the rejoicings thereon. For these few brief hours old men and women lay aside their cares and their dignity and become boys and girls again. Those who have known sorrow﻿—and who has not?﻿—take to themselves a day of forgetfulness. Great baskets are loaded to overflowing with the viands dear to the picnicker’s palate﻿—sandwiches whose corpulence would make their sickly brothers of the railway restaurant wither with envy, pies and pickles, cheese and crackers, cakes and jams galore. Old horses that, save for this day, know only the market-cart or the Sunday chaise, are hitched up to bear out the merry loads. Old wagons, whose wheels have known no other decoration than the mud and clay of rutty roads, are festooned gaily with cedar wreaths, oak leaves, or the gaudy tissue-paper rosettes, and creak joyfully on their mission of lightness and mirth. On foot, by horse, in wagon or cart, the crowds seek some neighbouring grove, and there the day is given over to laughter, mirth, and song. The children roll and tumble on the sward in the intoxication of “swing-turn” and “ring-around-a-rosy.” The young women, with many blushes and shy glances, steal off to quiet nooks with their imploring swains. Some of the elders, anxious to prove that they have not yet lost all their youth and agility, indulge, rather awkwardly perhaps, in the exhausting amusement of the jumping-rope. A few of the more staid walk apart in conversation with some favourite pastor who does not decline to take part in the innocent pleasures and crack ponderous jokes for the edification of his followers. Perhaps some of the more daring are engaged in one of the numerous singing plays, such as “Oh, la, Miss Brown,” or “Swing Candy, Two and Two,” but these are generally frowned upon: they are too much like dancing, and time has been when some too adventurous church-member has been “churched” for engaging in one.

In such a merrymaking was the community which surrounded the high school at Dexter engaged when the incident occurred which opened Fred’s eyes to his own state. Both he and Elizabeth had been in the prize ranks that year, and their friends had turned out in full and made much of them. Even Eliphalet Hodges was there, with old Bess festooned as gaily as the other horses, and both Miss Prime and Mr. Simpson were in evidence. The afternoon of the day was somewhat advanced, the dinner had been long over, and the weariness of the people had cast something of a quietus over the hilarity of their sports. They were sitting about in groups, chatting and laughing, while the tireless children were scurrying about in games of “tag,” “catcher,” and “hide-and-seek.”

===Chinese===
他是个血气旺盛的结实男孩。那段把他的心绪染上阴郁的管教，反倒给了他的体魄以力量和坚忍。他严峻，因为严峻是他所知的一切、也是他有缘得知的一切；可严峻往往不过是一道堤坝，拦着潜在的激情的洪流。不知道坝后翻腾着怎样的力量，这道堤坝便在某个倒霉的日子里显得脆弱——那一天，洪流挟着雷霆万钧冲开束缚，在狂怒中毁掉良田和含笑的草场。弗雷德·布伦特算是万幸，觉醒来得正是时候。

六月初，考试已毕，一年一度的游艺会也散了场，毕业班捧着文凭昂首走远，于是有关人等的心思，便自然而然转向那古老的传统——学校野餐会。到了这一天，家长们同师生一起，到林子里消磨一个夏日。这个节日的大阵仗，事前的筹备隆重得很，事后的欢庆也隆重得很。就这短短几个钟头，老汉和老妪撂下操劳与体面，重新做一回男孩女孩。那些尝过悲苦的人——谁又没尝过呢？——给自己偷来一个忘忧的日子。一只只大筐装得冒了尖，全是野餐人舌尖上的至爱——三明治，那肥壮劲儿足以叫车站餐馆里它那些病恹恹的同类羞愧至死；馅饼和泡菜，干酪和饼干，蛋糕和果酱，应有尽有。那些除了这一天只认得菜市板车和礼拜马车的老马，都套上了车，载走欢欢乐乐的一车车人。那些车轮除了坎坷路上的泥浆从未见过别的装点的旧货车，也喜气洋洋地挂上雪松枝编的花环、橡树叶，或是艳丽的皱纸花，在轻松和欢笑的差事上吱吱嘎嘎地前行。步行的、骑马的、坐货车的、坐板车的，人流涌向邻近的哪片树丛；到了那里，这一整天便交给了欢笑、嬉闹和歌唱。孩子们在草地上打滚撒欢，沉醉在“转秋千”和“绕玫瑰圈”里。年轻姑娘们红着脸、偷眼四顾，被恳求的小伙子们领到僻静的角落。几位长辈急于证明自己青春与身手犹在，多少有些笨拙地玩起跳绳这项累人的消遣。还有几位更持重的，陪着哪位不辞分享纯真乐趣的得意牧师在一旁散步，讲些沉甸甸的笑话来教化跟随的人。兴许还有几个胆子更大的，正玩着那许多歌唱游戏里的某一种，比如《噢啦，布朗小姐》，或者《摇糖果，二人一对》；不过这些通常要遭人皱眉：太像跳舞了，从前就有过胆子太大的教友，因为玩了这类游戏而受了“教会惩戒”。

出了那桩让弗雷德看清自己处境的事故时，德克斯特中学四周的社群正沉浸在这样的欢庆里。那年他和伊丽莎白都名列得奖的一档，朋友们倾巢出动，对他们大为捧场。连埃利法莱特·霍奇斯也来了，老贝斯跟别的马一样披红挂彩，普赖姆小姐和辛普森先生也都到场。那天的午后已深，午饭过去多时，人们的倦意在嬉戏的热闹上罩了一层沉静。他们成群地坐着，谈着笑着，不知疲倦的孩子们还在四处乱窜，玩“捉人”、“追捕”和“捉迷藏”。

===Original===
The grove where the festivities were being held was on a hillside which sloped gently to the bank of a small, narrow stream, usually dry in summer; but now, still feeling the force of the spring freshets, and swollen by the rain of the day before, it was rushing along at a rapid rate. A fence divided the picnic-ground proper from the sharper slope of the rivulet’s bank. This fence the young people had been warned not to pass, and so no danger was apprehended on account of the stream’s overflowing condition. But the youngsters at Dexter were no more obedient than others of their age elsewhere. So when a scream arose from several childish voices at the lower part of the hill, everybody knew that some child had been disobeying, and, pell-mell, the picnickers rushed in the direction of the branch.

When they reached the nearest point from which they could see the stream, a terrifying sight met their eyes. A girl was struggling in the shallow but swift water. She had evidently stepped on the sloping bank and fallen in. Her young companions were running alongside the rivulet, stretching out their hands helplessly to her, but the current was too strong, and, try as she would, she could not keep her feet. A cry of grief and despair went up from the girls on the bank, as she made one final effort and then fell and was carried down by the current.

Men were leaping the fence now, but a boy who had seen the whole thing from a neighbouring hillock was before them. Fred Brent came leaping down the hill like a young gazelle. He had seen who the unfortunate girl was﻿—Elizabeth﻿—and he had but one desire in his heart, to save her. He reached the bank twenty yards ahead of anyone else, and plunged into the water just in front of her, for she was catching and slipping, clinging and losing hold, but floating surely to her death. He struggled up stream, reached and caught her by the dress. The water tugged at him and tried to throw him over, but he stemmed it, and, lifting her up in his arms, fought his way manfully to the bank. Up this he faltered, slipping and sliding in the wet clay, and weak with his struggle against the strong current. But his face was burning and his blood tingling as he held the girl close to him till he gave her unconscious form into her father’s arms.

===Chinese===
举办欢庆的树丛在一面山坡上，山坡缓缓斜向一条又小又窄的溪岸；这溪夏天通常干涸，此刻还带着春汛的余威，又被前一天的大雨灌涨，正湍急地奔流。一道栅栏把野餐场和溪岸较陡的坡隔开。年轻人早已被叮嘱不得越过这道栅栏，所以溪水暴涨并没有让人担心出什么乱子。可德克斯特的孩子们，并不比别处同龄的孩子更听话。于是，当山下几声童音尖叫起来的时候，人人都知道准是哪个孩子违了禁，野餐的人们一窝蜂朝那条支溪涌去。

他们赶到能望见溪水的最近一处，一幅吓人的景象撞进眼里。一个姑娘正在又浅又急的水里挣扎。她显然是踩上了倾斜的岸坡，跌进了水里。她的小同伴们沿着溪岸奔跑，朝她徒劳地伸着手；可水势太急，她怎么挣也站不住脚。她作了最后一下努力，随即跌倒，被水流卷了下去，岸上的姑娘们发出一声悲伤绝望的惊呼。

男人们纷纷翻过栅栏，可一个从旁边小丘上望见了全过程的男孩抢在了他们前头。弗雷德·布伦特像一头小羚羊，蹦跳着冲下山坡。他看清了那遭难的姑娘是谁——伊丽莎白——他心里只剩一个念头：救她。他比谁都先赶到岸边二十码，抢到她前方纵身入水；她在水里又抓又滑，攀住又脱手，却分明正漂向死地。他逆流挣扎，够着了，一把抓住她的衣裳。水拖拽他，要把他掀翻，他顶住了，把她抱进怀里，堂堂男子般一路搏到岸边。上岸时他脚步踉跄，在湿泥里又滑又溜，同急流搏斗得浑身发软。可他的脸滚烫，血在血管里嗡嗡作响，他把姑娘紧紧搂在怀里，直到把她不省人事的身躯交到她父亲的手臂中。

===Original===
For the moment all was confusion, as was natural when a preacher’s daughter was so nearly drowned. The crowd clustered around and gave much advice and some restoratives. Some unregenerate, with many apologies and explanations concerning his possession, produced a flask, and part of the whisky was forced down the girl’s throat, while her hands and face and feet were chafed. She opened her eyes at last, and a fervent “Thank God!” burst from her father’s lips and called forth a shower of Amens.

“I allus carry a little somethin’ along, in case of emergencies,” explained the owner of the flask as he returned it to his pocket, with a not altogether happy look at its depleted contents.

As soon as Fred saw that Elizabeth was safe, he struck away for home, unobserved, and without waiting to hear what the crowd were saying. He heard people calling his name kindly and admiringly, but it only gave wings to the feet that took him away from them. If he had thrown the girl in instead of bringing her out, he could not have fled more swiftly or determinedly away from the eyes of people. Tired and footsore, drenched to the skin and chilled through, he finally reached home. He was trembling, he was crying, but he did not know it, and had he known, he could not have told why. He did not change his clothes, but crouched down in a corner and hid his face in his hands. He dreaded seeing anyone or hearing any person speak his name. He felt painfully conscious of a new self, which he thought must be apparent to other eyes.

The accident of the afternoon had cast a gloom over the merrymakings, and, the picnic breaking up abruptly, sent the people scurrying home, so that Miss Prime was at the house not far behind her charge.

===Chinese===
一时间人仰马翻——牧师的女儿险些淹死，场面自然如此。人群围拢来，七嘴八舌地出主意，也上了些救急的法子。有个未重生的，连声赔着不是、解释着自己怎么会有这物件，掏出一小瓶酒来，给女孩灌下几口威士忌，一边揉搓着她的手、脸和脚。她终于睁开了眼睛，她父亲唇间迸出一声滚烫的“感谢上帝”，引来了阵雨般的“阿门”。

“我随身总带点儿应急的东西。”酒瓶的主人一边把它揣回口袋，一边解释道，望着瓶中所剩无几的东西，神色不无尴尬。

弗雷德一见伊丽莎白脱了险，便悄悄拔腿往家走，也不等着听人群说些什么。他听见人们又亲热又赞叹地喊他的名字，可那只会给带他离开的双脚插上翅膀。哪怕他是把那姑娘推下水去的，而不是救上来的，他逃开众人的目光，也不过逃得这样快、这样决绝罢了。他又累又脚疼，浑身湿透，凉透了心，终于到了家。他在发抖，他在哭，可他自己并不知道；便是知道，他也说不出为什么。他没有换衣裳，缩在屋角，把脸埋进手心。他怕见任何人，怕听任何人提他的名字。他痛切地意识到一个新的自我，还以为旁人的眼睛一定也看得出来。

下午的意外给欢庆投下了阴影，野餐会草草收场，人们各自赶回家去，所以普赖姆小姐回到家里，并没落下她的被监护人多远。

===Original===
“Freddie,” she called to him as she entered the house, “Freddie, where air you?” And then she found him. She led him out of the corner and looked him over with a scrutinising eye. “Freddie Brent,” she said solemnly, “you’ve jest ruined yore suit.” He was glad. He wanted to be scolded. “But,” she went on, “I don’t care ef you have.” And here she broke down. “You’re a-goin’ to have another one, fur you’re a right smart boy, that’s all I’ve got to say.” For a moment he wanted to lay his head on her breast and give vent to the sob which was choking him. But he had been taught neither tenderness nor confidence, so he choked back the sob, though his throat felt dry and hot and strained. He stood silent and embarrassed until Miss Prime recovered herself and continued: “But la, child, you’ll take yore death o’ cold. Git out o’ them wet things an’ git into bed, while I make you some hot tea. Fur the life o’ me, I never did see sich carryin’s-on.”

The boy was not sorry to obey. He was glad to be alone. He drank the warm tea and tried to go to sleep, but he could not. His mind was on fire. His heart seemed as if it would burst from his bosom. Something new had come to him. He began to understand, and blushed because he did understand. It was less discovery than revelation. His forehead was hot. His temples were throbbing. It was well that Miss Prime did not discover it: she would have given him horehound to cure﻿—thought!

From the moment that the boy held the form of the girl to his heart he was changed, and she was changed to him. They could never be the same to each other again. Manhood had come to him in a single instant, and he saw in her womanhood. He began for the first time to really know himself, and it frightened him and made him ashamed.

He drew the covers over his head and lay awake, startled, surprised at what he knew himself and mankind to be.

To Fred Brent the awakening had come﻿—early, if we would be prudish; not too early, if we would be truthful.

===Chinese===
“弗雷迪，”她一进屋便喊他，“弗雷迪，你在哪儿？”随即她找到了他。她把他从屋角领出来，用挑剔的眼光上下打量。“弗雷迪·布伦特，”她郑重地说，“你这身衣裳算是毁透了。”他反倒高兴。他就想挨一顿骂。“不过，”她接着说，“毁了就毁了吧。”说到这里她撑不住了。“你会有新的一身，因为你是个顶顶聪明的孩子，我要说的就这一句。”有一瞬间，他真想把头埋进她怀里，把哽在喉头的那声呜咽放出来。可他既没学过温柔，也没学过倾诉，便把那声呜咽咽了回去，只觉得喉咙又干又烫又紧。他窘迫地默默站着，直到普赖姆小姐缓过劲来，接着说：“嗐，孩子，你要冻出病来的。快把湿衣裳换了，上床躺着，我给你烧点热茶。打死我也没见过这样的胡闹。”

男孩巴不得从命。他乐意一个人待着。他喝了热茶，想睡，却睡不着。他的脑子烧着。他的心仿佛要从胸膛里迸出来。一样崭新的东西落到他身上。他开始懂了，又因为懂了而脸红。那与其说是发现，不如说是启示。他的额头滚烫。太阳穴突突地跳。幸好普赖姆小姐没有察觉；不然，她准会给他一支苦薄荷糖，来治——思想！

从他把姑娘的身躯搂在心头的那一刻起，他变了，她在他眼里也变了。他们彼此再不可能是从前的样子了。男子气概在一瞬间降临到他身上，而他在她身上看见了女人。他头一回真正认识了自己，这叫他害怕，也叫他害臊。

他把被子拉过头顶，睁着眼躺在那里，为他所知道的自己、为人类本来的样子，又惊又愕。

对弗雷德·布伦特来说，觉醒到来了——若要装正经，可谓早了些；若要说实话，并不算早。

## VIII / 八

===Original===
If Fred Brent had needed anything to increase his consciousness of the new feeling that had come to him, he could not have done better to get it than by going to see Eliphalet Hodges next day. His war of thought had gone on all night, and when he rose in the morning he thought that he looked guilty, and he was afraid that Miss Prime would notice it and read his secret. He wanted rest. He wanted to be secure from anyone who would even suspect what was in his heart. But he wanted to see and to talk to someone. Who better, then, than his old friend?

So he finished his morning’s chores and slipped away. He would not pass by Elizabeth’s house, but went by alleys and lanes until he reached his destination. The house looked rather silent and deserted, and Mr. Hodges’ old assistant did not seem to be working in the garden as usual. But after some search the boy found his old friend smoking upon the back porch. There was a cloud upon the usually bright features, and the old man took his pipe from his mouth with a disconsolate sigh as the boy came in sight.

“I’m mighty glad you’ve come, Freddie,” said he, in a sad voice. “I’ve been a-wantin’ to talk to you all the mornin’. Set down on the side o’ the porch, or git a chair out o’ the house, ef you’d ruther.”

The boy sat down, wondering what could be the matter with his friend, and what he could have to say to him. Surely it must be something serious, for the whole tone and manner of his companion indicated something of import. The next remark startled him into sudden suspicion.

“There’s lots o’ things made me think o’ lots of other things in the last couple o’ days. You’ve grown up kind o’ quick like, Freddie, so that a body ain’t hardly noticed it, but that ain’t no matter. You’re up or purty nigh it, an’ you can understand and appreciate lots o’ the things that you used to couldn’t.”

===Chinese===
倘若弗雷德·布伦特还需要什么来加深他对这份新生情感的自觉，那么第二天去看埃利法莱特·霍奇斯，便是再好不过的办法了。他内心的思想斗争持续了一整夜，早晨起身时，他觉得自己一脸心虚，生怕普赖姆小姐看了出来，窥破他的秘密。他想要安宁，想躲开任何一个哪怕只是疑心他心底之事的人。可他又想见见什么人，跟人说说话。那么，还有谁比他的老朋友更合适呢？

于是他做完早晨的活计，便悄悄溜了出去。他不愿打伊丽莎白家门前经过，专拣小巷僻径走，一路走到目的地。那座房子看上去相当冷清寂寥，霍奇斯先生那位老帮工似乎也不像往常那样在园子里干活。不过寻了一阵之后，男孩还是在后廊上找到了他的老朋友，正在抽烟。那张平日爽朗的脸上罩着一层阴云；男孩刚一露面，老人便一声怅然长叹，把烟斗从嘴里拿了下来。

“你来了我真高兴，弗雷迪，”他声音悲戚地说，“我一早上都想找你说说话呢。就在廊子边上坐吧，要不进屋搬把椅子出来，随你的便。”

男孩坐下了，心里纳闷朋友到底出了什么事，又有什么话要对自己讲。想必一定是桩要紧事，因为对方整个的语气神态都透着郑重其事的意味。接下来那句话却叫他吃了一惊，陡然生起疑心。

“这几天啊，好多事让我想起了好多别的事。你长大得有点儿快，弗雷迪，快得人家几乎没留意到——不过这不要紧。你如今长大了，或者说差不多了，从前不懂的许多事情，你现在都能明白、都能领会了。”

===Original===
Fred sat still, with mystery and embarrassment written on his face. He wanted to hear more, but he was almost afraid to listen further.

“I ain’t watched you so close, mebbe, as I’d ought to ’a’ done, but when I seen you yistiddy evenin’ holdin’ that little girl in yore arms I said to myself, I said, ‘Liphalet Hodges, Freddie ain’t a child no more; he’s growed up.’ ” The boy’s face was scarlet. Now he was sure that the thoughts of his heart had been surprised, and that this best of friends thought of him as “fresh,” “mannish,” or even wicked. He could not bear the thought of it; again the tears rose in his eyes, usually so free from such evidences of weakness. But the old man went on slowly in a low, half-reminiscent tone, without looking at his auditor to see what effect his words had had. “Well, that was one of the things that set me thinkin’; an’ then there was another.” He cleared his throat and pulled hard at his pipe; something made him blink﻿—dust, or smoke, or tears, perhaps. “Freddie,” he half sobbed out, “old Bess is dead. Pore old Bess died last night o’ colic. I’m afeared the drive to the picnic was too much fur her.”

“Old Bess dead!” cried the boy, grieved and at the same time relieved. “Who would have thought it? Poor old girl! It seems like losing one of the family.”

“She was one of the family,” said the old man brokenly. “She was more faithful than most human beings.” The two stood sadly musing, the boy as sad as the man. “Old Bess” was the horse that had taken him for his first ride, that winter morning years before, when the heart of the child was as cold as the day. Eliphalet Hodges had warmed the little heart, and, in the years that followed, man, child, and horse had grown nearer to each other in a queer but sympathetic companionship.

===Chinese===
弗雷德一动不动地坐着，脸上写满了惶惑与窘迫。他想再听下去，却又几乎不敢再听。

“兴许我照看你照看得不够尽心，本该看得更紧些才是；可昨天傍晚，我瞧见你把那小姑娘搂在怀里，我就对自己说，我说：‘埃利法莱特·霍奇斯呀，弗雷迪不再是孩子了；他长大了。’”男孩的脸涨得通红。这下他确信自己心底的念头已经被窥破了，这位再好不过的朋友必定把他看成了“轻浮”、“冒充大人”，甚至邪恶。他忍受不了这个念头；泪水又一次涌上他的眼睛——那双眼睛素来是不大显出这种软弱迹象的。老人却不看听者，也不管自己的话起了什么作用，只管用低低的、半带追忆的调子缓缓说下去。“唔，这是让我想心事的一桩；还有一桩呢。”他清了清嗓子，狠狠吸了一口烟斗；有什么东西呛得他直眨眼——也许是灰尘，也许是烟，也许是眼泪。“弗雷迪，”他几乎是哽咽着说，“老贝斯死了。可怜的老贝斯昨儿夜里得急腹痛死了。我怕是去野餐那一趟，把它累坏啦。”

“老贝斯死了！”男孩叫道，心里悲伤，同时又松了一口气。“谁能想得到呢？可怜的老伙计！这就像家里少了一个人似的。”

“它就是家里的一口人哪。”老人断断续续地说。“它比大多数人都忠心。”两人伫立着黯然出神，男孩和老人一样伤心。“老贝斯”就是多年前那个冬天的早晨头一回载他出行的马——那时节，孩子的心和那天气一样冷。是埃利法莱特·霍奇斯温暖了那颗小小的心；此后经年，人、孩子和马，就在一种古怪却情意相通的陪伴里，越处越近。

===Original===
Then, as if recalling his mind from painful reflections, the elder man spoke again. “But it ain’t no use a-worryin’ over what can’t be helped. We was both fond o’ old Bess, an’ I know you feel as bad about losin’ her as I do. But I’m a-goin’ to give her a decent burial, sich as a Christian ought to have; fur, while the old mare wasn’t no perfessor, she lived the life, an’ that’s more ’n most perfessors do. Yes, sir, I’m a-goin’ to have her buried: no glue-man fur me. I reckon you’re a-wantin’ to know how old Bess dyin’ an’ yore a-savin’ ’Lizabeth could run into each other in my mind; but they did. Fur, as I see you standin’ there a-holdin’ the little girl, it come to me sudden like, ‘Freddie’s grown now, an’ he’ll be havin’ a girl of his own purty soon, ef he ain’t got one now. Mebbe it’ll be ’Lizabeth.’ ” The old man paused for a moment; his eyes rested on the boy’s fiery face. “Tut, tut,” he resumed, “you ain’t ashamed, air you? Well, what air you a-gittin’ so red fur? Havin’ a girl ain’t nothin’ to be ashamed of, or skeered about neither. Most people have girls one time or another, an’ I don’t know of nothin’ that’ll make a boy or a young man go straighter than to know that his girl’s eyes air upon him. Don’t be ashamed at all.”

Fred still blushed, but he felt better, and his face lightened over the kindly words.

“I didn’t finish tellin’ you, though, what I started on. I got to thinkin’ yesterday about my young days, when I had a girl, an’ how I used to ride back an’ forth on the pore old horse right into this town to see her; an’ as I drove home from the picnic I talked to the old nag about it, an’ she whisked her tail an’ laid back her ears, jest like she remembered it all. It was on old Bess that I rode away from my girl’s house after her first ‘no’ to me, an’ it seemed then that the animal sympathised with me, fur she drooped along an’ held down her head jest like I was a-doin’. Many a time after that we rode off that way together, fur the girl was set in her ways, an’ though she confessed to a hankerin’ fur me, she wanted to be independent. I think her father put the idee into her head, fur he was a hard man, an’ she was his all, his wife bein’ dead. After a while we stopped talkin’ about the matter, an’ I jest went an’ come as a friend. I only popped the question once more, an’ that was when her father died an’ she was left all alone.

===Chinese===
随后，年长的一位仿佛从沉痛的思绪里回过神来，又开口了。“不过，对无可挽回的事发愁也没有用处。咱们俩都疼老贝斯，我知道你丢了它跟我一样难受。可我要给它办一场体面的安葬，像基督徒所应得的那样；因为啊，这匹老马虽说从没口称信教，它过的却是教徒的一生，这比大多数口称信教的人都强。是的，先生，我一定要安葬它：我可不把它给收尸做胶的马贩子。我猜你想知道，老贝斯死了，跟你救伊丽莎白，这两件事在我心里怎么能扯到一块儿去；可它们就是扯到一块儿了。因为我瞧见你站在那儿搂着那小姑娘，心里忽然就冒出一个念头：‘弗雷迪如今长大了，很快就要有他自己的姑娘了，兴许眼下就已经有了。没准儿就是伊丽莎白。’”老人顿了片刻；目光落在男孩火辣辣的脸上。“啧，啧，”他又说下去，“你可不是不好意思了吧？嗨，脸红什么呢？有姑娘又不是什么丢人的事，也不是什么可怕的事。大多数人早晚都有姑娘。依我看，一个小子或者一个年轻人要想走正道，再没有比知道他姑娘的眼睛正望着他更管用的了。一点儿也别害臊。”

弗雷德还在红脸，但心里好受多了，这番厚道话让他的脸色开朗起来。

“不过，我起头要说的话还没说完呢。昨天我想起自己年轻的时候，那时我也有过一个姑娘，我常常骑着那匹可怜的老马，一趟趟赶进这座镇子去看她；野餐回来的一路上，我跟那匹老牲口念叨这些，它甩甩尾巴，把耳朵往后一伏，倒像全都记得似的。她头一回回绝我之后，我就是骑着老贝斯从她家走的；那时节这牲口仿佛也体恤我，一路蔫头耷脑、低着脑袋，跟我一个模样。打那以后，我们又多少次这样一同骑行离去，因为那姑娘性子有主见；她虽说亲口承认心里惦记着我，却偏要自立。我想是她父亲把那个念头灌进她脑袋的，那是个硬心肠的人，妻子死后，女儿便是他的全部。过了一阵，我们便不再提这件事，我只像个朋友似的来来去去。我只又开过一次口，那是在她父亲去世、她孤身一人的时候。

===Original===
“It was a summer day, warm an’ cheerful like this, only it was evenin’, an’ we was a-settin’ out on her front garden walk. She was a-knittin’, an’ I was a-whippin’ the groun’ with a switch that I had brought along to touch Bess up with now an’ then. I had hitched her out front, an’ she kep’ a-turnin’ her eyes over the fence as ef she was as anxious as I was, an’ that was mighty anxious. Fin’ly I got the question out, an’ the girl went all red in a minute: she had been jest a purty pink before. Her knittin’ fell in her lap. Fust she started to answer, then she stopped an’ her eyes filled up. I seen she was a-weak’nin’, so I thought I’d push the matter. ‘Come,’ says I, gentle like, an’ edgin’ near up to her, ‘give me my answer. I been waitin’ a long time fur a yes.’ With that she grabbed knittin’, apron, an’ all, an’ put ’em to her eyes an’ rushed into the house. I knowed she’d gone in to have a good cry an’ settle her nerves, fur that’s the way all womenfolks does: so I knowed it was no use to bother her until it was done. So I walks out to the fence, an’, throwin’ an arm over old Bess’s back, I told her all about it, jest as I’m a-tellin’ you, she a-lookin’ at me with her big meltin’ eyes an’ whinnyin’ soft like.

“After a little while the girl come out. She was herself ag’in, but there was a look in her face that turned my heart stone-cold. Her voice sounded kind o’ sharp as she said, ‘ ’Liphalet, I’ve been a-thinkin’ over what you said. I’m only a woman, an’ I come purty near bein’ a weak one; but I’m all right now. I don’t mind tellin’ you that ef I was ever goin’ to marry, you’d be my choice, but I ain’t a-goin’ to have my father’s sperrit a-thinkin’ that I took advantage of his death to marry you. Goodbye, ’Liphalet.’ She held out her hand to me, an’ I took it. ‘Come an’ see me sometimes,’ she said. I couldn’t answer, so I went out and got on old Bess an’ we jogged away. It was an awful disappointment, but I thought I would wait an’ let my girl come aroun’, fur sometimes they do﻿—in fact mostly; but she has never give me a sign to make me think that she has. That was twenty years ago, an’ I’ve been waitin’ faithful ever sence. But it seems like she was different from most women, an’ ’specially good on holdin’ out. People that was babies then have growed up an’ married. An’ now the old companion that has been with me through all this waitin’ has left me. I know what it means. It means that I’m old, that years have been wasted, that chances have been lost. But you have taught me my lesson, Bess. Dear old Bess, even in yore last hours you did me a service, an’ you, Freddie, you have given me the stren’th that I had twenty years ago, an’ I’m a-goin’ to try to save what remains of my life.
