I never felt how alone I was until now.” He was greatly agitated. He rose and grasped the boy’s arm. “Come, Freddie,” he said; “come on. I’m a-goin’ ag’in to ask Miss Prime to be my wife.”

“Miss Prime!” exclaimed Fred, aghast.

“Miss Prime was my sweetheart, Freddie, thirty years ago, jest like ’Lizabeth is yor’n now. Come along.”

The two set out, Hodges stepping with impatient alacrity, and the boy too astounded to speak.

===Chinese===
“那是个夏日，跟今天一样暖和明朗，只是那时候是傍晚，我们坐在她家前园的小径上。她织着毛线，我拿一根柳条抽打着地皮——那柳条原是带来时不时催一催贝斯的。我把它拴在前头，它一个劲儿隔着栅栏张望，好像跟我一样心急，而且是心急得很呢。末了我终于把那句话问出了口，姑娘的脸一下子红透了：先前她只是透着一层好看的粉红。毛线活儿滑落在她膝上。她先是想答话，随即又停住，眼睛湿了。我看出她心软了，便想索性把事促成。‘来吧，’我柔声说着，一面朝她身边凑近，‘给我个答复吧。为了一句肯，我已经等了好久啦。’话音刚落，她一把抓起毛线活儿，连围裙一齐捂在眼睛上，冲进屋里去了。我知道她是进去痛痛快快哭一场，好定下神来，女人家都是这个样子的：所以我明白，在她哭完之前去打扰她是没用的。于是我踱到栅栏边，把一条胳膊搭在老贝斯背上，把这一切都对它讲了，就跟我现在讲给你听一样；它呢，睁着一双温存的大眼望着我，轻轻地打着响鼻。”

“过了一小会儿，姑娘出来了。她已恢复了常态，可她脸上有一种神色，把我的心弄得冰凉。她开口时嗓音微微有些尖利：‘埃利法莱特，我把你说的话仔细想过了。我不过是个女人，还差一点儿就成了个软弱的女人；可我如今没事了。我不妨告诉你，倘若我这辈子打算嫁人，你便是我的意中人；可我不能让我父亲的在天之灵以为，我是趁着他故去才嫁给你的。再见，埃利法莱特。’她向我伸出手来，我握住了。‘有空常来看看我。’她说。我答不上话来，便走出去，跨上老贝斯，我们一路缓步而去。那是一场难堪的失望，可我想我还是等一等，让我的姑娘回心转意，因为有时候她们确实会回心转意——其实多半都会；可她从没给过我一丝表示，叫我以为她回心转意了。那是二十年前的事了，打那以后我一直忠心耿耿地等着。可她似乎跟大多数女人不一样，格外熬得住。当年还在襁褓里的人都长大成了亲。如今，陪我熬过这一切等待的老伙计又离开了我。我懂得这是什么意思。这就是说，我老了，年华虚掷了，机缘错失了。可是你给我上了这一课，贝斯。亲爱的老贝斯，连你临终的时辰还帮了我一把；还有你，弗雷迪，你把我二十年前的那股子力气又还给了我，我要试着挽救我余下的岁月。直到此刻，我才感到自己多么孤单。”他激动万分，站起身来，一把抓住男孩的胳膊。“来，弗雷迪，”他说，“走吧。我要再去求普赖姆小姐做我的妻子。”

“普赖姆小姐！”弗雷德叫出声来，惊得目瞪口呆。

“三十年前，普赖姆小姐就是我的心上人哪，弗雷迪，就跟你如今和伊丽莎白一个样。走吧。”

两人动身上路，霍奇斯迈着急切轻快的步子，男孩却惊得说不出话来。

===Original===
It was a beautiful morning at the end of June. The sense of spring’s reviving influence had not yet given way to the full languor and sensuousness of summer. The wind was soft and warm and fragrant. The air was full of the song of birds and the low droning of early bees. The river that flowed between the green hills and down through Dexter was like a pane of wrinkled glass, letting light and joy even into the regions below. Over the streets and meadows and hills lay a half haze, like a veil over the too dazzling beauty of an Eastern princess. The hum of business﻿—for in the passing years Dexter had grown busy﻿—the roar of traffic in the streets, all melted into a confused and intoxicating murmur as the pedestrians passed into the residence portion of the town to the cottage where Miss Prime still lived. The garden was as prim as ever, the walks as straight and well kept. The inevitable white curtains were fluttering freshly from the window, over which a huge matrimony vine drooped lazily and rung its pink and white bells to invite the passing bees.

Eliphalet paused at the gate and heaved a deep sigh. So much depended upon the issue of his present visit. The stream of his life had been flowing so smoothly before. Now if its tranquillity were disturbed it never could be stilled again. Did he dare to risk so much upon so hazardous a chance? Were it not better to go back home, back to his old habits and his old ease, without knowing his fate? That would at least leave him the pleasure of speculating. He might delude himself with the hope that some day﻿—He faltered. His hand was on the gate, but his face was turned back towards the way he had come. Should he enter, or should he go back? Fate decided for him, for at this juncture the door opened, and Miss Hester appeared in the doorway and called out, “Do come in, ’Liphalet. What air you a-standin’ out there so long a-studyin’ about, fur all the world like a bashful boy?”

The shot told. He was a bashful boy again, going fearfully, tremblingly, lovingly, to see the girl of his heart; but there was no old Bess to whinny encouragement to him from over the little fence. If he blushed, even the scrutinising eyes of Miss Prime did not see it, for the bronze laid on his face by summers and winters of exposure; but he felt the hot blood rush up to his face and neck, and the perspiration breaking out on his brow. He paused long enough to mop his face, and then, saying to Fred, in a low tone, “You stay in the garden, my boy, until it’s all over,” he opened the gate and entered in the manner of one who leads a forlorn hope through forest aisles where an ambush is suspected. The door closed behind him. Interested, excited, wondering and fearing, doubting and hoping, Fred remained in the garden. There were but two thoughts in his head, and they were so new and large that his poor boy’s cranium had room for no more. They ran in this wise: “Miss Prime is Uncle ’Liphalet’s girl, and Elizabeth is mine.”

Within, Miss Prime was talking on in her usual decided fashion, while the man sat upon the edge of his chair and wondered how he could break in upon the stream of her talk and say what was in his heart. At last the lady exclaimed, “I do declare, ’Liphalet, what kin be the matter with you? You ain’t said ten words sence you’ve been a-settin’ there. I hope you ain’t talked yoreself entirely out with Fred. It does beat all how you an’ that boy seem to grow thicker an’ thicker every day. One ’ud think fur all the world that you told him all yore secrets, an’ was afeared he’d tell ’em, by the way you stick by him; an’ he’s jest as bad about you. It’s amazin’.”

===Chinese===
那是六月底一个美丽的清晨。春回大地的生机之感，还没有让位于盛夏十足的慵懒与沉酣。风轻软和暖，带着芬芳。空气里满是鸟儿的歌唱和早起的蜜蜂低低的嗡鸣。那条流过青山之间、蜿蜒穿过德克斯特的河，宛如一面起了皱的玻璃，把光明与欢悦一直透进水底的世界。街市上、草场上、山峦上，都浮着一层薄薄的雾霭，像一袭面纱，罩住那位东方公主过于耀眼的美丽。市廛的喧声——因为随着岁月流转，德克斯特已经繁忙起来——街上车马的轰响，都融成一片纷杂而醉人的低语，随着行人走进镇上的住宅区，走向普赖姆小姐至今仍住着的那座小屋。园子还像从前一样端整，小径依旧笔直，修饰得一丝不苟。窗顶上一蓬高大的婚姻藤慵懒地低垂下来，摇响它粉的白的铃铛，邀请过往的蜜蜂；窗前那浆洗一新的白窗帘正微微飘拂。

埃利法莱特在园门口停下，深深叹了一口气。此行的成败，系着多少东西啊。他的生命之溪向来流得那样平顺；如今这份安宁一旦被搅乱，便再也无法复归平静了。他敢把这一切，押在这样一场毫无把握的冒险上吗？何不回到家里，回到旧日的习惯与旧日的安逸中去，对自己的命运一无所知呢？那样至少还给他留下揣度的乐趣。他或许可以拿希望哄骗自己，指望有朝一日——他迟疑了。他的手已扶上园门，脸却回望着来路。是进去，还是回头？命运替他做了决定：正在这当口，门开了，海斯特小姐出现在门内，扬声唤道：“快进来呀，埃利法莱特。你站在那儿琢磨什么呢，站了那么半天，活脱脱像个怕羞的毛头小子？”

这句话立见奇效。他又成了那个怕羞的男孩，怀着恐惧、颤抖和爱恋，去见心尖上的姑娘；只是再没有老贝斯隔着那道小栅栏，对他打响鼻鼓劲了。他纵然红了脸，连普赖姆小姐那双明察秋毫的眼睛也看不出来，因为经年累月的风霜早已给他脸上镀了一层古铜色；可他只觉得热血直往脸上、脖子上涌，额头上沁出了汗珠。他停了停，抹了一把脸，然后低声对弗雷德说：“你在园子里待着，我的孩子，等事情全办完了。”说罢推开园门，走了进去，那模样，恰似一员敢死之将，领着人马穿过疑有埋伏的林间甬道。门在他身后关上了。弗雷德留在园子里，又关切又激动，且惊且惧，且疑且盼。他脑子里只有两个念头，这两个念头又新鲜又庞大，把他那可怜的男孩脑壳塞得满满当当，再容不下别的了。它们是这样转的：“普赖姆小姐是埃利法莱特叔叔的姑娘，伊丽莎白是我的。”

屋子里，普赖姆小姐正照她素来那样斩钉截铁地讲个不停，那男人坐在椅子边上，盘算着怎样才能插进她的话流，说出心里的话。末了女士嚷道：“哎呀，埃利法莱特，你到底怎么了？打你坐在这儿，统共没说上十个字。我盼着你不是跟弗雷德把话都说尽了吧。真叫人想不通，你跟那孩子怎么好像一天比一天亲。瞧你成天护着他的那个劲儿，人家还当真以为你把什么秘密都告诉他了，还怕他给说出去呢；他对你也是一样地黏糊。真是怪事。”

===Original===
“Freddie’s a wonderful good boy, an’ he’s smart, too. They ain’t none of ’em a-goin’ to throw dust in his eyes in the race of life.”

“I’m shore I’ve tried to do my dooty by him the very best I could, an’ ef he does amount to anything in this world it’ll be through hard labour an’ mighty careful watchin’.” Miss Hester gave a sigh that was meant to be full of solemnity, but that positively reeked with self-satisfaction.

“But as you say, ’Liphalet,” she went on, “Fred ain’t the worst boy in the world, nor the dumbest neither, ef I do say it myself. I ain’t a-sayin’, mind you, that he’s anything so great or wonderful; but I’ve got to thinkin’ that there’s somethin’ in him besides original sin, an’ I should feel that the Lord had been mighty favourin’ to me ef I could manage to draw it out. The fact of it is, ’Liphalet, I’ve took a notion in my head about Fred, an’ I’m a-goin’ to tell you what it is. I’ve decided to make a preacher out o’ him.”

“H’m﻿—ah﻿—well, Miss Hester, don’t you think you’d better let the Lord do that?”

“Nonsense, ’Liphalet! you ain’t got no insight at all. I believe in people a-doin’ their part an’ not a-shovin’ everything off on the Lord. The shiftless don’t want nothin’ better than to say that they will leave the Lord to take care o’ things, an’ then fold their arms an’ set down an’ let things go to the devil. Remember, Brother Hodges, I don’t mean that in a perfane way. But then, because God made the sunlight an’ the rain, it ain’t no sign that we shouldn’t prune the vine.”

===Chinese===
“弗雷迪是个好得不得了的孩子，人也机灵。在人生的赛跑里，谁也别想蒙得住他的眼睛。”

“我敢说，我已经尽了最大的力，对他尽了我的本分；他日后在这世上若真能有点出息，那也是靠苦功夫加上十二分小心的看管。”海斯特小姐叹了一口气，本想叹得庄严肃穆，实则处处透着扬扬自得。

“不过就像你说的，埃利法莱特，”她接着讲，“弗雷德不是世上最坏的男孩，也不是最笨的——这话是我自己说的。你可别误会，我不是说他有多么了不起、多么不凡；可我渐渐琢磨出来，他身上除了原罪，总还有点别的什么，倘若我能想法子把它引出来，我便要觉得主待我实在恩厚了。实情是这样，埃利法莱特，我心里对弗雷德拿定了一个主意，我要告诉你是什么。我决定要把他造就成一个传道人。”

“呃——啊——是这么着，海斯特小姐，你就不觉得，这件事还是交给主来办的好？”

“胡说，埃利法莱特！你一点儿见识也没有。我的主张是，人该尽人的一份力，别把什么事都往主身上一推了事。那些没出息的，就巴不得说一句‘把事情交给主照管吧’，然后抱着胳膊往下一坐，任凭万事流到魔鬼那里去。记着，霍奇斯弟兄，我说这话可没有半点亵渎的意思。不过话说回来，上帝既然造了阳光和雨水，可不见得咱们就不该修剪葡萄藤。”

===Original===
Miss Hester’s face had flushed up with the animation of her talk, and her eyes were sparkling with excitement.

Eliphalet looked at her, and his heart leaped. He felt that the time had come to speak.

“Miss Hester,” he began, and the hat in his hand went round and round nervously.

“ ’Liphalet, fur goodness’ sake do lay yore hat on the table. You’ll ruin the band of it, an’ you make me as nervous as a cat.”

===Chinese===
海斯特小姐谈得兴起，脸上泛起红晕，眼睛里闪着兴奋的光。

埃利法莱特望着她，心猛地一跳。他觉得，开口的时机到了。

“海斯特小姐，”他开了口，手里的帽子紧张地转了一圈又一圈。

“埃利法莱特，看在老天的份上，快把帽子搁在桌上吧。你要把帽圈儿都拧坏了，还弄得我心里像猫抓似的。”

===Original===
He felt a little dampened after this, but he laid down the offending hat and began again. “I’ve been thinkin’ some myself, Miss Hester, an’ it’s been about you.”

“About me? La, ’Liphalet, what have you been a-thinkin’ now?” The “now” sounded as if his thoughts were usually rather irresponsible.

“It was about you an’﻿—an’﻿—old Bess.”

“About me an’ old Bess! Bless my soul, man, will you stop beatin’ about the bush an’ tell me what on airth I’ve got to do with yore horse?”

“Old Bess is dead, Miss Hester; died last night o’ colic.”

===Chinese===
这一下他有点泄气，但他还是把那顶惹祸的帽子放下，重新开了口。“我自己也琢磨了一些事，海斯特小姐，琢磨的是你的事。”

“我的事？哎呀，埃利法莱特，这会儿你又琢磨出什么来了？”那个“这会儿”字眼儿，听着仿佛他平日的念头向来不大可靠似的。

“是关于你跟——跟——老贝斯的。”

“我跟老贝斯！老天爷，你这个人，能不能别再拐弯抹角，痛痛快快告诉我，我究竟跟你那匹马有什么相干？”

“老贝斯死了，海斯特小姐；昨儿夜里得急腹痛死的。”

===Original===
“Well, I thought there was somethin’ the matter with you. I’m mighty sorry to hear about the poor old creatur; but she’d served you a long while.”

“That’s jest what set me a-thinkin’: she has served me a long while, an’ now she’s dead. Do you know what that means, Miss Hester? It means that we’re a-gittin’ old, you an’ me. Do you know when I got old Bess? It was nigh thirty years ago: I used to ride her up to this door an’ tie her to that tree out there: it was a saplin’ then. An’ now she’s dead.”

The man’s voice trembled, and his listener was strangely silent.

“You know on what errands the old horse used to bring me,” he went on, “but it wasn’t to be﻿—then. Hester,” he rose, went over to her, and looked down into her half-averted face, which went red and pale by turns﻿—“Hester, ain’t we wasted time enough?”

There was a long pause before she lifted her face: he stood watching her with the light of a great eagerness in his eyes. At last she spoke. There was a catch in her voice; it was softer than usual.

===Chinese===
“唔，我就寻思你准有什么心事。听说那可怜的老牲口死了，我心里很难过；不过它也伺候你这么多年了。”

“这正是让我琢磨的地方：它伺候了我这么久，如今死了。你知道这意味着什么吗，海斯特小姐？这意味着咱们都上年纪了，你和我。你知道我是什么时候得到老贝斯的吗？那是将近三十年前：我从前常常骑着它上这道门来，把它拴在外头那棵树上：那时候，那还是株小树苗呢。如今它死了。”

男人的声音颤抖了，听的人却出奇地沉默。

“你晓得这匹老马从前常常驮着我，来办的是什么样的差事，”他接着说，“可那时候——终究没有成。海斯特，”他站起身，走到她跟前，低头望着她那半偏半侧的脸——那张脸一阵红一阵白——“海斯特，咱们耽误的时光还不够多么？”

过了好一阵，她才抬起脸来：他站在那里望着她，眼里燃着殷切期待的光。她终于开口了。嗓音里带着一丝哽咽，比平日温柔。

===Original===
“ ’Liphalet,” she began, “I’m right glad you remember those days. I ain’t never furgot ’em myself. It’s true you’ve been a good, loyal friend to me, an’ I thank you fur it, but, after all these years﻿—”

He broke in upon her with something like youthful impetuosity. “After all these years,” he exclaimed, “an endurin’ love ought to be rewarded. Hester, I ain’t a-goin’ to take ‘no’ fur an answer. I’ve got lots o’ years o’ life in me yet﻿—we both have﻿—an’ I ain’t a-goin’ on with an empty home an’ an empty heart no longer.”

“ ’Liphalet, you ain’t a young man no more, an’ I ain’t a young woman, an’ the Lord﻿—”

“I don’t care ef I ain’t; an’ I don’t believe in shovin’ everything off on the Lord.”

“ ’Liphalet!” It was a reproach.

“Hester!” This was love. He put his arm around her and kissed her. “You’re a-goin’ to say yes, ain’t you? You ain’t a-goin’ to send me away miserable? You’re a-dyin’ to say yes, but you’re a-tryin’ to force yoreself not to. Don’t.” He lifted her face as a young lover might, and looked down into her eyes. “Is it yes?”

===Chinese===
“埃利法莱特，”她开口道，“你还记得那些日子，我真高兴。我自己也从没忘掉它们。你确实一直是我忠厚可靠的朋友，为这个我谢谢你；可是，隔了这么多年——”

他带着一股近乎年轻人的莽劲打断了她。“隔了这么多年，”他嚷道，“一份坚贞的爱情总该有个报偿。海斯特，这回我可不能拿‘不’字当答复了。我余下的年岁还多着呢——咱们俩都多着呢——空屋子配空心窝的日子，我再也过不下去了。”

“埃利法莱特，你已经不是年轻人了，我也不是年轻女人了，况且主——”

“老不老我不在乎；我也不信什么事儿都往主身上推的那一套。”

“埃利法莱特！”这是责备。

“海斯特！”这是爱情。他伸出胳膊搂住她，吻了她。“你是要答应的，对不对？你总不至于叫我凄凄惶惶地走吧？你恨不得一口就答应下来，只是硬逼着自己不松口。别憋着啦。”他像年轻的情人那样捧起她的脸，低头望着她的眼睛。“是不是一声‘好’？”

===Original===
“Well, ’Liphalet it ’pears like you’re jest so pesterin’ that I’ve got to say yes. Yes, then.” And she returned the quiet but jubilant kiss that he laid upon her lips.

“After all these years,” he said. “Sorrow may last fur a night, but joy cometh in the mornin’. It was a long night, but, thank the Lord, mornin’s broke.” Then, rising, he went to the door and called joyously, “Freddie, come on in: it’s all over.”

“ ’Liphalet, did that boy know what you was a-goin’ to say?”

“Yes, o’ course he did.”

“Oh, my! oh, my! Well, I’ve got a good mind to take it all back. Oh, my!” And when Fred came in, for the first time in her life Miss Prime was abashed and confused in his presence.

===Chinese===
“唉，埃利法莱特，瞧你这么死缠烂磨，我只好说好了。那么，好。”于是他往她唇上印下安静而狂喜的一吻，她也回了他一吻。

“隔了这么多年啊，”他说，“一宿虽然有哭泣，早晨便必欢呼。这一夜好长，可是感谢主，天到底亮了。”说罢他站起身，走到门口，欢欢喜喜地喊道：“弗雷迪，进来吧：全办完啦。”

“埃利法莱特，那孩子晓得你要说什么吗？”

“晓得，他当然晓得。”

“哎呀，我的天！哎呀，我的天！好吧，我真想统统收回来。哎呀，我的天！”弗雷德进来时，普赖姆小姐生平头一遭在他跟前红了脸、乱了方寸。

===Original===
But Eliphalet had no thought of shame. He took her by the hand and said, “Freddie, Miss Hester’s consented at last: after thirty years, she’s a-goin’ to marry me.”

But Miss Hester broke in, “ ’Liphalet, don’t be a-puttin’ notions in that boy’s head. You go ’way, Fred, right away.”

Fred went out, but he felt bolder. He went past Elizabeth’s house whistling. He didn’t care. He wondered if he would have to wait thirty years for her. He hoped not.

===Chinese===
埃利法莱特却全不觉得难为情。他拉起她的手，说：“弗雷迪，海斯特小姐总算答应啦：等了三十年，她要嫁给我了。”

海斯特小姐却插嘴道：“埃利法莱特，别往那孩子脑袋里灌念头。你去吧，弗雷德，快去。”

弗雷德走了出去，胆子却壮了起来。他吹着口哨走过伊丽莎白家的门前。他才不在乎呢。他寻思，自己是不是也得为她等上三十年。他可盼着不用。

## X / 十

===Original===
When Fate is fighting with all her might against a human soul, the greatest victory that the soul can win is to reconcile itself to the unpleasant, which is never quite so unpleasant afterwards. Upon this principle Frederick Brent acted instinctively. What with work and study and contact with his fellow-students, he found the seminary not so bad a place, after all. Indeed, he began to take a sort of pleasure in his pursuits. The spirit of healthy competition in the school whetted his mind and made him forgetful of many annoyances from without. When some fellow-salesman at the store gibed at him for being a parson, it hurt him; but the wound was healed and he was compensated when in debate he triumphed over the crack speaker of his class. It was a part of his training to do earnestly and thoroughly what he had to do, even though it was distasteful, and it was not long before he was spoken of as one of the most promising members of the school.

Notwithstanding its steady growth toward citydom, Dexter retained many of the traditions of its earlier and smaller days. Among them was that of making the church the centre of its social and public life. For this reason the young student came in for much attention on account of his standing in the religious college. Another cause which elicited the praise and congratulations of his friends was his extreme youth. That community which could send out a “boy preacher” always deemed itself particularly favoured by Providence. Dexter was no exception, and it had already begun to bestow the appellation upon young Brent, much to his disgust. He knew the species and detested it. It was mostly composed of ignorant and hypocritical young prigs, in whom their friends had seemed to see some especial merit and had forthwith hoisted them into a position that was as foolish as it was distasteful. They were hailed as youthful prodigies and exploited around the country like a patent medicine or a sideshow. What is remarkable at eighteen is not so striking at twenty-eight. So when their extreme youth was no longer a cause for surprise, the boy preachers settled down into everyday dullness, with nothing except the memory of a flimsy fame to compensate the congregations they bored.

Against this Frederick Brent fought with all his strength. He refused invitation after invitation to “talk” or “exhort,” on the plea that he wished to be fully prepared for his work before entering upon it.

===Chinese===
当命运倾尽全力与一个人的灵魂搏斗时，那灵魂所能赢得的最大胜利，便是与不如意之事和解——此后它们便再也不那么不如意了。弗雷德里克·布伦特正是凭本能依着这条原则行事的。由于有事做、有书读、又与同学朝夕相处，他发现神学校毕竟不是个那么糟糕的地方。的确，他开始从自己的功课里尝到一种乐趣。学校里健康的竞争风气磨砺了他的头脑，使他把许多外来的烦扰抛在了脑后。店里哪个售货员伙伴讥笑他要当牧师，他会难受；可一旦在辩论中压倒班上的头号演说家，那伤口便愈合了，还得了补偿。认真而彻底地做好他不得不做的事，哪怕心里不喜欢——这本是他所受教育的一部分；不久，他便被称作全校最有出息的学生之一了。

德克斯特虽朝着都市的模样稳步生长，却保留着它早先小城岁月的许多传统。其中之一，便是让教堂做社交与公众生活的中心。为此，这位年轻的学生因他在宗教学校的地位而颇受瞩目。招来朋友们称贺的另一重缘故，是他的年少。一个能推出“少年传道人”的地方，总自认为得了上天特别的眷顾。德克斯特也不例外，它已经开始把这个称号往年轻的布伦特身上安，这使他厌恶至极。他认得这一路货色，也鄙夷这一路货色。其中大多是无知而虚伪的年轻妄人，朋友们仿佛在他们身上看出了什么特别的才具，便立刻把他们抬举到一个既可笑又可厌的位置上。他们被当作少年奇才受到欢呼，像一味专利成药或一场杂耍余兴似的，被牵着四乡巡展。十八岁令人称奇的事，到了二十八岁便不那么惊人了。于是，等到年纪太轻再不足以让人诧异，那些少年传道人便安顿进日复一日的平庸里，只剩一段浮名的记忆，聊以补偿他们叫腻烦的会众。

弗雷德里克·布伦特竭尽全力抵抗这一切。人家一次次请他去“讲话”或“劝道”，他一次次推辞，理由是愿在上任之前把功课备得十足。

===Original===
But his success at school militated against him, for the fame of his oratorical powers was gradually but surely leaking out. The faculty recognised and commended it, so he could not hope long to hide behind his plea, although he dreaded the day when it would no longer serve his purpose.

Some of the “older heads” accused him of an unwarranted fear, of cowardice even, and an attempt to shirk his evident duty. The truth of it was that these same people wanted to hear him and then attack his manner or his doctrine. They could not, would not forget that he was the son of old Tom Brent, the drunkard, and of the terrible, the unspeakable Margaret, his wife. They could not forget that he was born and lived the first years of his life on the “mean” street, when it was a mean street; and when any obstinate old fossil was told of the youth’s promise, he would shake his head, as who should say, “What good can come out of that Nazareth?”

But the young man went his way and heeded them not. He knew what they were saying. He knew what they were thinking, even when they held his hand and smiled upon him, and it filled him with a spirit of distrust and resentment, though it put him bravely on his mettle. While he was a man, and in the main manly, sometimes he was roused to an anger almost childish; then, although he did not want to be a preacher at all, he wished and even prayed to become a great one, just to convince the old fools who shook their heads over him. To his ears had crept, as such tales will creep, some of the stories of his parents’ lives, and, while he pitied his mother, there was a great fierceness in his heart against his father.

But as in the old days when Miss Prime’s discipline would have turned all within him to hardness and bitterness Eliphalet Hodges stood between him and despair, so now in this crucial time Elizabeth was a softening influence in his life.

===Chinese===
可是他在学校的成绩偏偏与他作对：他演说才能的名声正在缓缓地、却确凿无疑地泄露出去。教员们认可它、表彰它，所以他休想长久躲在那个托词后面，尽管他惧怕那托词不再顶用的日子。

有些“老成持重”的人指摘他无端畏惧，甚至说他胆怯，说他存心规避明摆着的本分。实情却是，这帮人自己想听他讲，好随后攻击他的做派或他的教义。他们不能、也不肯忘记，他是醉汉老汤姆·布伦特的儿子，是他那个可怕的、说不出口的太太玛格丽特所生。他们不能忘记，他生在那条“下作”的街上，还在那里度过了最初几年的岁月——那时那条街还着实下作；每逢哪个顽固的老化石听人说起这后生的前程，他总要摇一摇头，仿佛在说：“拿撒勒还能出什么好的？”

但年轻人走自己的路，不理会他们。他知道他们在说什么。他知道他们心里想什么，哪怕在他们握着他的手、朝他微笑的时候，他也知道；这使他满心戒备与怨愤，却也勇敢地激起了他的好胜心。他虽是个男子汉，大体上也不失男子气概，有时却会被激起一股近乎孩子气的怒火；那时节，尽管他压根不想当传道人，他却盼望、甚至祷告自己能成为一位了不起的传道人，单为叫那些对他摇头的老糊涂心服口服。他父母生平的一些风言风语，也像这类话总会传开那样，爬进了他的耳朵；他怜悯他的母亲，心里对父亲却怀着大大的愤火。

从前，每当普赖姆小姐的管教要把他心里的一切都变成坚硬和苦涩时，总有埃利法莱特·霍奇斯挡在他与绝望之间；如今，在这紧要关头，伊丽莎白成了他生命里一股柔化的力量。

===Original===
As the days came and went, he had continued to go to see her ever since the night when he had stood with her at the gate and felt the bitterness of her lack of sympathy; but all that had passed now, and unconsciously they had grown nearer to each other. There had been a tacit understanding between them until just a few weeks before. It was on a warm spring evening: he had just passed through her gate and started towards the house, when the opening chords of the piano struck on his ear through the opened window and arrested him. Elizabeth had a pleasant little voice, with a good deal of natural pathos in it. As the minister’s daughter, the scope of her songs was properly, according to Dexter, rather limited, but that evening she was singing softly to herself a love-song. The words were these:

> If Death should claim me for her own today,
 

 And softly I should falter from your side,
 

 Oh, tell me, loved one, would my memory stay,
 

 And would my image in your heart abide?
 

 Or should I be as some forgotten dream,
 

 That lives its little space, then fades entire?
 

 Should Time send o’er you its relentless stream,
 

 To cool your heart, and quench for aye love’s fire?
> I would not for the world, love, give you pain,
 

 Or ever compass what would cause you grief;
 

 And oh, how well I know that tears are vain!
 

 But love is sweet, my dear, and life is brief;
 

 So, if some day before you I should go
 

 Beyond the sound and sight of song and sea,
 

 ’T would give my spirit stronger wings to know
 

 That you remembered still and wept for me.

She was alone in the room. The song was hardly finished when Brent stepped through the window and laid his hand over hers where they rested on the keys.

“Why do you sing like that, Elizabeth?” he said, tremulously.

===Chinese===
日子来来去去，自那晚他同她站在园门口、尝够了她不知体贴的苦涩之后，他仍不断去看她；但那一切早已过去，两人不知不觉地亲近起来。他们之间有一种默契，一直维持到几个星期之前。那是一个暖和的春夜：他刚穿过她家的园门，朝屋子走去，开窗里传出钢琴的前几个和弦，撞进他耳朵，把他钉在了原地。伊丽莎白有一副悦耳的小嗓子，天生带着许多哀婉。身为牧师的女儿，按德克斯特的标准，她唱的歌范围本该相当有限；可是那天晚上，她正独自柔声唱着一支情歌。歌词是这样的：

> 倘若死神今日便要将我认领，
 

 倘若我轻轻一晃，从你身畔凋零，
 

 哦，告诉我，爱人，我的身影可会停驻，
 

 我的形影可会在你心中长存？
 

 还是我会像一场被遗忘的梦，
 

 短短存留一刻，便消失得干干净净？
 

 时光可会向你流来它无情的急流，
 

 冷却你的心，把爱的火焰永远浇熄？
> 爱人啊，我断不愿给你半点伤痛，
 

 也断不愿做任何令你悲伤的事；
 

 哦，我多么明白，眼泪全然无用！
 

 可是爱这样甜，亲爱的，人生这样短；
 

 那么，倘若有一天我先你而去，
 

 去到歌声与海色再也到不了的地方，
 

 只要知道你还记得我、还为我哭泣，
 

 我的灵魂便会长出更强健的翅膀。

屋里只有她一个人。歌刚唱完，布伦特便跨过窗子，把手覆在她搁在琴键上的双手上。

“你为什么那样唱，伊丽莎白？”他说，声音发颤。

===Original===
She blushed and lowered her eyes beneath his gaze, as if she already knew the words that were on his lips, or feared that her soul lay too bare before him.

“Why do you think of death?” he asked again, imprisoning her hands.

“It was only my mood,” she faltered. “I was thinking, and I thought of the song, and I just sang it.”

“Were you thinking of anyone in particular, Lizzie?”

Her head drooped lower until her face was hidden, but she did not answer. A strange boldness had come to him. He went on: “I listened as you were singing, and it seemed as if every word was meant for me, Lizzie. It may sound foolish, but I﻿—I love you. Won’t you look at me and tell me that I am right in thinking you love me?” She half raised her face to his and murmured one word. In it were volumes; he bent down and kissed her. It was the first time he had ever kissed a girl. He did it almost fearfully. It was a kiss in which reverence struggled with passion.

===Chinese===
她红了脸，在他的注视下垂下眼睛，仿佛已经知道他唇边的话，又或害怕自己的灵魂在他面前袒露得太多。

“你为什么想到死？”他又问，把她的双手攥住不放。

“不过是我一时的心绪，”她支吾着说。“我想着心事，想起了这支歌，就唱出来了。”

“你想着的，可是什么特别的人，莉齐？”

她的头垂得更低，脸全藏了起来，却没有回答。他生出一股异样的胆气。他接着说：“我听着你唱，仿佛每一个字都是为我唱的，莉齐。这话也许听着傻，可是我——我爱你。你肯不肯看着我，告诉我，我想你也在爱我，是想对了？”她把脸半抬向他，喃喃吐出一个字。一个字里含着千言万语；他俯下身，吻了她。这是他头一回亲吻一个姑娘。他吻得几乎是战战兢兢的。那是一个敬意与激情相挣扎的吻。

===Original===
“You are to be my little sweetheart now, and I am to be in your thoughts hereafter when you sing; only we don’t want any more such songs as this one. I don’t want to ‘remember still and weep for you,’ I want to have you always by me and work for you. Won’t you let me?”

Elizabeth found her tongue for a moment only, but that was enough for her lover. A happy light gleamed in his eyes: his face glowed. He was transfigured. Love does so much for a man.

From that time forward, when he was harassed by cares and trouble, he sought out Elizabeth, and, even though he could seldom tell her all that was in his heart, he found relief in her presence. He did not often speak of his trials to her, for, in spite of his love for her, he felt that she could not understand; but the pleasure he found in her company put sweetness into his life and made his burdens easier to bear.

Only once had a little shadow come between them, and the fact that so little a thing could have made a shadow shows in what a narrow, constrained atmosphere the two young people lived. Young Brent still had his half-day position in the store, and when the employees of a rival establishment challenged Daniels’s clerks to a game of baseball, he was duly chosen as one of the men to uphold the honour of their house upon the diamond.

The young man was not fossilised. He had strength and the capacity for enjoyment, so he accepted without a thought of wrong. The Saturday came, the game was played. Fred Brent took part, and thereby brought a hornets’ nest about his ears. It would scarcely have been so bad, but the young man entered the game with all the zest and earnestness of his intense nature, and several times by brilliant playing saved his side from defeat. In consequence, his name was in the mouth of everyone who had seen or heard of the contest. He was going home that evening, feeling pleased and satisfied with himself, when he thought he would drop in a moment on the way and see Elizabeth. He had hardly got into the house before he saw from her manner that something was wrong, and he wondered what it could be. He soon learned. It is only praise that is slow.

===Chinese===
“从今以后你就是我的小爱人了，往后你唱歌的时候，心里想的要是我；只是咱们不要再唱这样的歌了。我可不要‘还记得我，还为我哭泣’，我要你永远在我身边，我要为你操劳。你肯不肯由我这样？”

伊丽莎白只寻着舌头说了一句话，可对她的爱人来说已经尽够了。他的眼里闪出幸福的光：他的脸发着亮。他整个人焕然一新了。爱情之于一个男人，竟能做这么多。

从那时起，每当烦忧困顿袭来，他就去寻伊丽莎白；即便他难得把心里的话全对她说，只要在她身边，他便觉得宽解。他不常向她倾诉苦楚，因为尽管爱她，他也觉得她不会懂得；可在她身边得到的欢愉，给他的生活掺进了甜味，让他的担子好扛了些。

只有一回，一小片阴影落到过两人之间；这样小的一件事竟能投下阴影，足见这两个年轻人过的是怎样一种狭窄拘束的日子。小布伦特仍在店里做着半天的工，当一家对手商号的伙计向丹尼尔斯的店员们挑战赛一场棒球时，他被顺理成章地选中，做球场上为本店争光的一员。

这年轻人并不是化石。他有的是力气，也懂得寻乐子，于是便应下了，半点没觉得有什么不对。星期六到了，球赛打了。弗雷德·布伦特上了场，也由此把一窝马蜂招到了自己耳边。事情本来不至如此糟糕，怎奈他以他那热烈天性的全部兴致与认真投入了比赛，几次凭出色的球技救本队于败局。于是，凡亲眼看过或听人说起这场赛事的人，个个都挂着他的名字。那天傍晚他往家走，心里正得意自足，忽然想顺路进去看一眼伊丽莎白。他刚进屋，就从她的神气里看出出了岔子，纳闷能是什么事。他很快便知道了。只有好话才走得慢。

===Original===
“Oh, Fred,” said the girl, reproachfully, “is it true that you have been playing baseball?”

“Baseball, yes; what of it? What are you looking so horrified about?”

“Did you think it was right for you, in your position, to play?”

“If I had thought it was wrong I assuredly should not have played,” the young man returned.

“Everybody is talking about it, and father says he thinks you have disgraced your calling.”

===Chinese===
“哎呀，弗雷德，”姑娘责备地说，“你真的去打棒球了？”

“棒球，是打了；那又怎么样？你干吗一副吓成这样的样子？”

“你掂量过吗，照你现在的身份，打球合适吗？”

“我若觉得不对，断然就不会去打了。”年轻人答道。

“大家都在议论呢，爸爸说，他觉得你玷辱了自己的圣职。”

===Original===
“Disgraced my calling by playing an innocent game?”

“But father thinks it is a shame for a man who is preparing to do such work as yours to have people talking about him as a mere ballplayer.”

The blood mounted in hot surges to the young man’s face. He felt like saying, “Your father be hanged,” but he controlled his anger, and said, quietly, “Elizabeth, don’t you ever think for yourself?”

“I suppose I do, Fred, but I have been brought up to respect what my elders think and say.”

“Don’t you think that they, as well as we, can be narrow and mistaken?”

===Chinese===
“打一场无伤大雅的球，就玷辱了圣职？”

“可爸爸觉得，一个准备干你这一行的人，让人家当个打球的谈论来谈论去，太不像话了。”

一股股热血涌上年轻人的脸。他恨不得说一句“让你爸爸见鬼去”，但他按住了怒气，平静地说：“伊丽莎白，你自己从来不动动脑子想事吗？”

“我想我也想的吧，弗雷德，可我从小受的教养，就是敬重长辈的想法和说法。”

“那你不觉得，他们跟我们一样，也会狭隘、也会弄错吗？”

===Original===
“It is not for me to judge them. My part is to obey.”

“You have learned an excellent lesson,” he returned, bitterly. “That is just the thing: ‘obey, obey.’ Well, I will. I will be a stick, a dolt. I will be as unlike what God intended me to be as possible. I will be just what your father and Aunt Hester and you want me to be. I will let them think for me and save my soul. I am too much an imbecile to attempt to work out my own salvation. No, Elizabeth, I will not play ball any more. I can imagine the horrified commotion it caused among the angels when they looked down and saw me pitching. When I get back to school I shall look up the four Gospels’ views on ball-playing.”

“Fred, I don’t like you when you talk that way.”

“I won’t do that any more, either.” He rose abruptly. “Goodbye, Elizabeth. I am off.” He was afraid to stay, lest more bitter words should come to his lips.

“Goodbye, Fred,” she said. “I hope you understand.”

===Chinese===
“轮不到我来评判他们。我的本分是顺从。”

“你这一课上得真好，”他刻薄地答道，“就是这个理：‘顺从，顺从。’好，我顺从。我甘当一根木头、一个呆子。我要跟上帝造我时的本意离得越远越好。我就做你爸爸、海斯特婶婶和你想要我做的那个样子。我让旁人替我思想，替我救灵魂。我自己寻思自救，实在太低能了。不打了，伊丽莎白，我再也不打棒球了。我可以想见众天使低头看见我投球时，天上引起的那场惊惶骚动。等我回了学校，我要去查查四福音书对打棒球持什么见解。”

“弗雷德，我不喜欢你这样说话。”

“这种话我也不再说了。”他霍地站起来。“再见，伊丽莎白。我走了。”他不敢多待，唯恐更多刻毒的话涌到唇边。

“再见，弗雷德，”她说，“但愿你能明白。”

===Original===
The young man wondered as he walked homeward if the girl he had chosen was not a little bit prim. Then he thought of her father, and said to himself, even as people would have said of himself, “How can she help it, with such a father?”

All his brightness had been dashed. He was irritated because the thing was so small, so utterly absurd. It was like the sting of a miserable little insect﻿—just enough to smart, and not enough to need a strong remedy. The news of the game had also preceded him home, and his guardian’s opinion of the propriety of his action did not tend to soothe his mind. Mrs. Hodges forcibly expressed herself as follows: “I put baseball-playin’ right down with dancin’ and sich like. It ain’t no fittin’ occupation for anyone that’s a-goin’ into the ministry. It’s idleness, to begin with; it’s a-wastin’ the precious time that’s been given us for a better use. A young man that’s goin’ to minister to people’s souls ought to be consecrated to the work before he begins it. Who ever heerd tell of Jesus playin’ baseball?”

Among a certain class of debaters such an argument is always supposed to be clinching, unanswerable, final. But Mr. Hodges raised his voice in protest. “I ain’t a-goin’ to keep still no longer. I don’t believe the boy’s done a bit o’ harm. There’s lots of things the Lord didn’t do that He didn’t forbid human bein’s to do. We ain’t none of us divine, but you mark my words, Freddie, an’ I say it right here so’s yore aunt Hester can hear me too, you mark my words: ef you never do nothin’ worse than what you’ve been a-doin’ today, it’ll be mighty easy for you to read yore title clear to mansions in the skies.”

“Omph huh, ’Liphalet, there ain’t nothin’ so easy as talkin’ when Satin’s a-promptin’ you.”

“There you go, Hester, there you go ag’in, a-pattin’ the devil on the back. I ’low the Old Boy must be tickled to death with all the compliments Christian people give him.”

===Chinese===
年轻人一路往家走，心里嘀咕，自己挑中的这姑娘是不是有点太古板。随即他想起她的父亲，自忖道——正如旁人说起他自己时也会说的——“有这样的父亲，她能有什么办法？”

他满腔的快意全被浇灭了。他恼的是这事情这样小、这样荒唐透顶。它像一只可鄙的小虫子叮了一口——刚刚够疼一下，又够不上用什么猛药。球赛的消息也赶在他前头到了家，他监护人对这事得体与否的高见，更无助于让他心平气和。霍奇斯太太发表了如下措辞激烈的见解：“我把打棒球跟跳舞什么的归成一路货。凡是要进传道这一行的，打棒球就不是正经营生。头一条，它是游手好闲；是糟蹋主赐给咱们派更好用场的宝贵光阴。一个要牧养人灵魂的年轻人，没上手之前就该先把身心献给这圣工。谁听说过耶稣打棒球来着？”

在某一类辩士听来，这样的论断向来被认为是铁板钉钉、无从反驳、一锤定音的。可是霍奇斯先生扬声抗议了。“我不能再闷着不响了。我不信这孩子有一星半点的坏处。主没做过的事多着呢，可他并没有一律禁止世人去做。咱们谁都不是神，可是你记住我的话，弗雷迪，我就当着你海斯特婶婶的面在这儿说，你记住我的话：只要你往后干的事不比今天干的更坏，那你要把自己在天堂华厦里的名分念得清清楚楚，易得很呢。”

“哼哼，埃利法莱特，撒旦在背后提词的时候，说什么都容易。”

“你又来了，海斯特，你又来了，又去拍魔鬼的马屁。照我看，基督教的善人们这么样捧他，那老家伙不定乐成什么样呢。”

===Original===
“A body’d about as well be complimentin’ the devil as to be a-countenancin’ his works, as you air.”

The old man stopped with a piece halfway to his mouth. “Now jest listen at that! Hester Prime, ain’t you ashamed of yoreself? Me a-countenancin’ wrong! Sayin’ that to me, an’ me ol’ enough to be﻿—to be﻿—well, I’m your husband, anyway.”

In times of excitement he was apt to forget this fact for the instant and give his wife her maiden name, as if all that was sharp in her belonged to that prenuptial period. But this storm relieved the atmosphere of its tension. Mrs. Hodges felt better for having spoken her mind, and Mr. Hodges for having answered, while the young man was relieved by the championship of his elder, and so the storm blew over. It was several days before Brent saw Elizabeth again; but, thanks to favouring winds, the sky had also cleared in that direction.

It was through such petty calms and storms that Fred passed the days and weeks of his first year at the seminary. Some of them were small annoyances, to be sure, but he felt them deeply, and the sting of them rankled. It is not to be supposed, because there was no specific outburst, that he was entirely at rest. Vesuvius had slumbered long before Pompeii’s direful day. His mind was often in revolt, but he kept it to himself or confided it to only one friend. This friend was a fellow-student at the seminary, a man older than Fred by some years. He had first begun a literary career, but had renounced it for the ministry. Even to him Fred would not commit himself until, near the end of the year, Taylor declared his intention of now renouncing the study of theology for his old pursuits. Then Brent’s longing to be free likewise drew his story from his lips.

===Chinese===
“夸魔鬼两句，跟你这样纵容他的作为，也差不多是一路货。”

老人举着一块吃的，停在半道。“听听这话！海斯特·普赖姆，你就不害臊吗？我纵容罪恶！对我说这种话，我年纪都大得可以当——可以当——唉，不管怎么说，我是你丈夫。”

一到激动的时候，他往往霎时间忘了这一层，把妻子的闺名唤出来，仿佛她身上所有扎人的锋芒，都属于婚前那段岁月似的。不过这场风暴倒松快了空气里的紧张。霍奇斯太太把心里话说出来，觉得痛快了些；霍奇斯先生回了话，也痛快了些；年轻人有长辈出头撑腰，也松了口气；于是风暴过去了。过了好几天布伦特才又见到伊丽莎白；但多亏风向帮忙，那边的天也放晴了。

弗雷德在神学校的头一年，便是在这样些小风小浪、小晴小阴里度过一天天、一星期一星期的。这些固然都是些小烦扰，他却感受得深，那刺痛在心里溃着脓。不要以为没有一场大爆发，他就全然安生了。维苏威火山在庞贝遭殃的那一天之前，也曾长眠过。他的心时常在造反，只是闷在肚里，或者只向一个朋友吐露。这朋友是神学校的同学，比弗雷德年长几岁。他起初本是走文学的路子的，后来弃了它投身圣职。就连对他，弗雷德也不肯交底，直到临近年尾，泰勒宣布自己如今要弃神学而去重操旧业，弗雷德那求自由的渴望，才也把自身的故事引到了唇边。

===Original===
Taylor listened to him with the air of one who had been through it all and could sympathise. Then he surprised his friend by saying, “Don’t be a fool, Brent. It’s all very nice and easy to talk about striking out for one’s self, and all that. I’ve been through it all myself. My advice to you is, stay here, go through the academic discipline, and be a parson. Get into a rut if you will, for some ruts are safe. When we are buried deep, they keep us from toppling over. This may be a sort of weak philosophy I am trying to teach you, but it is the happiest. If I can save any man from self-delusion, I want to do it. I’ll tell you why. When I was at school some fool put it into my head that I could write. I hardly know how it came about. I began scribbling of my own accord and for my own amusement. Sometimes I showed the things to my friend, who was a fool: he bade me keep on, saying that I had talent. I didn’t believe it at first. But when a fellow keeps dinging at another with one remark, after a while he grows to believe it, especially when it is pleasant. It is vastly easy to believe what we want to believe. So I came to think that I could write, and my soul was fired with the ambition to make a name for myself in literature. When I should have been turning Virgil into English for classroom, I was turning out more or less deformed verse of my own, or rapt in the contemplation of some plot for story or play. But somehow I got through school without a decided flunk. In the meantime some of my lines had found their way into print, and the little cheques I received for them had set my head buzzing with dreams of wealth to be made by my pen. If we could only pass the pitfalls of that dreaming age of youth, most of us would get along fairly well in this matter-of-fact old world. But we are likely to follow blindly the leadings of our dreams until we run our heads smack into a corner-post of reality. Then we awaken, but in most cases too late.

“I am glad to say that my father had the good sense to discourage my aspirations. He wanted me to take a profession. But, elated by the applause of my friends, I scorned the idea. What, mew my talents up in a courtroom or a hospital? Never! It makes me sick when I look back upon it and see what a fool I was. I settled down at home and began writing. Lots of things came back from periodicals to which I sent them; but I had been told that this was the common lot of all writers, and I plodded on. A few things sold, just enough to keep my hopes in a state of unstable equilibrium.

“Well, it’s no use to tell you how I went on in that way for four years, clinging and losing hold, standing and slipping, seeing the prize recede just as I seemed to grasp it. Then came the awakening. I saw that it would have been better just to go on and do the conventional thing. I found this out too late, and I came here to try to remedy it, but I can’t. No one can. You get your mind into a condition where the ordinary routine of study is an impossibility, and you cannot go back and take up the train you have laid, so you keep struggling on wasting your energy, hoping against hope. Then suddenly you find out that you are and can be only third- or at best second-rate. God, what a discovery it is! How you try to fight it off until the last moment! But it comes upon you surely and crushingly, and, cut, bruised, wounded, you slip away from the face of the world. If you are a brave man, you say boldly to yourself, ‘I will eke out an existence in some humble way,’ and you go away to a life of longing and regret. If you are a coward, you either leap over the parapets of life to hell, or go creeping back and fall at the feet of the thing that has damned you, willing to be third-rate, anything; for you are stung with the poison that never leaves your blood. So it has been with me: even when I found that I must choose a calling, I chose the one that gave me most time to nurse the serpent that had stung me.”

Taylor ceased speaking, and looked a little ashamed of his vehemence.

===Chinese===
泰勒听他讲，一脸过来人的神气，满可以体谅他。随后他说出的话叫朋友吃了一惊：“别犯傻，布伦特。说什么独自闯天地，说得倒轻巧好听。这些我全都经历过。我给你的忠告是：待在这儿，受完学校的操练，当个牧师。愿意钻车辙就钻吧，有些车辙是稳当的。等我们埋得深了，它们还能拦着咱们不倒。我要教你的这套，兴许是一种软弱的处世经，可它最是快活。我若能救哪个人不受自欺之害，我就要救。我告诉你为什么。我上学的时候，有个傻瓜往我脑袋里灌了个念头，说我能写作。我自己都说不清是怎么起的头。我开始自己涂涂写写，只图个自娱。有时把写的东西拿给一位朋友看——他也是个傻瓜：他叫我写下去，说我有才气。起初我不信。可一个人翻来覆去只对你叨叨一句话，久而久之你就信了，尤其那话又中听的时候。人信自己乐意信的东西，最容易不过。于是我便觉得自己能写作，心里烧起一团火，立志要在文坛上挣个名声。本该在课堂上把维吉尔译成英文的工夫，我都用来炮制自己那些或多或少畸形的诗行，或是痴痴构思什么小说、剧本的情节。可不知怎么，我居然没吃一个大鸭蛋就混出了学校。这期间我的几行诗印成了铅字，收到的那几张小支票，叫我脑袋嗡嗡直响，做起靠一支笔发财的梦。咱们年轻时那个痴梦的年纪里若能躲过那些陷阱，咱们大多数人在这座讲实际的老世上，原都能混得不错。可我们多半要盲盲目目跟着梦的指引走，直到一头撞上现实的界桩。这时咱们醒了，可十有八九已经太迟。

“我很庆幸，我父亲还有那份清醒，给我的雄心泼冷水。他要我学一门专门职业。可我被朋友们的喝彩抬得飘飘然，把这个念头嗤之以鼻。什么，把我的才气关在法庭或医院里？休想！如今回头看看自己那副傻样，我就恶心。我在家里安顿下来，开始写作。寄出去的许多稿子都从刊物那儿退了回来；可早有人告诉我，这是天下作家的通例，我便苦苦往前蹭。卖出去了几篇，刚够让我的指望维持着摇摇欲坠的平衡。

“唉，我那样熬了四年，怎么抓又怎么松手，怎么站起又怎么滑倒，眼看奖品在我将将够着的时候退远，这些细讲给你听也没用处。随后便是醒悟。我明白了，当初不如索性循着常规走下去。我发现得太迟，便来这里想法补救，可是补不了。谁也补不了。你的头脑陷入了一种状态，寻常的功课日程已成不可能；你又回不去，接不上自己早先铺下的轨道，只好一路挣扎下去，白白耗着力气，怀着无望的希望。随后你突然发现：你只能是三流，至多是二流。天哪，这是怎样一个发现啊！你怎么挣扎，怎么把它往外推，直到最后一刻！可它还是分毫不爽、排山倒海地压上来，于是你带着刀伤、青肿和溃口，从世界的面前悄悄退走。你若是条汉子，便横下心对自己说：‘我要低眉顺眼地谋一条生路’，然后去过一种渴望与追悔的日子。你若是懦夫，便要么越过人生的胸墙一头跳进地狱，要么爬回去，拜倒在毁了你自己的那东西脚下，甘当三流，什么都行；因为那毒已经蜇进你的血，永远出不去。我便是这样：等到发觉非选一行职业不可的时候，我挑的那一行，恰恰最能给我工夫，去将养那条蜇过我的蛇。”

泰勒住了口，为自己这般激切露出几分赧色。

===Original===
“This is your story,” said Brent; “but men differ and conditions differ. I will accept all the misery, all the pain and defeat you have suffered, to be free to choose my own course.”

Taylor threw up his hands with a deprecatory gesture. “There,” he said; “it is always so. I might as well have talked to the wind.”

So the fitful calms and Elizabeth’s love had not cured Frederick Brent’s heart of its one eating disease, the desire for freedom.

===Chinese===
“这是你的故事，”布伦特说，“可是人各不同，境遇也各不同。你受过的那些苦楚、那些疼痛与挫败，我全认下了，只要能自由选我自己的路。”

泰勒做个告罪的姿势，把双手一摊。“瞧吧，”他说，“总是这样。我这些话，还不如说给风听。”

于是，时断时续的平静和伊丽莎白的爱情，都没能医疗弗雷德里克·布伦特心里那唯一的蚀骨之疾——对自由的渴望。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
It was not until early in Brent’s second year at the Bible Seminary that he was compelled to go through the ordeal he so much dreaded, that of filling a city pulpit. The Dexterites had been wont to complain that since the advent among them of the theological school their churches had been turned into recitation-rooms for the raw students; but of “old Tom Brent’s boy,” as they still called him, they could never make this complaint. So, as humanity loves to grumble, the congregations began to find fault because he did not do as his fellows did.

The rumours of his prowess in the classroom and his eloquence in the society hall had not abated, and the curiosity of his fellow-townsmen had been whetted to a point where endurance was no longer possible. Indeed, it is open to question whether it was not by connivance of the minister himself, backed by his trustees on one side and the college authorities on the other, that Brent was finally deputed to supply the place of the Rev. Mr. Simpson, who was affected by an indisposition, fancied, pretended, or otherwise.

The news struck the young man like a thunderbolt, albeit he had been expecting it. He attempted to make his usual excuse, but the kindly old professor who had notified him smiled into his face, and, patting his shoulder, said, “It’s no use, Brent. I’d go and make the best of it; they’re bound to have you. I understand your diffidence in the matter, and, knowing how well you stand in class, it does credit to your modesty.”

The old man passed on. He said he understood, but in his heart the young student standing there helpless, hopeless, knew that he did not understand, that he could not. Only he himself could perceive it in all the trying horror of its details. Only he himself knew fully or could know what the event involved﻿—that when he arose to preach, to nine-tenths of the congregation he would not be Frederick Brent, student, but “old Tom Brent’s boy.” He recoiled from the thought.

Many a fireside saint has said, “Why did not Savonarola tempt the hot ploughshares? God would not have let them burn him.” Faith is a beautiful thing. But Savonarola had the ploughshares at his feet. The children of Israel stepped into the Red Sea before the waters parted, but then Moses was with them, and, what was more, Pharaoh was behind them.

===Chinese===
直到布伦特在圣经神学校的第二年年初，他才被迫经受他最怕的那场酷刑——登上城里的讲坛。德克斯特人素来爱抱怨，说自从神学校来到他们中间，他们的教堂就变成了生手学生的背诵课堂；可是对“老汤姆·布伦特的儿子”——他们至今还这么叫他——他们却从没有过这层抱怨。于是，人性既爱发牢骚，会众们便挑起毛病来：怪他不肯照他的同学们那样做。

他在课堂上的锋芒、在演说厅里的口才，这些风传一直没有平息，同乡们的好奇心被吊到了再也无法忍耐的地步。说实在的，布伦特最终被派去代理辛普森牧师的讲坛——那位牧师害了一场欠爽，是想象的、假装的，还是别样的——若说不是牧师本人默许、一边有他的执事们撑腰、另一边有学校当局促成，恐怕还大有可疑。

这消息虽在他意料之中，落下来却如晴天霹雳。他试图照例推辞，可是那位来传话的和善老教授冲他脸上一笑，拍着他的肩膀说：“没用的，布伦特。我劝你去，好好应付；他们非要你不可。我明白你在这事上脸皮薄，也知道你在班里多么出色，这份谦逊给你添光彩。”

老人走过去了。他说他明白；可是那个站在那里、无助又无望的年轻学生，心里知道他并不明白，也不可能明白。只有他自己，才能在种种磨人的骇人细节里体味这件事。只有他自己充分知道、也只有他自己能知道这件事意味着什么——当他起身讲道时，对十之八九的会众来说，他不是学生弗雷德里克·布伦特，而是“老汤姆·布伦特的儿子”。他一想到这里就畏缩。

多少炉边圣徒说过：“萨伏那洛拉为什么不去踩那烧红的犁铧？上帝总不至于让他烧死吧。”信心是样好东西。可萨伏那洛拉脚下就摆着犁铧。以色列的儿女们在海水分开之前就跨进了红海，可那时有摩西与他们同在，更何况，法老还在他们身后。

===Original===
At home, the intelligence of what Brent was to do was received in different manner by Mrs. Hodges and her husband. The good lady launched immediately into a lecture on the duty that was placed in his hands; but Eliphalet was silent as they sat at the table. He said nothing until after supper was over, and then he whispered to his young friend as he started to his room, “I know jest how you feel, Freddie. It seems that I oughtn’t to call you that now; but I ’low you’ll allus be ‘Freddie’ to me.”

“Don’t ever call me anything else, if you please, Uncle ’Liph,” said the young man, pressing Eliphalet’s hand.

“I think I kin understand you better than most people,” Mr. Hodges went on; “an’ I know it ain’t no easy task that you’ve got before you.”

“You’ve always understood me better than anyone, and﻿—and I wish you knew what it has meant to me, and that I could thank you somehow.”

“ ’Sh, my boy. It’s thanks enough to hear them words from you. Now you jest calm yoreself, an’ when Sunday comes﻿—I don’t know as I’d ought to say it this way, but I mean it all in a Christian sperrit﻿—when Sunday comes, Freddie, my boy, you jest go in an’ give ’em fits.”

===Chinese===
在家里，霍奇斯太太和她丈夫对布伦特要做的事，反应各是一样。好太太立刻就摆开一篇训导，讲他肩上放着的本分；埃利法莱特坐在桌边却一言不发。直到晚饭吃罢，他才对着往自己房里去的小友低声说：“你心里什么滋味，我全知道，弗雷迪。照理我如今不该再这么叫你了；可我看呀，你在我这儿一辈子都是‘弗雷迪’。”

“那么请你永远就这么叫我，利夫叔叔。”年轻人说着，紧握了握埃利法莱特的手。

“我想，我比大多数人都能懂得你，”霍奇斯先生接着说；“我也知道，你面前这桩差事不轻松。”

“你一向比谁都懂得我，而且——而且我多盼你能知道这对我意味着什么，多盼我能想个法子谢谢你。”

“嘘，我的孩子。听你说出这话，就尽够谢的了。现在你只管安下心来，等到礼拜天——我不知道这话该不该这么讲，可我是全凭一片基督徒的心肠说——到了礼拜天，弗雷迪，我的孩子，你就放开手脚，狠狠收拾他们一顿。”

===Original===
The two parted with another pressure of the hand, and it must be confessed that the old man looked a little bit sheepish when his wife hoped he had been giving Fred good advice.

“You don’t reckon, Hester, that I’d give him any other kind, do you?”

“Not intentionally, ’Liphalet; but when it comes to advice, there’s p’ints o’ view.” Mrs. Hodges seemed suspicious of her husband’s capabilities as an adviser.

“There’s some times when people’d a good deal ruther have sympathy than advice.”

“An’ I reckon, ’cordin’ to yore way o’ thinkin’ this is one o’ them. Well, I intend to try to do my dooty in this matter, as I’ve tried to do it all along.”

===Chinese===
两人又握了一回手才分手；不得不承认，当妻子盼着他方才是在给弗雷德出好主意时，老头儿显得有几分不好意思。

“海斯特，你总不至于以为，我还会给他出别的种的主意吧？”

“不是有意的，埃利法莱特；可是说到出主意，各有各的看法。”霍奇斯太太对她丈夫做参谋的本事，似乎信不过。

“有的时候，人宁要体恤，不要主意。”

“照你的想法，我猜眼下就是这样一个时候了。好吧，这件事我仍要尽力尽我的本分，一如既往。”

===Original===
“Hester, yore dooty’ll kill you yit. It’s a wonder you don’t git tired a-lookin’ it in the face.”

“I ain’t a-goin’ to shirk it, jest to live in pleasure an’ ease.”

“No need o’ shirkin’, Hester, no need o’ shirkin’; but they’s some people that wouldn’t be content without rowin’ down stream.”

“An’ then, mind you, ’Liphalet, I ain’t a-exchangin’ words with you, fur that’s idleness, but there’s others, that wouldn’t row up stream, but ’ud wait an’ hope fur a wind to push ’em.” These impersonalities were as near “spatting” as Mr. and Mrs. Hodges ever got.

Through all the community that clustered about Mr. Simpson’s church and drew its thoughts, ideas, and subjects of gossip therefrom, ran like wildfire the news that at last they were to have a chance to judge of young Brent’s merits for themselves. It caused a stir among old and young, and in the days preceding the memorable Sunday little else was talked of.

===Chinese===
“海斯特，你这个本分早晚要你的命。天天跟它打照面还不腻，真是桩奇事。”

“我才不会为了过舒坦日子就躲了它。”

“犯不着躲，海斯特，犯不着躲；可就是有些人，不顺着水划船就浑身不自在。”

“再说了，你听着，埃利法莱特，我这可不是跟你斗嘴——斗嘴是闲人才干的事——可也有些人，逆水就不划了，只会干等着，巴望一阵风来推他们。”这等不点名的交锋，便是霍奇斯先生和太太之间最接近“拌嘴”的时候了。

消息像野火一般，传遍了环绕辛普森先生的教会而居、并从这里汲取思想、见解和闲话题材的整个社群：他们终于有机会亲自评判小布伦特的斤两了。老老少少都骚动起来；在那个难忘的礼拜天之前的几天里，人们谈的几乎没有别的。

===Original===
When it reached the ears of old Dan’l Hastings, who limped around now upon two canes, but was as acrimonious as ever, he exclaimed, tapping the ground with one of his sticks for emphasis, “What! that young Brent preachin’ in our church, in our minister’s pulpit! It’s a shame﻿—an’ he the born son of old Tom Brent, that all the town knows was the worst sinner hereabouts. I ain’t a-goin’ to go; I ain’t a-goin’ to go.”

“Don’t you be afeared to go, Dan’l: there ain’t no danger that his docterns air a-goin’ to be as strong as his father’s whisky,” said his old enemy.

“Oh, it’s fur the likes o’ you, Thomas Donaldson, to be a-talkin’ o’ docterns an’ whisky in the same breath. You never did have no reverence,” said the old man, testily.

“An’ yet, Dan’l, I’ve found docterns an’ whisky give out by the same breath.”

Mr. Hastings did not think it necessary to notice this remark. He went on with his tirade against the prospective “supply:” “Why can’t Elder Simpson preach hisself, I’d like to know, instead o’ puttin’ up that young upstart to talk to his betters? Why, I mind the time that that boy had to be took out o’ church by the hand fur laffin’ at me﻿—at *me*, mind you,” the old man repeated, shaking his stick; “laffin’ at me when I was expoundin’ the word.”

===Chinese===
消息传到老丹尼尔·黑斯廷斯耳朵里——他如今拄着两根手杖蹒跚来去，火气却一点不减当年——他拿手杖敲着地，一字一顿地嚷道：“什么！小布伦特到咱们教会讲道，上咱们牧师的讲坛！真是岂有此理——他可是老汤姆·布伦特的亲儿子，全镇谁不知道，那是这一带顶坏的罪人。我不去；我不去。”

“你放心去吧，丹尼尔：他那套教义总不至于像他爹的威士忌那样烈。”他的老对头说。

“哦，教义和威士忌挂在一口气里说，这话正配你这种人讲，托马斯·唐纳森。你从来就没有半点敬畏。”老人烦躁地说。

“可是丹尼尔，我发现讲教义跟灌威士忌，倒是同一口气呵出来的。”

黑斯廷斯先生觉得不必理会这句话。他接着数落那位候任的“代讲”：“我倒要问问，辛普森长老干吗自己不讲，偏把这个不知天高地厚的小子抬出来，对长辈们讲道？嗨，我还记得那小子有一回笑我，被人牵着手架出教堂——笑的是我，你记住，”老头儿重复道，手杖直晃；“我在讲经布道，他居然笑我。”

===Original===
“That’s ter’ble, Dan’l; fur, as fur as I kin ricollec’, when you’re a-expoundin’ the word it ain’t no laffin’ matter.”

“I tell you, Thomas Donaldson, the world’s a-goin’ down hill fast: but I ain’t a-goin’ to help it along. I ain’t a-goin’ to hear that Brent boy preach.”

This declaration, however, did not prevent the venerable Dan’l from being early in his seat on the following Sunday morning, sternly, uncompromisingly critical.

As might have been expected, the church was crowded. Friends, enemies, and the merely curious filled the seats and blocked the aisles. The chapel had been greatly enlarged to accommodate its growing congregation, but on this day it was totally inadequate to hold the people who flocked to its doors.

The Rev. Mr. Simpson was so far recovered from his indisposition as to be able to be present and assist at the service. Elizabeth was there, looking proud and happy and anxious. Mrs. Hodges was in her accustomed place on the ladies’ side of the pulpit. She had put new strings to her bonnet in honour of the occasion. Her face wore a look of great severity. An unregenerate wag in the back part of the church pointed her out to his companions and remarked that she looked as if she’d spank the preacher if he didn’t do well. “Poor fellow, if he sees that face he’ll break down, sure.” Opposite, in the “amen corner,” the countenance of the good Eliphalet was a study in changing expressions. It was alternately possessed by fear, doubt, anxiety, and exultation.

===Chinese===
“那可了不得，丹尼尔；因为据我记得的，您讲经的时候，那实在没什么可笑的。”

“我告诉你，托马斯·唐纳森，这世道败坏得快着呢：可我不打算帮着它败坏。那个布伦特小子讲道，我不去听。”

然而这一宣言，并不妨碍德高望重的丹尼尔在下一个礼拜天早晨早早入座，板着面孔，铁面无情，专等着挑刺。

不出所料，教堂挤得满满当当。朋友、对头，还有纯粹看热闹的，坐满了座位，堵住了过道。礼拜堂为了容纳日渐壮大的会众，早已大大扩建，可这一天，它全然装不下涌到门前的人群。

辛普森牧师欠爽已愈，能够到场协助主领礼拜了。伊丽莎白也在，看上去又骄傲、又欢喜、又忐忑。霍奇斯太太坐在讲坛女宾席她惯常的位子上，为着这个日子，给软帽换了新的系带。她脸上带着十二分严峻的神色。教堂后排一个不悔改的活宝把她指给同伴看，说她那模样，传道人要是讲不好，她准要动手揍他。“可怜的家伙，他只要瞧见那张脸，准得垮掉。”对面“阿们角”里，好人埃利法莱特那张脸，真够表情学家研究一番：恐惧、疑惑、焦灼、狂喜轮番占据着它。

===Original===
Sophy Davis sat in a front seat, spick and span in a new dress, which might have been made for the occasion. People said that she was making eyes at her young fellow-salesman, though she was older than he. Mrs. Martin and her friend whispered together a little farther back.

A short time before the service began, Brent entered by a side door near the pulpit and ascended to his place. His entrance caused a marked sensation. His appearance was impressive. The youthful face was white and almost rigid in its lines. “Scared to death,” was the mental note of a good many who saw him. But his step was firm. As Elizabeth looked at him, she felt proud that such a man loved her. He was not handsome. His features were irregular, but his eyes were clear and fearless. If a certain cowardice had held him back from this ordeal, it was surely not because he trembled for himself. The life he had lived and the battles he had fought had given a compression to his lips that corrected a natural tendency to weakness in his mouth. His head was set squarely on his broad shoulders. He was above medium height, but not loosely framed. He looked the embodiment of strength.

“He ain’t a bit like his father,” said someone.

“He’s like his father was in his best days,” replied another.

“He don’t look like he’s over-pleased with the business. They say he didn’t want to come.”

===Chinese===
索菲·戴维斯坐在前排，一身崭新的连衣裙干净齐整，就像是专为这个日子做的。人们说，她正向她那位年轻的售货员同事挤眉弄眼，虽说她的年纪比他大。马丁太太和她的朋友坐在稍后面些，交头接耳。

礼拜开始前不久，布伦特从讲坛旁边的一扇侧门进来，登上了他的座位。他的入场引起了一阵明显的骚动。他的仪表气度逼人。那张年轻的脸苍白，线条近乎僵硬。“吓破胆了。”许多看见他的人心里都这么记下一笔。可他的脚步很稳。伊丽莎白望着他，为这样一个男人爱着她而自豪。他并不英俊。五官不端正，可他的眼睛清澈无畏。如果说当初有几分怯懦拖着他不敢来受这场酷刑，那断不是因为他在打自己的哆嗦。他过过的日子、打过的仗，给他的嘴唇添了一道抿紧的坚毅，矫正了嘴部天生的柔弱。他的头端端正正地安在宽阔的肩上。他中等偏高身材，骨架却不松散。他看上去就是力量的化身。

“他一点儿也不像他爹。”有人说道。

“他像的是他爹最好的年月里的样子。”另一个答道。

“瞧他那样子，可不像多乐意来干这差事。听说他本来不想来。”

===Original===
“Well, I guess it’s purty resky work gittin’ up to speak before all these people that’s knowed him all his life, an’ know where an’ what he come from.”

“They say, too, that he’s some pumpkins out at the college.”

“I ain’t much faith in these school-made preachers; but we’ ll soon see what he kin do in the pulpit. We’ve heerd preachers, an’ we kin compare.”

“That’s so: we’ve heerd some preachers in our day. He must toe the mark. He may be all right at college, but he’s in a pulpit now that has held preachers fur shore. A pebble’s all right among pebbles, but it looks mighty small ’longside o’ boulders. He’s preachin’ before people now. Why, Brother Simpson himself never would ’a’ got a special dispensation to hold the church all these years, ef it hadn’t been fur the people backin’ him up an’ Conference was afraid they’d leave the connection.”

“Well, ef this boy is anything, Lord only knows where he gets it, fur everybody knows﻿—”

===Chinese===
“这个嘛，在一群从小看着他长大、又知道他从哪儿来、是什么出身的人面前站起来讲话，我看这营生险得很呢。”

“还听说，他在学校里是个人物呢。”

“这些学堂里造出来的传道人，我不大信得过；不过他上了讲坛有多大能耐，咱们很快就见分晓。咱们听过的传道人多了，会比较。”

“说的是：咱们这辈子也听过几个传道人了。他可得规规矩矩够上标准。他在学堂里兴许不赖，可他如今站的这座讲坛，实实在在供奉过多少传道人。一块卵石混在卵石堆里没什么，可摆在大石头旁边就显忒小了。他如今是在人前讲道了。嗐，辛普森弟兄自己，要不是会众们扶持着，年会又怕他们退了教门，哪能得着什么特恩，把这教堂一占这么多年。”

“这个嘛，这小子若真有什么本事，也只有主知道打哪儿来的，因为人人知道——”

===Original===
“ ’Sh!”

The buzz which had attended the young speaker’s entrance subsided as Mr. Simpson rose and gave out the hymn. That finished, he ran his eyes over the front seats of the assembly and then said, “Brother Hastings, lead us in prayer.”

The old man paused for an instant as if surprised, and then got slowly to his knees. It was a strange selection, but we have seen that this particular parson was capable of doing strange things. In the course of a supplication of some fifteen minutes’ duration, Brother Hastings managed to vent his spleen upon the people and to pay the Lord a few clumsy compliments. During the usual special blessing which is asked upon the preacher of the hour, he prayed, “O Lord, let not the rarin’ horses of his youth run away with Thy chariot of eternal truth. Lord, cool his head and warm his heart and settle him firm. Grant that he may fully realise where he’s a-standin’ at, an’ who he’s a-speakin’ to. Do Thou not let *him* speak, but speak through him, that Thy gospel may be preached today as Thy prophets of old preached it.”

Throughout the prayer, but one thought was running through Frederick Brent’s mind, and his heart was crying in its anguish, “Oh, my God, my God, why do they hound me so?”

It is a terrible thing, this first effort before the home people, especially when home has not been kind.

===Chinese===
“嘘！”

辛普森先生起身宣布赞美诗，年轻讲道人入场时那阵嗡嗡声才平息下去。诗唱完了，他目光扫过会众的前排座位，说道：“黑斯廷斯弟兄，请你领我们祷告。”

老头儿怔了一瞬，仿佛吃了一惊，随后慢慢跪了下去。这人选选得古怪，不过我们早已领教过，这位牧师干得出古怪的事。在一段约莫一刻钟的祈求里，黑斯廷斯弟兄既向着众人发泄了胸中的怨气，又向着主奉上几句笨拙的恭维。到了照例为当刻的讲道人求特别祝福时，他祷告说：“主啊，别让他青春的烈马，拉跑了祢永恒真理的战车。主啊，凉一凉他的头脑，暖一暖他的心肠，把他安置得稳稳当当。求祢叫他想个明白，他此刻站在什么地界，向什么人说话。求祢不要让‘他’说话，只让祢借着他说话，好叫祢的福音今日传扬，如祢古时的先知传扬它一样。”

整个祷告期间，弗雷德里克·布伦特心里只转着一个念头，他的心在苦楚中呼喊：“哦，我的上帝，我的上帝，他们为什么这样追逼我？”

在父老乡亲面前的头一回亮相，是桩可怕的事，尤其是当家乡从未善待过你的时候。

===Original===
When he arose to meet the people’s eyes, his face was haggard and he felt weak. But unflinchingly he swept his eyes over the crowd, and that instant’s glance brought before him all the panorama of the past years. There before him was the sneaking Billy Tompkins, now grown to the maturity of being called “Bill.” Then there was Dan’l Hastings. Oh, that night, years ago, when he had been marched up the aisle with crimson face! In one brief second he lived it all over again, the shame, the disgrace, the misery of it. There, severe, critical, expectant, sat his guardian, the master-hand who had manipulated all the machinery of his life. All this passed through his mind in a flash, as he stood there facing the people. His face changed. The haggard look passed away. His eyes kindled, his cheeks mantled. Even in the pulpit, even in the house of God, about to speak His word, the blood sped hotly through his veins, and anger burned at his heart. But he crushed down his feelings for the moment, and began in a clear ringing voice, “Judge not, that ye be not judged. For with what judgment ye judge, ye shall be judged, and with what measure ye mete, it shall be measured to you again.” The lesson he drew from the words was God’s recognition of the fallibility of human judgment, and the self-condemnation brought about by ignoring the prohibition in the text. By an effort, he spoke deliberately at first, but the fire in his heart came out more and more in his words as he progressed. “Blinded by our own prejudices,” he said, “circumscribed by our own ignorance, we dare to set ourselves up as censors of our fellow-men. Unable to see the whole chain of life which God has forged, we take a single link and say that it is faulty. Too narrow to see His broad plan, we take a patch of it and say, ‘This is not good.’ There is One who works even through evil that good may come, but we take the sin of our brother, and, without seeing or knowing what went before it or shall come after, condemn him. What false, blind, petty judges we are! You women who are condemning your fallen sisters, you men who are execrating your sinful brothers, if Christ today were to command, ‘Let him who is without sin cast the first stone,’ look into your own hearts and answer me, how many of you would dare to lift a hand? How many of you have taken the beam out of your own eye before attempting to pluck the mote out of your brother’s? O ye pharisaical ones, who stand in the public places and thank God that you are not as other men, beware, beware. The condemnation that surely and inevitably shall fall upon you is not the judgment of Jesus Christ. It is not the sentence of the Father. It is your own self-condemnation, without charity, without forbearance, without love; ‘for with what judgment ye judge ye shall be judged.’

“Stand by the wayside if you will. Draw aside your skirts in the vainglory of self-righteousness from the passing multitude. Say to each other, if you will, ‘This woman is a sinner: this man is a criminal.’ Close your eyes against their acts of repentance, harden your hearts against their pleas for forgiveness, withhold mercy and pardon and charity; but I tell you of One who has exalted charity into the highest and best of virtues. I bring you the message of One whose judgment is tempered by divine love. He is seeing you. He is hearing you. Over the parapets of high heaven the gentle Father leans waiting to take into His soul any breath of human love or charity which floats up to Him from this sin-parched world. What have you done to merit His approval? Have you been kind, or have you been hard? Have you been gentle, or have you been harsh? Have you been charitable, or have you hunted out all the evil and closed your eyes to all the good? You have forgotten, O ye of little faith, you have forgotten, you without charity in your hearts, and you who claim to follow Christ and yet have no love for your fellows﻿—you have forgotten that God is a God of wrath as well as of love; that Christ hath anger as well as pity; that He who holds the hyssop of divine mercy holds also the scourge of divine indignation. You have forgotten that the lash you so love to wield over your brother’s back shall be laid upon your own by Him who whipped the money-changers from His temple. Listen! The day shall come when the condemnation you are accumulating against yourselves shall overwhelm you. Stop trying to steal the prerogative of heaven. Judge not. God only is just!”

The silence throughout the sermon was intense. During the closing words which have been quoted, it was like a presence in the chapel. The voice of the preacher rang out like a clarion. His eyes looked before him as if he saw into the future. His hand was uplifted as if he would call down upon them the very judgment which he predicted.

Without more words he sat down. No one moved or spoke for an instant. Dan’l Hastings let his cane fall upon the floor. It echoed through the silent place with a crash. Some of the women started and half cried out; but the spell was now partly broken. Mr. Simpson suddenly remembered to pray, and the gossips forgot to whisper when their heads were bowed. There were some pale faces in the crowd, and some which the galling of tears had made red. There was in the atmosphere something of the same tense silence that follows a terrific thunderclap. And so the service ended, and the people filed out of church silent still. Some few remained behind to shake the preacher’s hand, but as soon as the benediction was over he hurried out the side door, and, before anyone could intercept him, was on his way home. But he left a willing substitute. Mrs. Hodges accepted all his congratulations with complacent condescension.

“Dan’l,” said Thomas Donaldson, as he helped the old man down the church steps, “I was mistaken about the docterns an’ the whisky. It was stronger an’ better, because it was the pure stuff.”

===Chinese===
当他站起来迎接众人的目光时，他面容憔悴，自觉虚弱。但他毫不畏缩地扫视全场，那一瞬的目光，把逝去岁月的全幅图卷都带到了他眼前。那边坐着鬼鬼祟祟的比利·汤普金斯，如今长大成人，得了个“比尔”的称呼。那边是丹尼尔·黑斯廷斯。哦，多年前那个夜晚，他被人牵着红着脸一路押上过道！短短一秒钟里，他把那一切重新活了一遍——那羞耻、那丢丑、那苦楚。那边，严峻、挑剔、满怀期待地坐着他的监护人，那只操纵了他一生全部机关的主手。他站在那里面对会众，这一切在心头一闪而过。他的脸变了。憔悴之色褪去了。他的眼睛燃起来，双颊烧红了。哪怕在讲坛上，哪怕在上帝的殿中、正要传讲祂的道，热血仍在他血管里奔涌，怒火仍在他心头燃烧。但他一时强压下情绪，用清亮铿锵的声音开了口：“你们不要论断人，免得你们被论断。因为你们怎样论断人，也必怎样被论断；你们用什么量器量给人，也必用什么量器量给你们。”他从这些话里引出的教训是：上帝承认人的论断难免有误，而藐视经文的禁令，必招来自我定罪。他起初刻意说得从容，可随着讲下去，心头的火在言语里越冒越旺。“被自己的偏见蒙了眼，”他说，“被自己的无知圈住了，我们竟敢自立为同胞的裁判。看不清上帝锻成的整条生命之链，我们捏起单独一环，便说它有毛病。窄得看不见祂宏大的蓝图，我们割下一小块，便说‘这不好’。有一位，连恶也藉着作成善；我们却抓住弟兄的罪，不看也不知它的前因后果，便定他的罪。我们是何等虚妄、盲目、褊狭的判官啊！你们这些定堕落姊妹之罪的女子，你们这些咒骂有罪弟兄的男子，倘若基督今日下令：‘你们中间谁是没有罪的，谁就可以先拿石头打她’——扪心自问，回答我，你们中间有几个敢抬一抬手？你们中间有几个，是先除掉了自己眼中的梁木，才去挑弟兄眼中的刺？你们这些法利赛人哪，站在公共场所，感谢上帝说你们不像别人——当心，当心。那必定、无可规避地要落在你们头上的定罪，不是耶稣基督的审判，不是天父的判决，而是你们自己的自我定罪——没有怜恤，没有宽容，没有爱；‘因为你们怎样论断人，也必怎样被论断。’

“你们尽可以站在路旁。尽可以在自义的虚荣里，提起衣裾，躲开过往的人群。你们尽可以彼此说：‘这女人是罪人：这男人是罪犯。’对他们的悔改之举闭上眼，对他们的饶恕之求硬起心，收回怜悯、赦免与仁爱；可是我告诉你们有一位，祂已把仁爱擢为至高至美的德行。我带给你们的信息，来自一位以神圣之爱调和审判的主。祂正看着你们。祂正听着你们。温柔的天父俯在高天的胸墙上，等着把这罪恶烤焦的世界里向祂飘上来的每一缕人间的爱或仁爱，接进祂的灵魂。你们做了什么，配得祂的嘉许？你们是慈和，还是刻薄？你们是温存，还是粗暴？你们是乐善，还是专把恶处搜拣出来、对好处一概闭眼不看？你们忘记了，你们这小信的人；你们忘记了，你们这心里没有仁爱的，你们这自命跟从基督却对同胞没有爱的——你们忘记了，上帝是爱的上帝，也是烈怒的上帝；基督有怜悯，也有义怒；那手持神圣怜悯之牛膝草的，手里也握着神圣义怒的鞭子。你们忘记了，你们那么爱挥向弟兄背脊的鞭子，必由那位把兑换银钱的人赶出圣殿的，落在你们自己背上。听着！那日子必要来到，你们正在给自己积攒的定罪，必把你们淹没。休想窃取上天的特权。不要论断。惟有上帝是公义的！”

整个讲道期间，会堂里一片深沉的寂静。到刚才引述的那最后几句时，那寂静仿佛成了堂中的一个实体。讲道人的声音像号角一样鸣响。他的眼睛望着前方，仿佛望进了未来。他的手高高扬起，仿佛真要把他预言的那审判召下来，降在他们头上。

他不多一句话便坐下了。霎时间没有人动，也没有人说话。丹尼尔·黑斯廷斯的手杖落到了地上，哗啦一声响彻寂静的会堂。有几个女人惊得一颤，险些叫出声来；但那魔咒总算破了一半。辛普森先生猛然想起该祷告了；长舌的人们低下头时，也忘了交头接耳。人群里有几张发白的脸，也有几张被涩涩泪水腌得通红的脸。空气里弥漫着一种紧张的静默，好比一声炸雷过后的那种。礼拜就这样结束，人们依旧默然鱼贯而出。有少数人留下要同讲道人握手，可祝祷一完，他便匆匆出了侧门，趁没人拦得住，径自回家去了。不过他留下了一位心甘情愿的替身。霍奇斯太太带着居高临下的得意，把他所有的道贺一一收下。

“丹尼尔，”托马斯·唐纳森扶着老人下教堂台阶，说，“教义和威士忌那桩事，是我看错了。这一回的更烈、更好，因为它是地道原浆。”

===Original===
“I ain’t got a word to say,” said Dan’l, “ ’ceptin’ that a good deal of it was jest sass.” But he kept mumbling to himself as he hobbled along, “Jedge not, fur you’re a-pilin’ up sentences on yoreself. I never thought of it that way before; no, I never.”

Brent did not come out of his room to dinner that afternoon. Mrs. Hodges was for calling him, but the old man objected. “No, Hester,” he said, “Freddie jest wants to be let alone. He’s a-feelin’ now.”

“But, ’Liphalet, he ought to know how nice people talked about his sermon. I tell you that was my kind o’ doctern. It’s wonderful how a child will learn.”

Notwithstanding his belief that his young friend wanted to be left alone, the old man slipped into his room later on with a cup of tea. The young man sat before the table, his head buried in his hands. Eliphalet set the cup and saucer down and turned to go, but he paused at the door and said, “Thank the Lord fur the way you give it to ’em, Freddie. It was worth a dollar.” He would have hurried out, but the young man sprang up and seized his hand, exclaiming, “It was wrong, Uncle ’Liph, it was wrong of me. I saw them sitting about me like jackals waiting for their prey; I remembered all that I had been and all that I was; I knew what they were thinking, and I was angry, angry. God forgive me! That sermon was preached from as hot a heart as ever did murder.”

The old man stroked the young one’s hair as he would a child’s. “Never mind,” he said. “It don’t matter what you felt. That’s between you an’ Him. I only know what you said, an’ that’s all I care about. Didn’t you speak about the Lord a-whippin’ the money-changers from the temple? Ain’t lots o’ them worse than the money-changers? Wasn’t Christ divine? Ain’t you human? Would a body expect you to feel less’n He did? Huh! jest don’t you worry; remember that you didn’t hit a head that wasn’t in striking distance.” And the old man pressed the boy back into his chair and slipped out.

===Chinese===
“我无话可说，”丹尼尔说，“只是那里面好大一块，不过是嘴上逞能。”可他一瘸一拐往前走时，嘴里一直喃喃念叨：“不要论断人，因为你在给自己堆判决呢。我从前从没这么想过；没有，从来没有。”

那天下午吃晚饭时，布伦特没有出房门。霍奇斯太太要去叫他，老头儿拦住了。“别，海斯特，”他说，“弗雷迪这就只想一个人待着。他这会儿心里正翻腾呢。”

“可是，埃利法莱特，他该知道人家把他的讲道夸成什么样。我告诉你，那才合我口味的教义。小孩子家学得多快，真是了不得。”

他虽信着小友只想独自待着，过后还是端着一杯茶悄悄进了他的房间。年轻人坐在桌前，头埋在两只手里。埃利法莱特放下茶杯茶碟，转身要走，却在门口停了停，说：“谢天谢地，你把他们收拾得那个痛快，弗雷迪。这一场值一块钱。”他本想赶紧溜出去，可年轻人跳起来，一把抓住他的手，喊道：“错了，利夫叔叔，是我错了。我看见他们围坐在我四周，像一群等着扑食的豺狼；我想起我曾经的一切、我现在的一切；我知道他们心里想什么，于是我怒了，怒了。上帝饶恕我！那篇道，是从一颗滚烫的心里讲出来的——滚烫得跟杀过人的心一样。”

老人抚着年轻人的头发，像抚一个孩子的。“不要紧，”他说，“你心里怎么觉得的，不算数。那在你和祂之间。我只知道你说了什么，我也只在乎这个。你不是讲到主把兑换银钱的赶出圣殿吗？他们中间好些人，不比兑换银钱的更坏吗？基督不是神吗？你不也是人吗？哪能指望你心头比祂的还凉些？嗐！你别发愁了；记住，你敲到的每一颗脑袋，可都是自己凑到拳头跟前的。”说着，老人把小伙子按回椅子里，悄悄溜了出去。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
Bedside an absolute refusal again to supply, Brent made no sign of the rebellion which was in him, and his second year slipped quickly and uneventfully away. He went to and from his duties silent and self-contained. He did not confide in Mr. Hodges, because his guardian seemed to grow more and more jealous of their friendship. He could not confide in Elizabeth, on account of a growing conviction that she did not fully sympathise with him. But his real feelings may be gathered from a letter which he wrote to his friend Taylor some two months after the events recorded in the last chapter.

> “My dear Taylor,” it ran, “time and again I have told myself that I would write you a line, keeping you in touch, as I promised, with my progress. Many times have I thought of our last talk together, and still I think as I thought then﻿—that, in spite of all your disadvantages and your defeats, you have the best of it. When you fail, it is your own failure, and you bear down with you only your own hopes and struggles and ideals. If I fail, there falls with me all the framework of pride and anxiety that has so long pushed me forward and held me up. For my own failure I should not sorrow: my concern would be for the one who has so carefully shaped me after a pattern of her own. However else one may feel, one must be fair to the ambitions of others, even though one is the mere material that is heated and beaten into form on the anvil of another’s will. But I am ripe for revolt. The devil is in me﻿—a restrained, quiet, well-appearing devil, but all the more terrible for that.
> “I have at last supplied one of the pulpits here, that of my own church. The Rev. Mr. Simpson was afflicted with a convenient and adaptable indisposition which would not allow him to preach, and I was deputed to fill his place. I knew what a trial it would be, and had carefully written out my sermon, but I am afraid I did not adhere very strictly to the manuscript. I think I lost my head. I know I lost my temper. But the sermon was a nine days’ wonder, and I have had to refuse a dozen subsequent offers to supply. It is all very sordid and sickening and theatrical. The good old Lowry tried to show me that it was my duty and for my good, but I have set my foot down not to supply again, and so they let me alone now.
> “It seems to me that that one sermon forged a chain which holds me in a position that I hate. It is a public declaration that I am or mean to be a preacher, and I must either adhere to it or break desperately away. Do you know, I feel myself to be an arrant coward. If I had half the strength that you have, I should have been out of it long ago; but the habit of obedience grows strong upon a man.
> “There is but one crowning act to be added to this drama of deceit and infamy﻿—my ordination. I know how all the other fellows are looking forward to it, and how, according to all the prescribed canons, I should view the momentous day; but I am I. Have you ever had one of those dreams where a huge octopus approaches you slowly but certainly, enfolding you in his arms and twining his horrid tentacles about your helpless form? What an agony of dread you feel! You try to move or cry out, but you cannot, and the arms begin to embrace you and draw you towards the great body. Just so I feel about the day of the ceremony that shall take me into the body of which I was never destined to be a member.
> “Are you living in a garret? Are you subsisting on a crust? Happy, happy fellow! But, thank God, the ordination does not take place until next year, and perhaps in that time I may find some means of escape. If I do not, I know that I shall have your sympathy; but don’t express it.
> Ever sincerely yours, Brent.”

But the year was passing, and nothing happened to release him. He found himself being pushed forward at the next term with unusual rapidity, but he did not mind it; the work rather gave him relief from more unpleasant thoughts. He went at it with eagerness and mastered it with ease. His fellow-students looked on him with envy, but he went on his way unheeding and worked for the very love of being active, until one day he understood.

===Chinese===
除了斩钉截铁再次拒绝代理讲道，布伦特没有露出半点心里的反叛之意，他的第二年便悄没声息、平平静静地滑了过去。他来来去去履行功课，沉默而自持。他不曾向霍奇斯先生吐露心曲，因为监护人似乎对他们两个的友谊越来越犯醋意。他也不能向伊丽莎白吐露，因为他日益确信她并不完全体恤他。不过他的真情实感，可以从他写给朋友泰勒的一封信里看出来——那是上一章所记诸事之后约莫两个月。

> “亲爱的泰勒，”信上写道，“我一再对自己说，要给你写几行字，照我答应过的，让你知道我的进展。多少回我想起我们最后一次的谈话，如今我的想法仍和当时一样——尽管你有种种不利、屡屡挫败，得便宜的还是你。你若失败，那失败是你自己的，随你沉下去的，不过是你自己的希望、奋斗和理想。我若失败，随我一同倾颓的，却是那一整座骄矜与忧思的架子，那么久以来，是它推着我向前、撑着我不倒。为我自己的失败，我不会悲伤：我挂心的是那个人，是她如此精心地按她自己的一副图样塑造了我。人无论作何感想，总得公平对待旁人的抱负，哪怕自己不过是块材料，在旁人意志的铁砧上烧红了、捶打成形。可是我已经到了造反的火候。魔鬼在我心里——一个有节制、不出声、体面像样的魔鬼，唯其如此，反倒更加可怕。
> “我终于在本地代理过一次讲坛，就是我们自己教会的那座。辛普森牧师得了一场来得方便、用法灵便的欠爽，不便讲道，我便被派去补他的缺。我明知那会是怎样一场熬炼，把讲道词细细写好了，只怕我没有怎么严格照着稿子讲。我想我是失了方寸。我知道我是动了肝火。可那篇道轰动了一阵子，随后十来处再请代理的，我都不得不回绝了。这一切都这样卑俗、叫人恶心、像做戏。好心的老劳里想开导我，说这是我的本分、于我有益，可我把脚一跺，再也不代理了，如今他们也就随我去了。
> “在我看来，那一场讲道锻成了一条锁链，把我拴在一个我所痛恨的位置上。它是一纸公开宣言，宣告我已经是、或决意要做传道人；我要么认下它，要么不顾一切挣脱出去。你可知道，我觉得自己是个不折不扣的懦夫。我若有你一半的气力，早就脱身了；可是顺从的习惯，在人身上是越养越牢的。
> “这出欺瞒与无耻的戏，只差压轴的一场了——我的受任圣职。我知道别的同学们怎样翘首盼着它，也知道照着一切成规法典，我该怎样瞻望那个重大的日子；可是我就是我。你可曾做过那样的梦：一只巨大的章鱼缓缓地、却不容分说地向你逼近，用它的腕臂拢住你，把那可怕的触手缠上你无能为力的身子？你心里那阵恐惧的熬煎！你想动，想喊，却动弹不得、喊不出声，而那些腕臂开始抱拢你，把你往那庞大的躯体里拽。对于那将把我领进这个我命里本不属于它的团契的典礼之日，我的感受正是如此。
> “你如今住在阁楼里么？靠一块面包皮过活么？幸福啊，幸福的人！不过，感谢上帝，受任要到明年才举行，也许在这段时间里，我还能寻到什么脱身之计。倘若寻不到，我知道我会得到你的同情；只是别把它说出口。
> 你永远的真挚的，布伦特。”

然而这一年渐渐过去，没有一点解脱的动静。他发觉自己在下一学期里被格外迅疾地往前推着，他却并不在意；功课倒使他免去了些更不愉快的念头。他怀着热忱去做，轻松地驾驭了它。同学们眼红地望着他，他却不理会，一路做下去，单为喜欢有事做的本身——直到有一天，他明白了。

===Original===
It was nearing the end of the term when a fellow-student remarked to him, “Well, Brent, it isn’t every man that could have done it, but you’ll get your reward in a month or so now.”

“What do you mean?” asked Brent. “Done what?”

“Now don’t be modest,” rejoined the other; “I am really glad to see you do it. I have no envy.”

“Really, Barker, I don’t understand you.”

“Why, I mean you are finishing two years in one.”

===Chinese===
学期快结束时，一位同学对他说：“嗐，布伦特，这事可不是人人都办得到的，再过一个月上下，你就该得你的报偿了。”

“你什么意思？”布伦特问。“办到了什么？”

“别谦虚了，”对方答道，“眼看你办成，我是真心高兴。我一点儿不眼红。”

“说真的，巴克，我不明白你的意思。”

“嗨，我是说，你把两年的功课一年念完了。”

===Original===
“Oh, pshaw! it will hardly amount to that.”

“Oh, well, you will get in with the senior class men.”

“Get in with the senior class!”

“It will be kind of nice, a year before your time, to be standing in the way of any appointive plums that may happen to fall; and then you don’t have to go miles away from home before you can be made a full-fledged shepherd. Well, here is my hand on it anyway.”

Brent took the proffered hand in an almost dazed condition. It had all suddenly flashed across his mind, the reason for his haste and his added work. What a blind fool he had been!

===Chinese===
“嗐，哪里至于！”

“哦，反正，你要插进毕业班去了。”

“插进毕业班！”

“比旁人早一年，正好候着那些兴许会掉下来的委任美缺；再说，也省得跑出家门老远，才能当上个道行俱全的牧羊人。来，无论如何，握个手。”

布伦特近乎恍惚地握住伸来的手。刹那间，他全明白了——自己为什么赶急、为什么加功。他这个瞎了眼的傻瓜！

===Original===
The Church Conference met at Dexter that year, and they had hurried him through in order that he might be ready for ordination thereat.

Alleging illness as an excuse, he did not appear at recitation that day. The shock had come too suddenly for him. Was he thus to be entrapped? Could he do nothing? He felt that ordination would bind him forever to the distasteful work. He had only a month in which to prevent it. He would do it. From that day he tried to fall gradually back in his work; but it was too late; the good record which he had unwittingly piled up carried him through, nolens volens.

The week before Conference met, Frederick Brent, residing at Dexter, by special request of the faculty, was presented as a candidate for ordination. Even his enemies in the community said, “Surely there is something in that boy.”

Mrs. Hester Hodges was delighted. She presented him with his ordination suit, and altogether displayed a pride and pleasure that almost reconciled the young man to his fate. In the days immediately preceding the event she was almost tender with him, and if he had been strong enough to make a resolve inimical to her hopes, the disappointment which he knew failure would bring to her would have greatly weakened it.

Now, Conference is a great event in the circles of that sect of which Cory Chapel was a star congregation, and the town where it convenes, or “sets,” as the popular phrase goes, is an honoured place. It takes upon itself an air of unusual bustle. There is a great deal of housecleaning, hanging of curtains, and laying of carpets, just prior to the time. People from the rural parts about come into town and settle for the week. Ministers and lay delegates from all the churches in the district, comprising perhaps half of a large State or parts of two, come and are quartered upon the local members of the connection. For two weeks beforehand the general question that passes from one housewife to another is, “How many and whom are you going to take?” Many are the heartburnings and jealousies aroused by the disposition of some popular preacher whom a dozen members of the flock desire to entertain, while the less distinguished visitors must bide their time and be stuck in when and where they may. The “big guns” of the Church are all present, and all the “little guns” are scattered about them, popping and snapping every time a “big gun” booms.

===Chinese===
那年教会的年会在德克斯特召开，他们催着他快马加鞭，好叫他到时候能受任圣职。

他推说有病，那天没去上班上课。这打击来得太突然了。他就这样落入圈套么？他就无计可施么？他感到受任圣职将把他永远捆在这件违心的工作上。他只有一个月的工夫去阻止它。他一定要阻止。从那天起，他设法在功课上渐渐往后缩；可是太迟了；他不知不觉攒下的那份好成绩，不管他愿不愿意，硬是把他载了过去。

年会开幕前一个星期，居住在德克斯特的弗雷德里克·布伦特，应校方特别之请，被提名为受任圣职的候选人。连地方上他的对头们也说：“这孩子肚子里确实有点东西。”

海斯特·霍奇斯太太欢喜极了。她送了他一套受任礼服，方方面面显露出的骄傲与快活，几乎使这年轻人与命运和解了。在大典前的那几天里，她对他几乎是温柔的；倘若他真有足够的狠劲，立下一个违背她指望的决心，那么他明知失败将带给她的失望，也会大大削弱这个决心。

要知道，年会可是件大事——在科里礼拜堂作为明星教会所属的那个宗派的圈子里；年会莅临、或照俗话说“落座”的城镇，也是个受荣宠的地方。它自会披上一层数一数二的忙碌气象。会前正些日子，大扫除的、挂窗帘的、铺地毯的，忙做一团。四乡的人们进城来，安顿下过这一星期。全区各教会——地盘兴许囊括一个大州的一半，或两个州各一块——的牧师和平信徒代表纷纷前来，被分派到本地会众的教友家里落脚。会前两个星期，从这家主妇传到那家主妇的一句通用问话是：“你打算接几位、接谁？”争抢某位红人牧师的安置，能激起多少熬心与醋意——羊群里有十几家都想招待他；而名气稍逊的来客就只得耐着性子，听凭人家见缝插针、随时随处安插。教会的“大炮”们悉数到场，所有的“小炮”都散布四周，一门“大炮”一响，它们便噼噼啪啪跟着炸。

===Original===
But of all the days of commotion and excitement, the climax is ordination day, when candidates for the ministry, college students, and local preachers are examined and either rejected or admitted to the company of the elect. It is common on that day for some old dignitary of the church, seldom a less person than the president of the Conference himself, to preach the sermon. Then, if the fatted calf is not killed, at least the fatted fowls are, and feasting and rejoicing rule the occasion.

This ordination day was no exception. A class of ten stood up before the examining committee and answered the questions put to them. Among them stood Frederick Brent. He wished, he tried, to fail in his answers and be rejected, even though it meant disgrace; but, try as he would, he could not. Force of habit was too strong for him; or was it that some unseen and relentless power was carrying him on and on against his will? He clinched his hands; the beads of perspiration broke out on his brow; but ever as the essential questions came to him his tongue seemed to move of its own volition, without command from the brain, and the murmurs of approval told him that he was answering aright. Never did man struggle harder for brilliant success than this one for ignominious failure. Then some whisper in his consciousness told him that it was over. He felt the laying of hands upon his head. He heard the old minister saying, “Behold, even from the lowliest God taketh His workers,” and he felt a flash of resentment, but it was only momentary. He was benumbed. Something seemed to be saying in his mind, “Will the old fool never have done?” But it did not appear to be himself. It was afar off and apart from him. The next he knew, a wet cheek was laid against his own. It was Aunt Hester. She was crying and holding his hand. Afterwards people were shaking hands with him and offering their congratulations; but he answered them in a helpless, mechanical way, as he had answered the questions.

He sat through the sermon and heard it not. But some interest revived in him as the appointments were being read. He heard the president say, “It gives me pain to announce the resignation of one who has so long served in the Master’s vineyard, but our dear brother Simpson has decided that he is too old for active work, and has asked to be retired. While we do this with pain and sorrow for the loss﻿—though we do not wholly lose him﻿—of so able a man, we feel that we cannot do better than appoint as his successor in this charge the young man whom you have all seen so brilliantly enter into the ranks of consecrated workers, the Rev. Frederick Brent.”

A murmur of approval went round the assembly, and a few open “amens” broke forth as the unctuous old ecclesiastic sat down. It sounded to the ears of the young preacher like the breaking of waves on a far-off shore; and then the meaning of all that had happened sifted through his benumbed intellect, and he strove to rise. He would refuse to act. He would protest. He would tell them that he did not want to preach. But something held him down. He could not rise. The light went blue and green and purple before him. The church, with its sea of faces, spun round and round; his head fell forward.

“He has fainted,” said someone.

===Chinese===
然而在这一切骚动兴奋的日子当中，顶点是受任日：这一天，圣职候选人、神学生和本地传道人受考问，或被拒斥，或被接纳进选民的行列。照例这一天要由教会的某位耆宿显贵讲道——这人物，轻易小不到年会会长本人。随后，即便不宰肥牛犊，至少也要宰肥鸡，宴乐与欢庆主宰这一场合。

这个受任日也不例外。十个人一班，站在考问委员会面前，回答向他们提出的问题。弗雷德里克·布伦特站在其中。他盼望、也竭力想答砸了被刷下去，哪怕这意味着丢脸；可是任凭他怎样努力，就是办不到。习惯的势力对他太强大了；抑或是，某种看不见、不容情的力量，正违拗着他的意志，把他一程一程往前送？他攥紧双手；汗珠沁出额头；可每逢要害的问题落到他头上，他的舌头仿佛凭着自身的意志在动，全不听大脑的号令，而四下赞许的低语告诉他：他答对了。世人求辉煌成功，再没有谁比这人求丢脸的失败挣扎得更狠了。随后，意识里有个低声告诉他：完了。他觉出按手在他头上。他听见老牧师说：“看哪，上帝也从至卑者中取用祂的工人。”他心头闪过一阵愤懑，但只是一瞬。他麻木了。心里仿佛有什么在说：“这老糊涂还有完没完？”可那又不像是他自己在说话。它远远的，离开他很远。再回过神来，一只湿漉漉的脸颊贴上了他自己的脸颊。是海斯特婶婶。她哭着，握着他的手。再往后，人们纷纷同他握手道贺；他却只能无助地、机械地应答着，一如方才答题那样。
